# SWG3JS

Star Wars Galaxies, rebuilt for the browser with [Three.js](https://threejs.org/). A for-fun recreation of the SWG feel: big open planets, a free third-person camera, wildlife wandering the plains, and a galaxy map for hopping between worlds. Plus the things SWG never had: a jump button and Force powers.

## Play

```bash
npm install
npm run dev
```

Open the printed URL. The first screen is the **character select**: up to five characters kept in this browser as cards (name, species, class, the world it is on, when it was last played), a click on one enters the galaxy as it, the × on a card deletes it, and **Create new character** opens the **creator**: the appearance and wardrobe panels at full size over no world at all (nothing streams while you shape and dress, so nothing stutters), with the species and gender picker, the shape sliders, the colours, the hair and the clothes, and a bar below for the name, the class (Jedi or Bounty Hunter) and the world to start on. Entering shows a **loading screen**, the planet's picture grey and dim and coloured in from the left as the world arrives (the pack's stages, the ground chunks around the feet and the placed objects within working range, weighted by how long each takes, with a percentage and what is being waited for under the planet's name), until the ground under the feet, the chunks around them and the buildings within working range are in, and every shader in the scene has compiled behind it (the effects, the bolts and the kit's visuals put one of themselves in the scene first, so the first shot, hit or throw finds its shaders ready; a building that streams in later compiles in the background as it arrives, and a shader that still compiles on a live frame is reported in the console as `shaders: N compiled during play`); the character is held still meanwhile, so it never falls through ground that has not arrived (travelling by the map and the map's jumps wait the same way). Where a character stands is written into its record every few seconds and when the page closes, so it is back on the same spot next time; a new character starts at its world's spawn.

| Key | Action |
| --- | --- |
| WASD | Move (relative to camera) |
| G (hold) | Emote wheel: move the mouse to a slot and let go; the arrows play slots 1 to 4 outright; the Escape menu's Emotes page fills the eight slots from the rig's own emotes (the game's 270), its sit, and its 32 dances (a dance or a sit loops until you move; while dancing, **1** to **8** play the dance's flourishes, the game's `/flourish`) |
| Mouse | Look (pointer locked); past a mild upward pitch the third-person camera stays put and the view tilts on up |
| Wheel | Zoom camera: glides by a fraction per notch, in to the shoulders, and one notch further to first person (the body stays in view, headless, seen from the eyes as the animation carries them, and turns with the mouse) |
| Space | Jump (from prone: stand up) |
| Ctrl / X | Crouch (hold) |
| V | Kneel and get up again |
| Z | Lie prone and get up again |
| I | Inventory: the backpack (what the character owns, with the credits in its header: double-click puts on, takes off, takes up or puts away; shift+double-click a blade to the left hand; Delete twice destroys), the housing deeds, the appearance editor, the skills, and the Clothes (give) and Weapons (give) developer tabs — the Weapons tab also holds the instruments, which are not weapons and are how the only music in this game is played |
| B | Spawner: the garage (vehicles, mounts, ships) and the NPCs (turrets, creatures), as tabs |
| K | Jedi: next saber style · Bounty Hunter: pistol or rifle |
| Shift | Walk (on a vehicle: boost) |
| LMB | Attack: saber swing or blaster fire |
| E | Use: mount or dismount the nearest vehicle, ride a lift, go through a zone gate, or open a travel terminal, a ship terminal or a ticket collector |
| R / F held | While placing a building or a prop: hold R and move the mouse up or down to turn it (up turns it clockwise seen from above; a slow hand turns it a little, a quick one a long way), hold F to raise or lower it, Shift with R to tip and roll a prop about the view's own axes; the wheel with either held steps to the grid (15°, 0.25 m for a building, 0.1 m for a prop), the wheel alone pushes it out and pulls it in, a click puts it down, Esc gives it up without opening the menu (one picked back up goes back where it stood), Delete gives up a deed or a prop taken fresh and throws one picked back up away for good, as O does (a house of yours picked up is put back where it stood, never taken down), and nothing fights while it is in hand. Both are rebindable on the Controls page. On the dev server alone, **L** with something in hand chooses where it is kept when it goes down: yours, or one of the two files of permanent placements (see Permanent placements; a release's Controls page does not list it) |
| C | Switch class (Jedi / Bounty Hunter) |
| T | Hold to fast-forward the day |
| N | Noclip fly mode (Space up, Ctrl down, Shift fast, + and - change the speed) |
| F | Flashlight on and off |
| In water | Chest-deep water swims: Space surfaces, Ctrl dives, or look down and swim forward; under water you swim where you look |
| M | The map, with four tabs: **Here** (the planet's own map, or the space zone in three axes), **Galaxy** (every world, to jump to), **Waypoints** and **Group** (only while a server holds your group); it opens on the tab you used last, and M again shuts it |
| Y | The map open on its Waypoints tab; Y again on that tab shuts it |
| . (full stop) | The map open on its Group tab, while a server holds your group (with none it does nothing); the full stop again on that tab shuts it |
| J | The journal: your jobs and the documents waiting to be read, everything you have read, heard and been told (filtered by world, person and job, with notes of your own), and what the ISB has on you that you have found out (at a ship's controls J cuts the engines instead) |
| O | Pick up the nearest prop of yours exactly where it stands (with one in hand: put it away; at your own door: pick the house itself up where it stands, to move it); beside something the world stands for good, it says so and picks nothing up; at a ship's controls O is the ultra cruise instead |
| H | Toggle help |
| Backquote (the key left of 1) | The debug menu: every `__debug` helper, with what the Debugging section says of it, run from a window that leaves the game going (see Debugging); Esc or the key again shuts it |
| Esc | The menu: resume, switch character, Controls (every action with a primary and a secondary key, click one and press the new key, Backspace clears; mouse sensitivity, inverted Y), Sound (a master and a slider for each layer, with switches for headphones, the room echo, keeping the sound when you switch tabs, and the sabers) and Graphics (render scale, field of view, exposure, fog, shadows on and off, their resolution, distance, softness and smallest caster, object reach, ground detail and far radii, and the weather: on or off, density, rain opacity, wet ground, storms dim shadows, hold a level, the kind, Life Day), all applied live and kept in this browser; with a panel or the map up, Esc closes that instead |

**Jedi**: `1` to `6` the Force powers in the slots (Jump, Speed, Push, Lightning to start; the Skills tab of the inventory picks others, and bare hands) · `L` lightsaber.
**Bounty Hunter**: `1` to `6` the gadgets in the slots (Thermal Detonator, Fragmentation Grenade, Trip Mine, Det Pack, Stim Pack and Bare Hands to start; the Skills tab picks others) · `RMB` aim · `K` pistol or rifle · `V` kneel and `Z` prone for steadier shots.
**Elevators**: `E` at an elevator terminal (up or down as it says), or standing in a lift shaft (a building's or a ship's): a menu of the levels the shaft reaches, by the rooms they open into; click one or press its number. `E` beside a building with no way in on foot (a dungeon whose door was a server object, a station whose doors are up in the air) puts you inside.
**Dying**: the card over your body lists the cloning facilities on this world, nearest first, each named by the place it stands in and how far it is from where you fell; pick one and you come round inside it, in the room its own layout names `spawn` where it has one and in the room its doors open into otherwise. That word is the archives' and the meaning is ours: it is an ordinary room name in the client's layouts, used by plenty of buildings that have nothing to do with dying, and standing the dead up in it is this game's reading. Coming round at a facility also becomes where the plain Respawn button puts you for the rest of the session. A world with none (the lava planet, every zone of the tree planet, and space, where there is no ground to come round on) has the plain Respawn button it always had, and the message line says why.
**Vehicles**: the mouse steers (the vehicle turns toward where you look; `A/D` steer too) · `Alt` hold to look around without steering · `W/S` throttle and brake · `Shift` boost · `Space` hop (bikes and mounts) · flyers climb and sink as you look up and down, or on `Space`/`X` · ships: `W` builds speed; in flight the mouse moves a cursor that stays where you leave it: inside the small circle in the middle it aims the guns (the circle and the lead reticle turn green on the lead), outside it the ship keeps turning toward it, harder the further out; `A/D` roll, `Space`/`X` pitch, `click` fires, `Tab` next target · `U` opens and closes the wings (a ship whose wings open) · `E` off.

URL options: `?planet=lok` spawns on a specific world, `?class=bounty_hunter` picks a class, `?lowfx=1` disables shadows and halves resolution for weak machines.

## The grappling hook

Every character carries one for now. Look at something and a small diamond shows where the dart would bite: anything that stands still, the ground, a building, a rock or a trunk, within 100 m and no nearer than 1.5 m. Never a window, the water or anything that moves, but for the loose crates and balls stood round each world's arrival, which it hooks (they are the developer's own, stood only with `localStorage['swg.scratch'] = '1'` set before a world loads).

| Key | On the rope |
| --- | --- |
| Mouse 4 (the back side button) | Fire; press again while it flies to call it back, or while it holds to let go at the speed you have |
| Shift (hold) | Reel in |
| X (hold) | Reel out |
| Space | Boost off: a push along the line, and it lets go (only while swinging; on the ground Space jumps, though a Jedi's Force jump is not held while the line is in, and in water it swims up) |
| F (hold) | Haul straight in; over a roof's lip it goes up and over, and onto a walkable anchor it stands you there. On a crate or a ball, yank it to you: it drops where it comes to, at your feet from close by and a few metres short from far off, and the line lets go |
| Move keys | Sway, while swinging; on the ground you walk as ever and the rope stops you only at its length |

The rope bends round edges, corners and trunks as you swing past them and comes off them as you swing back; wound round too many it fouls and lets go, and if what it holds goes away it comes loose. It swings at the game's full gravity. It holds in water: reel in toward a bank and you are drawn along the surface and then lifted out up the rope, haul and you are out in a second or two, and a swing low through shallow water dies away rather than skimming on. A fall that ends in deep water no longer hurts when you walk out of it. A crate or a ball on the line is a tether as well as a thing to yank: walk away past the rope's length and it is dragged along behind you (Shift still walks and X still crouches while it is on the line), and the line lets go if something stands across it for a moment or before the crate is dragged far enough from where it was stood to be put back there. Something heavier than the tether's strength is refused ("Too heavy to pull."). The hook's range, reel, haul and tether strength go through the progression's stat table (Athletics will raise them when it is built), and Acrobatics already makes a swing build a quarter faster at 100. All five keys are on the Controls page; the hook's numbers are `GRAPPLE_TUNE` in `src/player/grapple.ts`, live through `__debug.grapple`.

It is dangerous too. A swing into a wall faster than 16.8 m/s hurts (about a third of your health from level on 20 m of rope, at most 60, and everything past 50 m/s), measured against what you hit, and so does flying into one just after letting go at speed; a swing hanging against a wall, a haul into one and a landing on the ground cost nothing (a landing is the fall's own rule). A hard slam staggers you for half a second, with the view shaken and the screen's edges darkened through your own vignette (not drawn with Effects or the vignette off). The rope holds through it. A slack rope catching you jolts the view a moment. Your left arm points up the rope, your body leans into the swing and eases back upright when you let go (and while you float), the view pulls back with speed, and a thin arc round the crosshair says how much of the rope's hundred metres is out, turning amber past ninety. It fires with a launcher's clank, bites with three clicks, ratchets while you reel, rumbles while you haul and whooshes as you boost off, all from the game's own bank and nobody's ear yet.

Other players see your rope and you see theirs, on a server restarted for it (`npm run relay`; a relay that has not been restarted passes nothing of a rope, and the others see a figure in the air with nothing in its hand, as before). Their rope comes from their left hand, bends where theirs bends, sags by the slack their own game worked out, and their dart flies out to its anchor and stops where it strikes; their arm goes up the line, and their fire, bite and let-go are heard at that hand. A rope hooked on a friend's vehicle stays on that vehicle on every screen, the rider's own included: each point of it that rides a vehicle goes over in that vehicle's own frame, and every browser puts it on its own picture of the hull. Other players' thrown sabers are seen in the air at last: the line that rebuilt every state they sent had always left the blade out.

The rig is a thing you own now: **Grapple rig** in the backpack, under Worn and held as carried, in no slot and no hand, given to every character once. With no server, or with one that does not say its ledger keeps gear, it is given by a mark on the character and stays in this browser's backpack, never handed up and never taken away by that server's list; with a server that keeps gear, its first list of a session decides, and asks for one where there is none. It can be traded and destroyed like anything owned. Owning it changes nothing yet: every character fires whether it owns one or not, until `__debug.grapple({ everyone: false })` makes firing ask for it ("You carry no grapple rig.").

## Installation

1. **Node 22.18 or newer** (24 is fine) and a Chromium browser. Chrome on Windows is what the game is tested in.
2. Clone the repo and `npm install`.
3. Copy `.env.example` to `.env` (git-ignored) and fill in where your own installs are:
   ```
   SWG=C:/SWG                                                                # the folder holding the client's .tre archives
   JKA=C:/Program Files (x86)/Steam/steamapps/common/Jedi Academy/GameData  # Jedi Academy, for the saber and jump animations
   ```
   The converter replaces `@SWG` and `@JKA` on its command line with these, so paths with spaces need no quoting.
4. Convert the assets into `assets-private/` (git-ignored, never committed) with `npm run swg -- convert`, which does the whole job in one command; **Converting your own SWG install** below says what it costs and how to run one step on its own.
5. `npm run dev` and open the printed URL.

## The launcher

For a player who does not want Node, git or a command line: one Windows program, `swg3js.exe`, which keeps the game up to date from `main`, converts your own install on your own machine, and plays the game in your browser as `npm run dev` does.

1. **Download** `swg3js.exe` from the repository's [latest release](https://github.com/erik-t-irgens/swg3js/releases/tag/latest). Nothing else is needed: Node is inside it. The release holds the game's code and nothing from Star Wars Galaxies or Jedi Academy; everything the game shows is made from your own installs.
2. **Run it.** It is not signed, so the first time Windows SmartScreen says "Windows protected your PC". Click **More info**, then **Run anyway**. (If your browser flagged the download, keep it from the browser's download list first.) A console window opens and says what it is doing; the launcher runs for as long as that window is open, and closing it stops everything the launcher started.
3. It fetches the newest version of the game (about 4 MB) and opens its own page in your browser, at `http://127.0.0.1:47031/launcher/`. With no internet it starts the version it already has.
4. **Pick your folders**, each with **Choose…** (or paste a path): the Star Wars Galaxies folder that holds the `.tre` archives, Jedi Academy's `GameData` folder (optional: without it there are no saber moves, jumps or saber sounds), and a folder of its own for the converted content, on a disk with about 16 GB free. Each says in a sentence whether it is right.
5. **Convert.** The launcher hands the job to the converter's own driver — the same one `npm run swg -- convert` runs — which asks what is missing and runs exactly that, several steps at once where they cannot tread on each other, until nothing is: the page shows how many are running of how many, what each one is doing now and how long it has run, and keeps a log. A first full conversion takes a few hours. **Stop** stops it cleanly, and **Convert** later carries on from where it was, since the converter always knows what is left; after an update the page shows whatever the new version asks for.
6. **Play** opens the game in a new tab. Your characters are kept by your browser for that address, which is why the launcher keeps to the same port.
7. **Playing together**: to host, set a join word under **Host for friends** and press **Start the server**; the page lists the address a friend on your network types into their own launcher's **A friend's server** field (across the internet, forward the port on your router and give them your public address). A friend then presses **Play** as usual. The first time the server starts, Windows Defender Firewall asks whether `swg3js.exe` may communicate on networks: tick **Private networks** (your home network) and press **Allow access**. If you press Cancel, or the network you are on is marked Public and you ticked only Private, a friend's launcher cannot reach you and nothing on the page says why; to change it later, open **Windows Security**, **Firewall & network protection**, **Allow an app through firewall**, press **Change settings** and tick `swg3js.exe` for the kind of network you are on.

Everything the launcher keeps is in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\swg3js\` (the downloaded game, its settings and its logs) except the converted content, which is wherever you put it. **Check for updates** fetches a newer version while it runs, and **Quit** stops it. The launcher's own workings are in `tools/launcher/README.md`.

For **Claude Code in VS Code**: `CLAUDE.md` at the repo root is the handoff, read automatically at the start of a session, with what the project is, the rules, the architecture, what took a long time to learn, where things stand and what comes next; `.claude/launch.json` starts the dev server from the extension. Ask it to read `CLAUDE.md` and `docs/ASSETS.md` before anything else.

## What exists today (v0.3)

- **Rigged character pipeline**: skinned GLTF characters with an animation state machine (idle, walk, run, airborne, seated), clip speed matched to movement, and world-space arm solving so aiming and saber swings override the clip pose. The Mixamo X Bot ships as a stand-in in `public/assets/characters/`; add `?rig=0` to use the primitive body.
- **SWG asset converter** in `tools/swg/`: reads TRE archives (including the `.toc`-indexed archives of the later client) from a locally owned install and writes textured static meshes as GLB, with retail-manifest verification. Verified on a real install. See `tools/swg/README.md` and `docs/ASSETS.md`.
- **Asset packs**: `npm run swg -- pack` builds `assets-private/<planet>/` from a spec; the game scatters the converted rocks, debris and flora across the terrain and places a hand-laid outpost of real buildings near spawn, with exact mesh collision. Without a pack the low-poly placeholders are used, so the public build stays asset-free. `npm run swg -- creatures` and `npm run swg -- player` convert the game's own creatures and a dressed player character (skin baked from the game's texture-renderer recipes) into `assets-private/creatures/` and `assets-private/player/`; the game swaps them in when the manifests exist.
- **Design doc** in `docs/DESIGN.md` with the settled decisions, pillars, combat model, skill system and phases.

- **Physics** via [Rapier](https://rapier.rs/) (Rust compiled to WebAssembly). Terrain chunks near the player get heightfield colliders, trees and rocks stand on the collision shapes the client authored for them (a trunk, a rock's few cylinders, nothing for a bush the client walked through; a cylinder guessed from the model where a pack has no `flora-collision.json`), creatures are dynamic rigid bodies that climb a step no higher than their own template's (at most the player's half metre) by probing for it when one holds them back and setting themselves on its tread (`src/world/mobiles/stepUp.ts`), and the player is a kinematic character controller with auto-step, slope limits and ground snapping. Force Push, detonators and deaths all move real bodies.
- **Speeder bike**: a dynamic body held up by four spring ray casts, with throttle, steering, lateral grip, boost, hops and engine drag. Mount it with `E`.
- **Hitbox combat**: the saber blade is swept as a capsule against creature colliders every frame of a swing, and the blaster fires bolts that fly at 58 m/s and hit the first thing in their path, so a moving target is missed. No hit-chance rolls anywhere.
- **Two classes**: Jedi (saber, Force Jump, Speed, Push, Lightning) and Bounty Hunter (blaster rifle, physics-simulated thermal detonators, stim pack). Switch with `C`. The placeholder jetpack is gone; the game's own jetpack will come back as something you equip.
- **Creatures fight back**: health, aggression on the dangerous worlds (rancors, kimogila, boar-wolves, mawgax), stuns, knockback, ragdoll-ish deaths and respawns.
- **Day and night**: a 12-minute day with a moving sun, sunset tint, moon, stars and moonlight. Hold `T` to fast-forward.
- **Player health** with regeneration, a defeat screen and respawn.
- **A display of our own**: every shape, mark and colour on the screen is drawn by this game, and none of the original client's interface art is used anywhere. On foot it is two bars with a grey ghost behind each that walks down after a blow so you can see its size, a row of square cells with one of our own marks, the key you have bound and what it costs, a cell for each hand with what is in it, and a crosshair. Over whatever the crosshair rests on, that thing's name and health. Along the bottom, at most four things you can press here, each with your own key on it; the old long line of every key is still there behind a setting and starts off. Being hurt draws a wide arc on the side the blow came from, a shot of yours that tells ticks the crosshair and ticks it red on a kill, and small numbers over heads are behind a setting that starts off, since the game said its results in words and those words go to the message line. It has a page of its own in the Escape menu, **Interface**, beside Graphics and Sound: the scale (0.75 to 1.5, 1 as it was designed), a sharper overlay canvas (two device pixels per screen pixel instead of one), switches for the ship's condition, the target block and the arcs round the reticle, the message line and how many of its lines stand at once, that long line, and four for a fight. What you can press is worked out eight times a second into one record that both the short bar and the long line read, so the long line costs nothing to leave on; before it, writing it asked the world on every frame for the lift underfoot, the elevators near, the doorless building near and the nearest vehicle.

- **Twelve planets**: the ten launch-era worlds (Tatooine, Naboo, Corellia, Dantooine, Lok, Endor, Dathomir, Yavin IV, Talus, Rori) plus the expansions' Mustafar and Kashyyyk. Kashyyyk is several separate terrains in the game (Kachirho, the hunting grounds, the Dead Forest, the Rryatt Trail, the caverns, the Kkowir forest, the Avatar platform), so the galaxy map lists its zones and travel lands in whichever you pick. Each is a seed plus a set of terrain, palette, sky, fog, vegetation and wildlife parameters in `src/data/planets.ts`.
- **Streaming procedural terrain**: 64 m chunks generated from layered simplex noise around the player, with analytic normals (no seams), vertex-colored by height, slope and shoreline. Planets are effectively unbounded.
- **Real ground in private builds**: the terrain generator paints the game's shader families across the ground exactly where the original rules put them, and the ground material blends each family's texture (at its own world scale) across every triangle, so sand meets rock and grass the way it did in the game. Each family brings its own bump map as well, from the same shader the colour comes from, so a dune's ripples and a rock face's grain catch the sun instead of lying flat; 312 of the 323 families across the worlds have one, and the eleven without are drawn flat as before. It brings the client's own gloss mask too — the slot the effect's own name calls a specmap, on 290 of the 323 — so wet rock and polished concrete shine where the game shaded them to and sand stays matt. `__debug.normals(x, y)` scales the relief live and `__debug.gloss(x)` the shine, both 0 for none; `localStorage['swg.ground.bump'] = '0'` turns the whole thing off without a build. The families are laid on the client's own 8 m pattern, as its generator lays them (each pole takes the family of the nearest 8 m chunk corner, before every layer that reads the family map and again at the end), which moves where some trees and rocks stand and what a footstep sounds like, closer to the client's own baked flora map. **The ground is drawn the client's way** once the terrain command has written terrain shaders version 4 (`src/world/terrainTextures.ts`, the rules in `src/world/groundTiles.ts`, the arithmetic mirrored in `src/world/groundMath.ts`): the mesh stands on the client's own height points (on the twelve worlds whose layout centre is odd in an axis it, its physics heightfield and the far tiles stood a metre off them, out by up to 3.8 m on Naboo and 24 m on Talus at the worst spot, and the client's fans were never drawn); every 4 m tile takes the families at its four corner poles, draws the first-listed of them whole and lays each of the others over it through the client's own painted masks (one corner, one side, three corners, turned onto the corners that family holds; two opposite corners take the stronger of two quarters), each family in the alternate its pole chose, so the 4 m repeat of one picture is gone and borders are ragged and painted rather than the 2 m triangle grid; the pictures are the client's way round (along the terrain's own x, inferred from our code); and the colour map the terrain's colour affectors paint tints all of it -- by default normalised so each world's median brightness is today's with its hue kept, no pole brightening the ground past 1.6. One program draws every planet's ground, so a travel builds none, and its GPU memory goes up with the alternates (Tatooine 10.7 to 25.9 MB, Naboo 20 to 48.6, Mustafar to 53.9). The client's own cloud shadows are built into it and off until the owner has looked (`__debug.ground({ clouds: true })`). A pack from before version 4, or `localStorage['swg.ground.tiles'] = '0'`, keeps the program the ground was drawn with before, word for word; `swg.ground.children = '1'` loads each family's first child only, the client's own `shaderGroupUseFirstChildOnly`. `__debug.ground()` says how it is drawn and moves it, and `__debug.ground({ at: true })` says everything the generator worked out for the ground underfoot.
- **Grass and the far hills' tree cards in private builds** (once the `radial` pass has run): the terrain's own grass, flowers and small plants stand where its own affectors plant them, out to the world's own 20 to 32 m, tinted by the ground's own colour rule (the owner's call; `__debug.grass({ tint: 'colourMap' })` is the client's raw colour map), lit by the leaf cards' client sum and swaying in the trees' wind, and beyond where our trees stop the file's own far cards stand to the far tiling's end. Grass **takes the sun's shadow** (it reads the cascades itself, so a tuft under a tree is darkened as a leaf in shadow is) and **can cast** its own, near you, which starts off as the original's never did; both are switches in the Graphics page's **Flora** section with the rest of the plants' distances and shadows (below).
- **Particle effects in private builds**: the game's own emitter descriptions (campfires, smoke, sparks, steam, candle flames, waterfall mist, Mustafar's lava plumes) play where the world snapshot places them, batched per texture and asleep beyond their range.
- **The world's own sound in private builds**: every area plays the game's own day and night ambient beds, taken from the same environment rows the sky follows and blended at the same weights, so a storm's rain rises with its clouds and walking from the grassland into the swamp crossfades in sound as it does in sight. Each row's second sound is its bed of quiet one-shots — a bird, a distant call every fifteen to thirty seconds. Inside a building the interior table's row for that room takes over, and the game's placed sound objects (crowds, cantina bands, sparks, fires, the starport announcer) play where the snapshot puts them, with the waterfalls', steam vents' and fires' own particle sounds. A sound on the other side of a wall is heard through it, quieter and with the edge taken off, and a sound standing in the room with you carries that room's own echo, a longer one in the six rooms the game's interior table marks apart from the other 259 (the four capitol lobbies, Mos Eisley's cantina and one station greenhouse); both the echo and headphone (rather than speaker) placement are switches in the menu. The echo's own amounts are ours, since the client's are not in the archives. The mixer's sliders are in the menu's Sound section; `__debug.ambience()` says what is playing and why, and `__debug.audio().echo` what the room is doing.
- **Everything else you can hear in private builds**: feet land on what the ground, the room or the thing underfoot is made of, on the frame each animation marks the step on, and every creature, droid and person calls, grunts, breathes and falls with the voices its own client data gives it; guns fire, hit and miss with the sounds the weapon tables name, fists and blades swing and land, grenades arm and blow up, and lightsabers hum, light, go out and whoosh through a move on the frames Jedi Academy's own animations mark (its files are the default set; the game's own are a switch in the Sound menu). Every machine sounds too: a speeder idles and revs, a ship's engine is the loop its own fitted engine part carries, wings sound as they start to move, a ship passing close whooshes, water runs under a speeder crossing it, a blow sounds for the layer it reached and a lost system powers down, a hull blowing up is heard, a target picked and a line got on it have the game's own tones, a hyperspace jump sounds through all three of its stages, a lift sounds at the stop it puts you at, and a ship's rooms hum while you are aboard. Still silent: no music at all, another player's ship, mount or blade over the relay, the doors, and the thousands of spoken lines in the bank, which nothing in the game says. `__debug.vehicleSounds()`, `footsteps()`, `gunSounds()` and `sabers()` report all of it headless.
- **Travel in private builds**: the game put travel behind three things and this uses all three where the game put them. A **travel terminal** inside a starport or shuttleport sells a ticket, on the world's own map with its ports dotted on it, at the fares the routes table charges (a starport also reaches the other worlds, on the terminal's galaxy side); a **ticket collector** outside takes one, but only while a shuttle is really there, which its own words say whether or not you can board; and a **shuttle** keeps a round on the wall clock everybody shares, so two players at one starport watch the same one land with nothing sent between them. More than one ticket can be held and a click says which one is being handed in. The **ship terminal** the game places in its starports is a third side of the same window and is your own ship rather than a seat on somebody else's: it launches into the world's orbit and flies to the other pads, free, with no timetable. Every fare comes out of the credits on the character, which the backpack's header shows. All three are **drawn** where the data says they stand: the terminal is a model converted into each world that needs one, the collector is the protocol droid the mobiles pack already carries, and a shuttleport's shuttle stands on its pad. `npm run swg -- travel @SWG assets-private --retail-only` is what writes and converts all of it; `__debug.terminal()` reports it headless (`shuttlesDrawn` is every shuttle on its rig, where it is in its round and what it is sounding and burning), and `__debug.terminal({ rigs: { … } })` moves the drawn shuttles' own numbers. The shuttles land, wait and lift off on the game's own rigs — a **starport's** transport is five pieces hung on its skeleton's joints, which is how its struts fold and its door opens — and hum, fire their engines, raise smoke on the pad and sound their landings at the moments the clips themselves mark.
- **The fittings in private builds**: every static prop the server stood that no world snapshot carries. Two sources, both the emulator's. A building template's own children are what stands wherever that kind of building stands — the elevator panel beside a lift's doorway, the bank terminal outside a bank, the cloning and insurance terminals, the sign hanging over a cantina's door — and they come out of the same blocks travel reads, keeping everything travel has no use for, so the two can never stand two things in one place. A screenplay's own placements are the other: one object at one place on one world, which is where the campsites, the crafting stations, the dungeon props, the tiki torches and the powered-down droids come from. The `snapshot` command could already take the outdoor half of those with `--core3` and dropped every one inside a room; this takes both, joining an indoor one through the client's own object id for the cell, and takes them without reconverting a world. Measured: 889 of them over the converted worlds, every one drawn with a model out of the archives, and not one standing within two and a half metres of something the snapshot already places. They are solid, so you walk into them rather than through them. `npm run swg -- fittings @SWG assets-private --retail-only` writes them; `__debug.fittings()` reports them and `__debug.fittings({ go: true })` walks to the nearest.
- **Every prop and every piece of furniture in private builds**: `npm run swg -- props @SWG assets-private --retail-only` writes the game's whole catalogue of props as one pack (8,596 things over 2,894 models, about 900 MB), keyed on what a thing is rather than on where one happens to stand, and the inventory's **Props** tab lists them in folding groups with the game's own names, sizes and pictures. Double-click one and it is in your hands as a see-through ghost, out where you look: the wheel pushes it out and pulls it in, R held with the mouse moving up and down spins it about the world's up (Shift with R tips it about the view's own across axis and rolls it about its along axis instead), F held raises and lowers it a long way, the wheel with either key held steps to the grid, a click puts it down and Escape gives it up; nothing fires, swings or throws while it is in hand. It is refused for two reasons and no others — buried under the floor, or swallowed by a wall — so a rug lies flush, a shelf bracket reaches into the wall it hangs on and a crate sinks halfway into a plinth. What goes down is kept per world in this browser and stood again on the next visit; **O** picks the nearest one of yours back up and puts it straight into your hands, exactly where it stood until you walk or look away (so a small turn is a small turn), which is the edit mode without a mode to be in: put it down somewhere else and it has moved, press Escape and it goes back where it stood, and press O again, Delete or the placing bar's **Put away** and it is thrown away for good. With nothing of yours near, O at the doorstep of a building of yours picks the house itself up where it stands, anchored there as a prop is: turn it, raise it or carry it off and click, and it is moved -- the same house under the same key, never built again from nothing, whoever stood in it stood out on its doorstep first and the things put down inside it left where they stood (said on the message line) -- while Escape or Delete puts it back where it stood. A house a server keeps moves when the server answers, for everybody on that world, and only on a server whose greeting says it can (the relay must be restarted for that); against one that cannot, O at the door opens the Housing tab with its take-down asked, as it always did. The tab's **Your buildings** list takes any of them down, asking in the page first. Forty-four of the models carry a particle effect out of their own appearance, so a garden fountain sprays, a fireplace burns, a candlestick has a flame, a lamp glows and a wrecked speeder smokes. `__debug.prop()` reports what is in hand and everything standing, and `__debug.prop(null, { find: "chair" })` searches the catalogue.
- **Real creatures in private builds**: the converter turns the game's skeletal appearances (skinned meshes, skeletons, compressed keyframe animations) into skinned GLBs, and the planets' creatures walk, run, attack and fall with their own animations.
- **Real flora and water in private builds**: the planet's own trees, rocks and plants grow where the terrain rules and the engine's seeded random numbers put them (the same spots on every server), and water sits at the terrain's global table height with every lake and pool from the terrain layers. Each lake and sea is drawn with the look of the water shader its own terrain rules name: its colour and opacity come from that shader's main texture (Lok's sea is sulphur yellow, Dathomir's near black, the misty falls pools a clear teal), how rippled it is from its normal map, and how fast those ripples drift from its scroll rate. A pack converted before this falls back to the planet's own colour and says so in the console.
- **Real SWG terrain in private builds**: `src/swg/terrain/` is a port of the game's terrain generator (its seeded fractals, layer boundaries, filters and affectors, and the ground modifications buildings apply). When a converted pack carries the planet's `.trn`, chunks are generated from it in a Web Worker and the ground matches the original game to the centimetre, so snapshot buildings sit exactly on it.
- **Props**: instanced trees (six styles: pine, round, palm, dead, giant, swamp) and rocks scattered deterministically per chunk, with cylinder colliders.
- **Water**, per-planet gravity, a gradient sky shader with sun discs (Tatooine gets two), exponential fog, and shadow-casting sunlight.
- **Player**: SWG-style orbit camera, run/walk, jumping, air control, wading and swimming, a low-poly Jedi with a walk cycle and a toggleable lightsaber.
- **Force powers** with a regenerating pool: Force Jump, Force Speed, Force Push (sends creatures flying), Force Lightning (arcs to the nearest creature in front of you).
- **Wildlife**: per-planet creatures (banthas, kaadu, rancors, kimogila...) that wander, flee if skittish, get knocked around and stunned.
- **Galaxy map** overlay for instant travel.

Debug counters are exposed on `window.__stats` (frame, physics and render milliseconds, draw calls).

## Roadmap

Rough order, all up for discussion:

1. **Feel**: rigged GLTF characters and creatures with real animations (Mixamo for humanoids), saber stances and combos, blocking, dodge rolls, footsteps and ambient audio.
2. **Models**: replace primitives with proper assets. See "On assets" below.
3. **Places**: hand-placed cities and points of interest on top of the procedural base (Mos Eisley, Theed, Coronet), starports, buildings with interiors.
4. **Life**: NPCs with dialogue, quests, enemy humanoids that shoot back, loot.
5. **Systems**: skills and professions, inventory, crafting, harvesters, housing.
6. **Space**: ships, the JTL-style space layer, travel between planets by actually flying.
7. **Multiplayer**: authoritative server, chat, guilds. The code keeps world state in plain data with this in mind.

## Jedi Academy movement and saber combat (this branch)

The branch `claude/jka-combat` swaps the player's ground and air movement and the lightsaber combat for translations of Jedi Academy's (OpenJK, GPL-2.0; see `LICENSES/OpenJK-GPL-2.0.txt`). Swimming and noclip are untouched.

- **Movement**: Quake-style friction and acceleration, a little air control, and jumps that keep lifting while Space is held, up to the Force Jump level (level 3 by default, spending Force while it lifts). Landing from higher than your own jump's reach hurts. Ctrl crouches (half speed and a shorter capsule), tapping Ctrl while moving rolls that way, and landing with Ctrl held while moving rolls out of the landing for a third of the damage. `__debug.profile('swg')` in the console restores the original numbers.
- **Wall moves** (Force Jump level 2 and up, each a fresh press of Space): strafing and running beside a wall runs along it (press Space again to flip off, or run out of wall to stop); strafing into a wall flips off it; jumping at a wall just off the ground runs up it and flips back (level 3 runs higher); back and Space backflips; and at level 3, pressing at a wall in mid-air grabs it and springs off. A force jump that starts while pushing a direction becomes a flip that way.
- **Saber**: the first click draws the saber; the direction keys held with the click pick the swing (forward: overhead, sideways: horizontal cuts, diagonals: diagonal cuts); holding attack chains swings through Jedi Academy's arcs until the style's chain runs out and the saber returns to ready. **K** cycles the five styles: fast, medium, strong, the dual sabers (a second saber in the left hand) and the staff (a blade at each end); each has its own animations, speeds, chain lengths and damage, and the dual and staff styles chain without end. `__debug.saber()` reports the state.
- **Special moves**, as the game's rules pick them: pressing jump with attack held and forward pushed (medium flips over into a downward slash, strong leaps into the death-from-above, the dual sabers leap twice, the staff butterflies; the fast style has none, its special is the crouched lunge); jump with attack and a strafe (a cartwheel, the staff's sideways butterfly); the staff's back, jump and attack backflip attack. The press of jump starts the leap at once, cutting short any swing in progress, rather than waiting for the game's narrow window after the jump; crouched and forward (fast lunges, the dual sabers and the staff spin); back with an enemy behind (the back stab or back attack, low when crouched); attack out of a forward roll (the roll stab); the dual sabers hitting both sides at once with enemies on both; and the katas, both mouse buttons together while standing still (each style's own long special). **R** throws the saber, which flies where you look, returns when you let go (or at its reach, after six seconds, or off a wall) and cuts what it passes; with the staff, R kicks in the direction held (in the air too). The special moves cost Force.
- **Controls**: every action can be rebound from the console and the choice is kept in local storage: `__debug.bind('crouch', 'KeyV')` (KeyboardEvent code names, or `Mouse0`, `Mouse1`, `Mouse2`; several keys may be given; none restores the default), `__debug.bindings()` lists them, `__debug.resetBindings()` clears them. Only the keys you chose are kept: an action you never rebound follows the defaults as they move, except that a key you chose for something else stays yours (a default that moves onto it leaves it alone, and has it back once you move your own choice off it). On the Controls page a key pressed for one action is taken off whichever other holds it, unless the two share it by default (right mouse is both the other attack and the block), and Backspace on an action's last key leaves it with none. X crouches as well as Ctrl out of the box, because a Mac turns Ctrl-click into a right click.
- **Facing**: the body faces the camera while fighting, in the air, rolling and during the wall moves, so a flip or a wall run reads the way the move was made. On the ground SWG has no sideways or backwards clips (its run is `loop_standing:0:speed2` whichever way you go), so the game does what Jedi Academy does: the legs turn at most 45 degrees off the camera (sideways runs angle them, diagonals half as much), backing up plays Jedi Academy's back-pedal (`BOTH_RUNBACK1`, `BOTH_WALKBACK1`, imported by default) with the legs forward, and the torso twists back to face the camera. Without those clips the body turns the way it runs. The **head** is a third thing beside the legs and the torso: it follows where the view looks, within a cone of 55 degrees and 30 degrees measured from the chest the torso twist has just left (so turning the body does not first fling the head), a third of the turn on the neck and two thirds on the head, eased at 6 radians a second, and eased back to nothing through any one-off clip that poses the head — a kata, a kick, a death, an emote, and an ordinary swing riding the upper body over running legs — and while riding anything, at a bridge's controls included, whose pose is authored with the head where it wants it. Another player's head follows theirs too: their heading already crosses and one byte of pitch goes with it, which is the whole of it on the wire. `__debug.headLook()` reports where the head has got to in degrees and moves every number it has (`{ yaw: 70 }` a wider cone, `{ rate: 2 }` a slow ease to watch, `{ ride: 1 }` the head following at a ship's controls, `{ yaw: 0, pitch: 0 }` the game exactly as it was).
- **Blaster bolts** (after Jedi Academy's E-11, `src/combat/bolts.ts`): the bounty hunter's LMB fires a bolt every 0.35 s from the muzzle at whatever the crosshair is on. From the hip it scatters 1.6 degrees each way; with **RMB** held you aim, the shot flies true and the camera comes in over the shoulder with a narrower view.
- **Blaster carries** (`gunIdle`, `gunReady`, `gunAim`, `kneel` and the prone states in `src/player/rig.ts`), the game's own, as its animation table names them: the relaxed carries are `loop_pistol_standing:speedN` (a pistol at the side like a hilt) and `loop_rifle:speedN` (across the chest), idle, walk and run, with the body turning the way it runs. After a shot the combat carry comes up and stays five seconds past the last one: the table's one combat stance, `loop_combat_standing:speedN`, is the unarmed one (fists up), so with a blaster the combat carry keeps the gun's own carry and faces the camera, unless the table has the loops the game's state hierarchy names for it (`loop_pistol_combat_standing_aimed`, `loop_pistol_combat_kneeling_aimed`, the `pistol_combat_standing_fire_N` shots), which the game plays when it has them. `npm run swg -- ash @SWG object/creature/player/shared_human_male.iff --find=pistol` prints that hierarchy: every state with its idle loop, actions and links, and which of those names the table lacks. The converter ranks a speed selector's branches by the speed each animation carries, so `:speed0` is the still one everywhere (the files list them in their own order). **RMB** aims: the table's own aimed loop on the upper body over the carry's legs, and where a table has not got one, the last frame of the transition into the aimed pose (`trn_pistol_combat_to_pistol_combat_aimed`, `trn_rifle_a_standing_ready_to_aimed`) with the inverse played once when the aim ends. Until the direction selector's four-character tag was read as four characters, no table appeared to have one and the transition was always what played. A shot standing or kneeling is one of the game's additive clips (`add_pistol_fire_N`, `add_rifle_fire_N`): a recoil on the arms added to whatever pose is up, which is why those clips stood as T-poses in the gallery when played alone; prone shots are the full `<kind>_combat_prone_fire_N`, and once the table's nested entries are read the hierarchy's own `pistol_combat_standing_fire_N` and `rifle_standing_aimed_fire_N` play instead. A whole-body shot takes the upper body while it plays and the state clip drives the legs alone meanwhile, since two clips on one bone would blend half and half; `__debug.upper('clip')` plays any clip that way. **V** kneels (`loop_pistol_kneeling`, `loop_rifle_kneeling`; `loop_kneeling` unarmed) and **Z** lies prone (`loop_<kind>_prone`, `loop_<kind>_combat_prone`, `loop_<kind>_combat_prone_aimed`, still and crawling); the game's transitions play between standing, crouched, kneeling and prone (`trn_standing_to_kneeling`, `trn_prone_to_standing` and kin, the blaster's own `trn_<kind>_combat_<from>_to_<kind>_combat_<to>` set while the combat carry is up, their `_aimed` forms while aiming). The game's poses point the arm off to the left of the body, so the torso turns 30 degrees to the right, the legs still facing the camera: in the hip-fire carry with either blaster in every posture but prone, aiming with the rifle everywhere but prone, and aiming with the pistol only kneeling or crouched, since its two-handed standing aim points straight (`__debug.gun('rifle', { ready: 30, aim: 30, aimKneel: 30 })` tunes each). The hip-fire carry after a shot is the pistol's one-handed riding carry (`loop_pistol_riding`) and the rifle's "ready" pose, which the table has only as the end of its transitions (`trn_rifle_a_standing_hold_to_ready`, or `aimed_to_ready` when the aim has just ended), held there; aiming holds the transition into the aimed pose. All of these ride the upper body over the relaxed idle, walk, run or kneel, and over walking or running legs only, the torso is held steady (the lowest spine bone is turned back by the pelvis's rock; `__debug.steady(false)` shows the difference). The combat stances and aimed shots the state hierarchy names (`loop_pistol_combat_standing_aimed`, `pistol_combat_standing_fire_N` and the rifle's) **are** in the retail table and always were: the reader matched the direction selector's tag as three characters where it is four (`DIR `, as `VAL ` is in the string selector), so every direction selector came back empty and 34 of the table's 908 logical names had no leaves at all. That is where "870 logical names" came from, and the `player` command's old advice to convert without `--retail-only` was blaming the archives for a reader bug. The direction codes are a bitmask (4 front, 8 back, 1 right, 2 left) and the front branch is the one baked; a table that genuinely lacks a stance still falls back to the relaxed carry and the additive shot, and the `player` command says which names are missing. A kneel steadies the hip shot to half its scatter, prone to a third, a crouch to three quarters. **K** switches between the pistol's and the rifle's carries as it cycles the saber styles (`__debug.gun('pistol')` or `('rifle')` does the same; there is one placeholder gun model until the weapons are converted); without the converted clips the arms are aimed by hand as before. The reloads are converted but not played yet. Bolts fly straight at 2300 units a second (58 m/s) with no drop, hurt the first thing they run into (20 damage), and can be sidestepped: whoever shoots aims where you are, not where you will be. **Blaster turrets** (`src/combat/turrets.ts`) are stood 20 m ahead of you from the spawner's NPC tab (B), which also stands the planet's creature ahead and removes either kind; none appear on their own. A turret turns to face you within 45 m, fires every 0.8 s with a degree of scatter, has 120 health, and stands again 25 s after it is destroyed. `__debug.turret(20)` stands one ahead of you, `__debug.turrets()` and `__debug.bolts()` report them, and `__debug.shootPlayer()` fires one bolt at you.
- **Blocking**: **RMB** held with the saber lit is the block: the style's stance comes up, and a bolt that reaches you from ahead is turned back into the air (`src/combat/deflect.ts`, after `WP_SaberCanBlock` and `G_ReflectMissile`). It then belongs to you and hurts what it hits. At saber defence rank 3 (the default) it flies where you look, and you can block in the middle of a swing; at rank 2 it is turned back the way it came with some scatter; at rank 1 it scatters widely, and swings leave you open. `__debug.saberDefense(n)` sets the rank. Nothing is blocked while the button is up. The parry played comes from where the bolt struck (`BOTH_P1_S1_T_`, `_TR`, `_TL`, `_BR`, `_BL`; the dual sabers' `P6_S6` and the staff's `P7_S7` sets), imported by default. Bolts from behind, while the saber flies, mid-flip or mid-roll are not blocked. Only a lightsaber blocks; other melee weapons, when they come, will not.
- **Jumps**: every jump is Jedi Academy's, since SWG's own jump clip is not usable: the jump, the pose in the air and the landing come in the direction pushed (forward, back, left, right, as `PM_JumpForDir` picks them) and in their force jump versions when the jump is a Force one (`PM_ForceJumpAnimForJumpAnim`), all imported by default. Only the Jedi has a Force jump level; the bounty hunter's jump is the plain one, with no wall moves, flips or backflips. Standing still and holding attack chains swings round the quadrants the way `PM_AttackMoveForQuad` does, each starting where the last ended.
- **Pace**: the ground speed follows the clip that plays for it, so the feet stay planted: walking is as fast as the walk clip travels (SWG's own, or Jedi Academy's saber walk with the block held), crouch-walking with the walk key as its clip, and backing up with the block held a touch slower than the run, as its back-pedal is; a plain crouch moves at Jedi Academy's crouched walking speed, a little quicker than the clip, with the clip scaled up to it, and running keeps Jedi Academy's speed with the run clip scaled to it, except with the block held, where the run drops to the saber run's own pace within 65 to 80 percent of the full run and the back-pedal to its own within 50 to 80 percent. Jedi Academy's clips were made for less than its speeds (the game lets the feet slide), so the paces taken from them are held within those bands, and its walks go no slower than 1.2 m/s. The importer measures each Jedi Academy locomotion clip's speed from its feet (the planted foot slides back under the body at the travel speed) and stores it beside the clip; SWG's clips carry their own. Looping clips (idles, walks, runs, stances) now close properly: the converter adds the key that takes the last frame back to the first over one frame interval, which is what the loop was missing when it hitched.
- **Postures**: Ctrl or X held crouches (`trn_standing_to_crouched` and back play when standing still); without the block held it is SWG's own crouch (`loop_crouched:speed0` still, `loop_crouched:speed1` moving, at the clip's pace, and the same clip slowed with the walk key as the movement is), with the block held Jedi Academy's. **V** kneels (`loop_kneeling`; moving, the crouch key, a jump or a swing gets up, Z goes down to prone) and **Z** lies prone and gets up again (`loop_prone:speed0` still, `loop_prone:speed1` crawling, at the crawl's own pace, with the walk key too); the capsule shrinks to the crouch height for both. A saber swing, the block, swimming or the jump key stand you up (the jump key only stands you up; it jumps on the next press). SWG's female walk is its own clip (`loop_skill:speed0`), to be picked up when the other species and genders are converted.
- **Weapons in hand**: the saber and the rifle sit on the skeleton's own weapon hold points (`hold_r`, `hold_l`, the bones the game hangs weapons from, in the palm) when the skeleton has them, and on the wrist bones otherwise. Where the blade points in the hand is solved by the importer from Jedi Academy's own swings: each medium and strong swing starts and ends in a named quadrant (the overhead starts pointing up, a side cut starts pointing that way), which pins the blade's axis in the wrist frame by least squares over fourteen swings; the left hand's axis is the right's mirrored through the dual stance. When the Jedi Academy archives hold a player model and the default hilt (`models/players/kyle/model.glm`, `models/weapons2/saber_1/saber_w.glm`), the axis comes from the game's own geometry instead: a hilt bolts to the player's `*r_hand` tag triangle and its blade runs along the `*blade1` tag of the hilt, both at the skeleton's base pose, so the blade's direction in the hand bone can be read off; the importer prints both axes and how each fits the swings, keeps the swing solve as the default (the swings were made to look like it) and carries the tag axis beside it as an alternative (`jkaGrip.tags`). The axis is written beside the clips (`jkaGrip` in the manifest and in `parts.json`, carried by the clip bundle), and the game aligns the hilt to it, so wrist twists in the katas turn into the saber spins they are; without it the game guesses from the bind pose. The solved axis is used through Jedi Academy's one-off clips (its swings, katas and throws) and the bind-pose guess for everything held, its stances, runs and walks as much as the game's own clips, blended over a few frames when the source changes: the stances read right with the guess and the swings with the solve. In the console, `__debug.grip({ source: 'tags' })` swaps the tag axis in and `{ source: 'solved' }` the swing solve; `__debug.grip({ tilt: 20, turn: -10 })` turns the axis by degrees about two axes at right angles to it, for every clip alike, since one axis serves the stances and the swings both (the retargeted hand keeps a constant offset from the game's hand); the result's `effective` field is the axis to bake into the manifest once it looks right. `stanceRoll` and `jkaRoll` still roll the hilt about the forearm.
- **Animations**: SWG's own idle, walk, run and swimming are what you see by default, saber lit or not, with the saber hanging at your side and the body turning the way it runs. Jedi Academy takes over while the block is held, and through any swing, throw, flip or roll: the style's stance when standing, Jedi Academy's saber run and walk (`BOTH_RUN2`, `BOTH_WALK2`; the staff's and the dual sabers' own `BOTH_RUN_STAFF`, `BOTH_RUN_DUAL` and walks, since the game gives the three single-saber styles one set) and back-pedals, the crouch and its back-pedal, when moving with the legs angled to the camera, its jump and landing in the air, and its crouch. Swimming with the block held layers the stance on the torso and arms over the swimming legs. A swing started on the move plays on the upper body alone over Jedi Academy's run (or strafe, or back-pedal) on the legs, the two played apart the way that game plays its torso and legs; standing still, the swing has the whole body, and the ordinary SWG run comes back once the saber is down. The clips come in when the player is converted with `--jka=<path to Jedi Academy's GameData or base folder>` (see the converter README); the importer retargets them onto the SWG skeleton, and the parts rig receives them through `clips-save` and `clips-apply`. Without them, everything is SWG's and swings fall back to the stand-in arm swing. `__debug.player().rig` names the clip playing.

## Species and the character creator (a start)

`npm run swg -- species @SWG assets-private --retail-only` converts every playable species and gender the archives hold (`object/creature/player/shared_<species>_<gender>.iff`: human, Twi'lek, Zabrak, Wookiee, Rodian, Trandoshan, Mon Calamari, Bothan, Ithorian, Sullustan, each male and female) into a parts pack under `assets-private/characters/<species>_<gender>/`, applies the player's Jedi Academy clip bundle (`player/jka.clips`, from `clips-save`) to each rig, and writes `characters/index.json`: for each character its shape sliders (the meshes' blend targets) and its customization variables, structured: every palette variable with its colours in order (skin, hair, eyes, lips as the species has them) and every index variable with its number of choices, and which shaders and texture renderers read each. `--only=twilek,human_female` converts a few; `--var=index_color_skin=12,index_color_hair=3` bakes them with those values.

In the game, the creator (and the inventory's tab of the same name, I) has an **Appearance** tab of its own, wider than the others, with a species and gender picker in its header that swaps the rig live (the choice is remembered; `__debug.species()` lists them and `__debug.species('rodian_male')` switches), the doll beside it (drag turns it, the right button pans it, the wheel zooms in on the face, a double-click frames it again), and **Shape**: a slider per blend target, two-ended pairs (`blend_jaw_0` and `_1`) as one slider from -1 to 1, moved live on the body and everything worn over it. **Skin, hair and eyes** are live: every palette is a row of swatches with a slider under it to scrub through, every index variable (a texture choice) a slider, and moving one renders the skin, hair or eye texture again in the browser. A worn piece's own colours (the game's private variables, a shirt's colour 1 and 2) sit in a section of their own per piece, only while it is worn, and reach only that piece: the body's colour 1 is not the shirt's; a private copy of a shared variable (the head's own skin colour) follows the shared one. The wardrobe's items carry recipes too (`wardrobe` writes `customize.json` beside the catalogue), so clothes put on from the wardrobe recolour live as well, and hairstyles sit in the Appearance tab (a species' styles, either gender's, and their colour) rather than the clothing slots. Height is a slider of its own, a scale over the model within the species' creation range. Age is a choice among the head's wrinkle maps, the game's compressed normal maps (x in the alpha, y in the green) unpacked and lit as normal maps (`__debug.normals(1, 1)` flips them the other way up, `__debug.normals(2, -2)` doubles them, `(0, 0)` turns them off; the same call reaches every normal map in the world); a mesh whose recipe carries one (the head, the body) is lit with it once any of its colours has rendered. The images are decoded by the game's own PNG reader rather than a canvas, since a canvas premultiplies the alpha and blacked out the colour under the masks' transparent texels. A blend variable the skin's blueprint reads (the fat and muscle levels pick among skin textures) is driven by the matching shape slider, and a height variable scales the whole character. The game's texture renderer, which the converter runs at conversion time to bake those textures from the game's blueprints (`.trt`), shaders, effects, textures and palettes, is ported to the browser (`src/player/texrender.ts`, the same fixed-function pixel pipeline), and the `species` and `parts` commands write its inputs into the pack as recipes: `characters/<id>/customize.json` names, for each material the converter baked, the blueprint that renders it and the shader baked over it, with every texture (and every texture a choice can pick) as a PNG under `customize/` and every palette's colours; `src/player/customizer.ts` renders the recipes that read a changed variable, one after another in the characters' own worker (`src/player/recipeWorker.ts`), and puts the result on the material. A pack converted before this carries no recipes: its swatches show the `species --var=...` command instead until it is converted again. The look (sliders and colours) and the outfit are kept in the character's record in the browser and restored when it is played again. A humanoid species without a wardrobe of its own borrows the human one of its gender, since they share the skeleton.

With the creator's own table converted (`npm run swg -- customization @SWG assets-private --retail-only`, seconds, no model), the Appearance tab is laid out as the game's creator was: its groups in its order (the body, the eyes and nose, the face, then each species' own), its rows in theirs under the game's own words (the string table's, never typed into the code), the males' second muscle slider shown once, a Zabrak's horns and a Bothan's or Rodian's hair trim each under the hair's own tab, the hairstyles a grid of the converter's own pictures at the top of the species' hair tab -- this gender's in the game's order with the creation styles first, then the other gender's, then what the game never offered (the Sullustan's eighteen, under a hair tab of ours) -- with a cell for no hair leading for every species (the owner's call: taking the hair off leaves no hole in any base head; a new Twi'lek, Zabrak or Trandoshan, which the game kept from going bald, still starts in the game's first style and may take it off), a new style taking the hair colour last picked and going on compiled before it shows (`__debug.hair()`), a colour that follows another (the facial hair follows the hair) hidden behind it and written with it, a female's lips and eye shadow set by her skin unless changed after (`__debug.creator({ linkSelf: false })` stops it), and each colour a strip that opens a picker under its row (`src/ui/dyePicker.ts`): its own palette first, the creation colours in the game's own columns and every other colour under "More colours"; for a worn piece, a hairstyle's colour and the **Dye** row of ours every garment palette by name, every creator palette by family and species, and all the garment colours in one grid (1,244 once near twins are folded); for an eye colour every species' eye colours too, on every species that has an eye colour at all (all but the Ithorians). A colour picked from anywhere but the row's own palette is carried whole (a negative palette value), so any piece takes any colour; a skin, lip, marking or horn colour keeps to its own palette. The few body sliders the game never showed stand in a group of ours at the bottom, a body colour whose palette offers one colour is not shown, and a worn piece's own colours stand after the table's groups in a section a piece, under the game's name for it, one row a colour however many of its meshes carry it. Every character's colours render in a worker of their own, and two pieces drawn with one shader (a left and a right glove) take two colours (`__debug.dye()`). A worn piece's colour is the thing's own, kept by its bare name: two of one shirt may be two colours, the colour stays with the shirt when it is taken off, traded (it arrives on the other character in it, whichever gender), reloaded or carried to another server, putting on the other copy draws the piece in that copy's colours or the game's own, a colour set with no server answering wins over the server's when it is newer, and the backpack and the trade window show a small swatch of a thing's colour (`__debug.tint()`). Two copies of one lightsaber hilt (or any blade the off hand takes) can be held one in each hand. A peer's garment and hair colours now show the first time they are seen, a colour change alone reaches the others, and only the colours on show go out on the wire. Still to come: the wardrobe's own items rendered live (a hairstyle's colour), hair styles as a slot the species allows, the species' own height and build ranges, and a saved character shared between browsers.

Zoomed into first person the head, hair and anything worn on the head are drawn into the shadows only, so the body stays in view and the shadow keeps its head.

Characters own their items. The backpack (I) shows them with the game's own names, descriptions and a picture each; a double-click puts a piece on or a weapon in hand, and the game's body slots decide what comes off (a shirt and a jacket are worn together, the Padawan robe takes off the jacket, trousers and shoes). A new character starts with a kit (a shirt, work pants, shoes and a belt, and the class's weapon in hand); a character from before gets the same kit and keeps what it wears. What is worn and held is saved, and other players see the clothes and the weapon. The Clothes (give) and Weapons (give) tabs are developer tools: whatever they put on is given too.

## The weapons rack

`npm run swg -- weapons @SWG assets-private --retail-only` converts every weapon the game can hold into `assets-private/weapons/`, sorted by class from its template path: pistols, carbines, rifles, heavy weapons, one-hand swords (the batons and clubs with them), knives, two-hand swords (the axes with them), polearms and lances, fist weapons (knucklers, the punch dagger, the razor, the fan), lightsabers; a weapon whose appearance is a blade file is a lightsaber whatever folder it sits in (the named sabers under the sword and polearm folders). **I** opens the inventory; its Weapons tab is the rack: a row per weapon with a button per hand it can go in, and the kinds the game does not play yet (grenades and thrown weapons, turrets, mines and traps, the unarmed "weapon") listed at the bottom with why. A weapon goes on the hand's hold point exactly as the game hangs it there, and its class decides everything else: a blaster switches to the bounty hunter's kit with the pistol's or the rifle's carries (carbines and heavy weapons use the rifle's) and its own torso turns (`__debug.gun('carbine', { ready, aim, aimKneel })`, one set per kind); a sword or knife switches to the jedi's kit and fights with the fast, medium and strong styles (K cycles those alone), a blade in each hand (swords of either grip, knives, polearms, lightsabers; only the double-bladed staff stays in the right hand) fights as the dual style and nothing else, a polearm or lance fights as the staff, and a lightsaber from the rack hangs its own hilt where the placeholder's was with the blade out of it. A held blade sweeps its own length for hits. `__debug.equip('dl44')`, `__debug.equip('knife_survival', 'left')` and `__debug.equip(null, 'left')` do the same from the console, `__debug.weapons('sword')` lists matches. Every gun fires with its own sound and every bolt lands with the sound of what it struck: the pack carries each weapon effect's fire, its hit on a creature, on metal, on stone, on wood and on anything else, and its miss in water, on the ground and on nothing at all, and the game takes the muzzle, and the blow on a body, from the per-weapon rows of the game's own combat tables where they name that gun. `__debug.gunSounds('dl44')` prints one gun's whole set.

Every Force power now fires the game's own effect rather than the same blue-white spark: the client kept thirty-seven of them, their sounds are the ones its own client effects name, and the lightning, the choke and the drain are drawn with the beam appearances it kept beside them. The beams come off a small pool built with the world and compiled behind its loading screen, so nothing is made on the frame a power is first used, and a bolt fired at a wall ends on the wall rather than in the air. Four powers — the jump, the push, the repulse and the slow — have no effect in the archives at all and look exactly as they did; `__debug.powers()` prints every power's effect and whether it is the game's or ours, `__debug.powers({ on: 0 })` puts them all back as they were, and `__debug.forceLightning()` is the beams' own knob.

The rack, the garage and the NPC tab share one layout: a folding group per class or kind with its count in the heading (closed until opened, except the group holding what is in a hand, and every group a search finds something in), a grid of short names inside, read out of the ids (`sword_lightsaber_one_handed_gen4_must` shows as "Gen 4" tagged "must", `carbine_dc15` as "DC15"; the full id and the reach are in the tooltip), and **R** and **L** buttons for the hands. What is in each hand is written at the top.

With a blaster up, the barrel is measured against the crosshair every frame and the torso turned the rest of the way (a spine twist first, then the whole body's facing once the spine has turned 35 degrees), so the gun lines up in every carry: hip fire or aimed, standing, crouched or kneeling; lying down the game's prone poses are left as they are. The game's poses turn out to be nearly right on their own once the barrel is read along the model's true long axis (the manifests' bounds came with their corners swapped, which had put the "far end" of every weapon on the wrong axis). `__debug.gun()` reports the correction in degrees; `__debug.gun(undefined, { fix: false })` turns it off to see the poses bare, with the tuned turns (`gun('carbine', { aim: 30 })`) as they were. Prone, every shot plays the aimed prone shot clips, since the unaimed ones throw the off hand about. The camera's centre follows the posture: the standing eye height, lower crouched or kneeling, near the ground prone.

A lit lightsaber burns what it touches, as Jedi Academy's did: each blade is cast along its length against the world's fixed surfaces every frame, and where it meets one the stroke goes on from where that blade last touched, a red-hot line cooling to a dark char that fades over a dozen seconds; a blade dragged along a wall or swung through the floor draws one continuous stroke (`src/combat/saberMarks.ts`, one mesh of nine hundred pieces, the oldest overwritten; `__debug.marks()` counts them). Every mesh takes a burn but flesh: walls, floors, props, hulls and vehicles; creatures and fighters do not. A mark is laid in the world, so one left on a vehicle stays where the vehicle was. Swords and polearms burn nothing and throw no light: only a lightsaber (the placeholder's or a hilt from the rack) glows, and its light is the blade's own colour. Aboard a moving ship the blade's smear is kept in the hull's frame, so the ship's own motion draws none.

### Guns, after Jedi Academy's and the game's own

Every gun on the rack is one of seventeen kinds (`src/combat/guns.ts`), each with two triggers: the left mouse, and the middle mouse or Q for the other. The kind comes first from the client's own weapon effect: a gun's template names a family and an index into `datatables/weapon/weapon.iff`, whose row names the shot's particle effect, the muzzle flash and the hit effects, so the flame throwers (a fireball), the lightning rifles (a lightning beam), the slugthrowers (a tracer), the sonic and scatter guns, the bowcasters, the disruptors, the crossbows and the acid and rocket weapons declare themselves; a name settles what the table leaves plain (ion, carbonite, repeater, concussion, launcher), and the class settles the rest (pistols, carbines, rifles, heavy weapons). The converter carries the family and index in the manifest as `fx` with the effects converted into the pack's `particles/`, and the game draws the client's own bolt, flash and hit for every gun that flies a bolt, plus the flame, lightning, acid and ice beams for the held triggers.

The Jedi Academy kinds keep both of its triggers (`bg_weapons.c`, `g_weapon.c`): the pistol fires a bolt and charges on the other trigger to five times the damage; the rifle fires true and the other trigger is the rapid scatter, the carbine the other way round; the disruptor hits the instant it fires along a beam, and charges on the other trigger to four times the damage; the bowcaster charges into a fan of up to five bolts, or fires one that bounces off walls; the repeater streams, or lobs a slow concussive ball with a blast; the ion (DEMP2) bolt stuns and throws vehicles, or charges an ion blast; the flechette spreads five shards, or throws two bouncing mines that go off on a fuse or when something walks up; the rocket blasts where it lands, or follows what was under the crosshair; the concussion rifle's heavy bolt has a small blast, its other trigger an instant beam that throws whatever it hits. The game's own kinds are built the same way: the flame thrower is a cone of flame while the trigger is held, burning on for seconds after (the other trigger a fireball with an arc); the lightning rifle a bolt held on what is ahead that jumps to what stands near it (a ball of lightning on the other); the slugthrower a fast slug that drops over distance; the sonic guns a slow wide wave that staggers, with a pulse around you on the other trigger; acid eats at what it hits for seconds, or sprays; the crossbow's bolt falls as it flies; carbonite freezes what it hits to a crawl. Spread is less aimed and less in a steadier posture, a charge shows as a bar under the crosshair, and a plain bolt is a bright core with a glow tapering to the tail. `__debug.gunType()` says what the gun in hand is and what each trigger does.

### Fighters

The spawner's NPC tab (B) has **Fighter**: a humanoid of a random species from the character packs, with a random look (its height and every colour and choice the pack lets vary) and a weapon off the rack, a lightsaber half the time, else a sword or polearm or a gun. It thinks with the creatures' own brain (`src/world/mobiles/brain.ts`) and not with a temper of its own, so a fighter and a bantha are read out of one struct by one function: it takes the nearest thing its side picks a fight with within the brain's sight (32 m, which is the wildlife's number and shared with them), stares at a fresh one that is far off for half a second before it comes, and then keeps that one while it lives rather than turning to whoever walks closer; it swings a blade or a club once it is in reach (its blade drawn like yours, in a colour of its own), and fires a gun from a stand-off distance, at your middle with a little scatter, and only along a line it has really cast, so it no longer shoots through walls. It remembers everyone who hurt it for twenty seconds and turns on the most recent of them whatever side they are on, while a blow from its own side, from something that would never pick a fight with it, passes it by as it does a creature's. Past a leash from where it was stood — sixty metres outdoors, twenty-five inside a building — it breaks off, runs home and forgets its grudges; a target it cannot reach in height is given up after eight seconds and left alone for twenty; and with nothing to fight it wanders at a walk near where it was stood, standing and fidgeting one time in eight.
