Below a quarter of its health its nerve breaks and it runs from whoever hurt it, and from you, rather than fighting. It can be cut, shot, burned, stunned, frozen or gripped like anything else. Dead, it plays its incapacitation and lies there for a while. It dresses off its species' wardrobe: something on the chest, the legs and the feet, a hat, gloves or a back piece now and then, the pieces made for Wookiees (the Kashyyykian sets and the `wke` pieces) on Wookiees only and on every Wookiee; the Ithorians, whose skeleton is their own, dress from their own wardrobe. A piece made for the human skeleton put on another species' rig has its joints re-pointed by name (a Rodian has no lip or lid joints; those ride the root). The first fighter of a species parses that species' rig, two hundred megabytes of clips, which takes a few seconds; the clips are then shared, and each fighter's clothes are compiled a mesh at a time in the background before it shows, so later spawns cost a few short frames rather than one long one. `__debug.fighter(3)` stands three ahead, `__debug.fighter(1, 'wookiee')` one of a species, `__debug.fighter(0)` lists them with what they wear, and `__debug.fighters()` says what every one of them out is thinking and retunes what its body does. A first pass at the mind: no cover, no dodging and no rolling — the brain hands back a goal and a pace, and outdoors the fighter walks at it in a straight line. Indoors it does not: it walks the floors the client itself drew for each room, so it comes round the furniture and out through the doorway instead of pressing into the wall between it and you (see **Finding the way indoors**). It now walks that line **with the player's own body under it**: the same character controller on the same numbers (a capsule of the same radius and the same 1.6 m, autostep half a metre with a 0.2 m tread, 55 degrees climbed and 60 before it slides, snapped to the ground within 0.35 m), so it is stopped by a wall it used to walk through, climbs a sill, a kerb and a flight of stairs, is refused by a ladder-steep face, and takes its height from whatever it is really standing on instead of from a ray cast half a metre over its feet. Only what stands still stops it, so a crowd of fighters passes through itself rather than jamming and reporting itself stuck; outdoors the planet's own ground is a floor it may never go below, since the heightfield is only built near you; and a fighter in a building the player has walked a long way from — whose rooms have no collision just then — holds the height it had rather than falling through a floor that is no longer there. It also **stands and aims like a player**: a relaxed carry with the weapon down, the combat carry once there is something to fight and for five seconds after, and the aimed pose held over its legs with a target in front of it and in reach of the gun, each with its own idle, walk and run; the barrel is measured against where the next bolt is going and the difference goes into the spine up to 0.6 rad with the body turning onto the rest, which is `Player.correctAim` on the player's own numbers, held through the recoil and through a stagger rather than thrown away and won back between shots. `__debug.fighters()` prints each one's stance, its aim in degrees, how much of the ground it asked for it got, whether it is falling and whether the room it is in has a floor; `__debug.fighters({ stance: { … } })` and `{ body: { … } }` move all of it live. The Force powers reach a fighter the same way they reach a creature: it can be gripped, lifted off the ground, choked and thrown, and it falls where it lands rather than sliding along the ground. A fighter spawned inside a building stands on its floor, its room is followed through the doors as the player's is, and it leashes and wanders by that room rather than by the open ground.

A fighter also has a **posture** beside that carry: upright, crouched, kneeling or lying down. A gunner that stops to trade fire past six metres goes down on one knee and fires the game's own kneeling shots (twelve of them, six a weapon); one hurt past half its health at ten metres or more lies down instead, where its rig can be drawn lying down at all; a low body asked to move crouches and walks at the crouch clip's own pace rather than standing straight up, and a body asked to run, sent on a long walk, taken off its feet by a knock, a throw or a Force grip, or holding anything but a gun, stands. Being shot at does **not** stand it up: an ordinary bolt staggers a fighter for a fifth of a second and the posture deliberately does not read that, or a body would go upright for exactly as long as anybody was shooting at it, which is the one moment cover is for. The collision capsule and the point the whole game aims at move together with the posture and never apart, so a body that has gone down is really a smaller thing to shoot at — the shell is the player's own 1.6 m standing and 1.0 m low, and a bolt crossing at a standing body's chest passes clean over a low one. A crouched body takes no action at all, which is the client's own data rather than a choice: of the state hierarchy's 147 states twelve are the crouch and every one carries zero actions. `__debug.fighters({ posture: 'kneel' | 'crouch' | 'prone' | 'auto' })` puts every fighter out into one posture and holds it there (`auto` hands them back to the rule); `__debug.fighters({ postures: { … } })` moves when a body goes down (`kneelFrom`, `proneUnder`, `proneFrom`, `settle`, all invented); and `__debug.fighters({ body: { lowHalf: 0 } })` takes the low collision shell from 1.0 m to 0.7 m on every fighter already standing about, which is the knob if prone-as-cover is wanted rather than prone-as-a-pose. Each fighter's row gains `posture`, `wants` (what the rule asked for this frame; the two differ only for the length of the settle), `forced`, `shell: { tall, aimAt }` — how tall its collider really stands and what height the rest of the game shoots at — and `low: { canProne, crouchPace }`, which is why a body never lies down and how fast it goes when it is low. On a pack converted before the animation tag fix the kneeling carries and the kneeling shots resolve to nothing and a fighter kneels with its gun down and falls back on the additive recoil, the crouch stays Jedi Academy's, and a rig with no prone loop at all simply never lies down.

And **where a body is pointed and where it is going are now two numbers**. Until this every body in the game moved along its own heading — a creature sets its velocity from it, a fighter sets the ground it asks for from it — so keeping a gun on you and going anywhere were one act, and the only sidestep anything had was a twist of the whole body for a second. A fighter now has a heading (the way its feet go) and a facing (the way its gun points), and the drawn body carries the difference through the very machinery the aim already used: the spine takes up to 0.6 rad of it and the model turns onto the rest. Because there is **no strafe clip in either game** — none in a species rig's named clips and none in Jedi Academy's — a slide is held to what the legs can show (`GROUND_STEP.legMax`, 60 degrees), so it is a diagonal rather than a sideways slip; past that the gun gives way and the whole body turns to walk, which is what a body walking to a crate behind it or backing out of your face does. How far inside that ceiling any one body leans is its **tier's**: a body travelling at an angle off its own facing puts the sine of that angle of its speed sideways, so the tier's `strafe` share is read as `asin(strafe)` — nothing at the bottom two rungs, about 20 degrees at the third, 37 at the fourth and 58 at the top. At twenty degrees the aim's own spine fold takes the whole of it and the drawn body does not lean at all; at fifty-eight the body itself comes round, so the rungs really do tell apart by eye.

Three things ride on that split and each is a **tier's** to earn. `src/world/groundSkill.ts` is the ground's answer to `PILOT_SKILL` — five rungs, every number invented, covering a body's reaction, its scatter, how far off its nose it will pull the trigger, how much lead it takes, how disciplined its bursts are, whether it slides at all, how often it looks for cover and how far it will walk for a spot it can shoot back out of. A fighter is stood at tier 3, which is deliberately the body that already existed (its cone is today's exactly and its reaction and scatter sit inside today's span either side). `__debug.fighters({ tier: 5 })` moves every fighter out and every one stood after; **`__debug.fighters({ tier: 0 })` is the fighter this game had before any of this** — the flat shot clock, the box scatter, one heading, no slide and no cover — which is the comparison to make by eye. `__debug.fighters({ skill: { 5: { strafe: 0 } } })` moves one rung of the ladder in place, and `__debug.fighters({ step: { … } })` moves the movement's own numbers (the lean, the standoff ring, the slot each body takes on it, how long a slide runs, how near it lets an ally stand).

The trigger stops being a metronome: a fighter fired single shots every 0.35 to 0.65 seconds for ever with no gap, which is the whole of why it read as a machine, and it now fires a burst of its tier's length at its tier's spacing and then rests — which is the shape the creatures have always had. It holds a **standoff ring** at seven tenths of its gun's range rather than walking into your face, takes its own slot on that ring from its own key so two bodies never pick the same line, is nudged off any ally standing on top of it, and slides sideways in short bursts between which it settles, goes down on one knee and shoots. And it **takes cover**: `src/world/cover.ts` samples spots behind the streamer's own placed objects, casts one ray that must be blocked at a kneeling body's chest and one that must be clear at a standing body's, and keeps the nearest it can really walk to — so a gunner whose shot is blocked goes and stands behind something instead of walking into the open, which is what it used to do. Cover is **ours entirely and Half-Life 2's rather than the game's**: the archives carry a `Cover` state word and 139 commands gated on it and nothing anywhere that computes a position, because cover was a server-side state and the server never shipped. It is **outdoors only**, and that is the streamer's doing rather than a shortcut — an object placed inside a building has no collision at all, so cover in a cantina would look as though it worked and would stop no bolts. Each fighter's row gains `tier`, `lean` (how far its feet are off its gun this frame, in degrees; 0 for every body that has not earned the split), `cover` and `trigger`; `__debug.fighters()` also prints the searcher's own counters, whose `cover.blockers` reading 0 over a session in a town is the one way the whole thing quietly does nothing.

**Whether** a body wants cover is decided in the creatures' own brain (`decide`, the one both a fighter and a bantha think with) behind a flag only a fighter sets, so there is one ladder of rules and one set of tests rather than a second mind for the fighters: a body wants somewhere to stand while it is shooting, and while its shot is blocked and whoever it is fighting is within half again its own gun's reach. The chase underneath it is untouched, so a body that looks and finds nothing closes exactly as it always did. The flag is a capability and not a kind — a tier of its own, a gun in the hand, out of doors, not under a long walk — and deliberately not "has a ranged attack", which 2,550 of the catalogue's 5,140 entries are and most of them spit. A body that is really behind something is in the game's own **`Cover` state**: state 0 in the client's own table, beside Aiming and Alert, with 139 commands gated on it, and a state here rather than a field for that reason. It is a new word on the relay's own list, so **a server already running must be restarted** before another browser will accept it — nothing sends it yet in any case, since the bodies that take cover are the fighters and a fighter is nobody else's. A body standing in cover it cannot shoot back out of ducks behind it; one that can see out of its spot kneels and shoots, as it did before. Those two kinds are held for quite different lengths of time, and that is the point: a spot it **can** shoot out of is a firing position, so it is renewed for as long as it holds, while a spot it cannot is a **duck and not a position** — held three seconds from the moment the body is really in it and then given up, with eight seconds after it in which no such spot is worth taking at any price, so the body presses forward instead. Held alike, a hole was a one-way door: the body fires nothing from one, and the player cannot shoot it there either, so a gunner that ducked behind a wall simply stayed there. And a body that is **already** behind something claims where it stands rather than walking off to look — a rise in the ground counts as readily as a crate — which is what keeps the standoff ring from sliding it straight back out from behind the thing it was standing behind.

`__debug.cover()` is the whole of it in numbers, since none of it can be looked at from a tab that draws no frames: the searcher's counters and what its last search cost, the ladder's three cover columns a tier (how often it looks, how far it will walk, what a firing position is worth against a hole to hide in), and a row per fighter with its tier, its state, whether it wants cover, where its spot is, what that spot scored and how long ago it last looked. `__debug.cover({ reach: 20 })` moves the search's numbers live (the same knob as `__debug.fighters({ cover: … })`). `__debug.cover({ probe: true })` runs one real search **now**, from the nearest fighter's feet against you, and says what was offered to hide behind, which of those were too short or too narrow to be worth a ray, and which spot won and why; `__debug.cover({ probe: 2 })` picks a body by its row, and `__debug.cover({ probe: 'here' })` searches from **your own** feet against the nearest fighter, which is how to ask whether a place you have walked to is cover at all without standing a body on it. Read `ground` before anything else — how many placed objects have collision just now and how many of those have a shape to hide behind: nought blockers in a town is the near-object wire unconnected rather than a world without crates in it, and every counter below it reads exactly the same either way. In a body's own row, a spot with `blocker: -1` and `away: 0` is one it claimed where it already stood, and `restsFor` counting down is a body that has just come out of a hole and is pressing forward on purpose.

### Creatures, droids and NPCs (the catalogue)

The `mobiles` conversion holds every creature, droid and person the game shipped: 5,067 entries over 532 models and 150 animation packs. The game fetches the catalogue once at boot, behind the select screen. From the console, `await __debug.mobile('rancor', 14)` stands any entry that is on its own model, whether creature, droid, special or humanoid NPC (about 1,100 of them). The body is shaped from the template's collision values and the model's bind-pose box: an upright capsule, or a capsule plus hull balls along a long body, so a krayt dragon blocks along its whole length and a bolt in its tail hurts. It walks and runs at speeds held near its own clips' speeds, so its feet do not slide, and plays its pack's clips by role.

Each one has a brain on the world's simulated clock. It wanders near home, and an aggressive one hunts whatever it is hostile to (the player, fighters, the other side). A defensive one fights back when hurt, a skittish one runs, a passive one (a hologram, a vendor) never fights. It turns on whoever hurt it, its neighbours of the same group turn with it, and it goes home past a leash. Ranged ones spit (creatures) or fire a blaster bolt (droids and people). Allies do not hurt each other. A spawned one plays its incapacitation, falls to the physics a couple a frame, and is taken away ten seconds later. People are stood the same way, in their own clothes: a person on a parts model of their own, and a dressed NPC (a player species wearing the outfit its template names, from the species wardrobes and the wearables only NPCs wear, each piece in its own colours). Armed people hold a weapon off the rack: troopers a rifle, officers and the underworld a pistol, Wookiees a bowcaster and Trandoshans their hunting rifle whatever their job, the combat droids (IG-88, 4-LOM) a gun, and anyone else what their clips hold; a protocol or a surgical droid holds nothing. They fire that gun's bolt. The Jedi, Sith and Inquisitors carry a lightsaber, swing Jedi Academy's swings, and their blades light what is around them as the fighters' do. What a world has living in it on its own -- the lairs, herds and camps, the people at their posts -- is the catalogue's too, stood from the spawns pack's rows.

The NPC tab (B, then NPCs) lists the whole catalogue: creatures by family, droids, people on their own models, dressed NPCs by species and temper, the specials, and the holograms, pets, vendors and tutorial folk. It has a find box, category chips and a "ready only" switch. Each row has spawn and clear; each group has "one of each" (the first eight that can be stood, stopping at the first refusal and saying it) and clear. The counts refresh in place. Things stand a few metres ahead, on the floor when you are inside a building. The one model the game's own archives lack (the gubbur) is shown greyed as unavailable, and spawning it says why. Two settings in Graphics, Distance and detail: "Spawned creatures and NPCs" (how many may be out, 40) and "Creature animation range" (past it they hold their pose, 160 m).

### Finding the way indoors

Every room of every building in the game carries a walkable floor the client itself authored for it — the triangles a body may stand on, which triangle lies across each edge, and which doorway is behind an open one. The converter read the name of that file for years and threw the file away; it now reads it, and each pack carries it as `floors.json` beside the manifest. Inside a building a creature, an NPC or a fighter searches that mesh for the way to where it is going, pulls the corners off the walls, checks that its own width fits round each one, and walks at the next corner rather than at its goal, so it comes round a counter and out through a doorway instead of pressing into the wall nearest you. A body wider than the floor was drawn for pays for a check of its own at each corner and is refused one it cannot fit through.

It gives way in three steps, and the first two need no conversion at all. There is no floor mesh outdoors or for anything flying, so this half of it answers nothing there and the ground has a grid of its own (see **Finding the way outdoors**). In a pack converted before this the doorway polygons alone are still a room-to-room graph, which is enough to send a body at the right door out of the room it is in. Only a reconverted pack gets the real corners. Nothing is switched: `__debug.nav()` says which of the three a world is in (`converted: false` means the whole of it is asleep), how many rooms have been read, how many searches have been asked for and what each cost, and `__debug.nav({ reach: 0.6, every: 0.25 })` moves any of the invented numbers live. `__debug.nav({ describe: true })` reads every room of the building you are standing in and says what came out of each.

Two rules worth knowing. A lift shaft is never routed through: its stops are doorways with nothing between them, so a route through one walks a body into the air — and a fighter now falls rather than being teleported, so that would be a fall you could watch. And a body finds its way between the street and the rooms both ways (`src/world/nav/doorway.ts`). Each building's ways in from the street are worked out once in the world, each as a point just outside its doorway and a point just inside; a body outside chasing somebody who went in is walked to the best of them — the walk inside from the room they are in plus the walk outside to the door, so from behind a house with a back door it takes the back door — round the building on the ground's own grid, then through, and the moment the room it is in flips under it the building's floors take it on. From inside, a body after somebody in the street or another building leaves by the door nearest them rather than the one nearest itself. Where the goal is comes from the room the world follows it in (the player's own room, a body's, a fighter's), so a body chasing something nobody can place steers as it always did; and a creature or a fighter from the street that follows you in keeps the street's leash, not the room's, which would have turned it round on the doorstep. A blow does not go through a wall: a body whose foe stands on the other side of one walks round to the door instead of stopping to swing. It does go through a doorway, though -- a creature on the step bites somebody standing just inside -- because the rooms only say that a wall *could* be there, and whether one is is a line between the two against what stands still, asked only when the rooms put them in different places and only of a foe a blow could nearly reach.

### Finding the way outdoors

Out of doors there is no floor the client drew, so the converter bakes one: `npm run swg -- navgrid all assets-private` walks a whole world at the terrain's own two metres and writes, beside the manifest, which cells a body can stand on, which separate walkable region each of them belongs to, and how far each one stands from the nearest thing it cannot walk on. A body sent across a planet is then handed corners instead of a goal, exactly as it is indoors, and the grid can say the one thing nothing else in the game can — that a place cannot be walked to at all — instead of leaving a body leaning on a mesa all evening. A world nobody has baked plays exactly as it always did: every body in it steers straight at its goal.

Two things about it are the owner's calls rather than facts about the game, and both are worth knowing because they are visible.

**A body gives a mountain a wide berth.** A cell within eight metres of anything it cannot walk on costs the search more than a cell in the open, falling to exactly nothing at eight metres, so a route stands off a cliff or a town wall instead of scraping it, and the corner-pulling keeps that room rather than straightening the route back against the thing it went round. Open country pays nothing for this — past the berth the term is zero and the search opens the very cells it did before — and the surcharge is added and bounded, so a gap one cell wide that is the only way through is dearer and never closed. The one non-obvious part is the shape: the search buys its speed by accepting any route within a factor of the cheapest, so a surcharge only moves a route as far out as its own slope beats that, which is why it is a straight ramp and not a gentle curve. A gentler number does not give a gentler berth, it gives most of no berth at all. `__debug.nav({ outdoor: { berthCost: 0 } })` is the game with no berths in it, for comparison.

**The grid calls 45 degrees climbable, not the player's 55, and the world's people and creatures walk it as well as the fighters.** A fighter has the player's own character controller and really does climb 55; a person or a creature from the catalogue is a dynamic body driven by its velocity, the solver decides, and it was measured stopping at about 45 degrees at a walk and 49 flat out, with 50 stopping every body of every size. So the grid is cut to the body that can do least, at a walk, since a patrol, a wander and a body walking home all go at one. The game reads the angle each grid was baked at and hands the people and creatures no grid steeper than 45 (a world still carrying an older, steeper bake paths its fighters alone until `status` has asked for the re-bake and it has run), and no body wider than the metre the grid's margin leaves clear, so the biggest creatures still steer straight. The cost is real and is deliberate: ground between 45 and 55 degrees is ground a fighter could have walked up and a route for one may now go round it. A longer walk is the cheaper of the two mistakes — the other is a creature routed up a hill it cannot climb, leaning on it until its stuck watch gives the route away. `--slope=55` on the command bakes the first grid back.

It has one visible price, and the bake says so itself rather than leaving it to be found. A world's walkable ground comes in separate regions, and a place on one of them cannot be walked to from another; **two towns on Corellia — Bela Vistal and Kor Vella — stand on ground the world's main region does not reach** below 48 and 55 degrees, so a body sent to either is told it cannot get there and steers straight at it exactly as it did before there was a grid. Going from 47 degrees to 45 cut no other town on any world. Five towns were already in that state at any angle and always have been (Coronet, Tyrena and Vreni Island are cut off from Corellia's main region by water, Theed by Naboo's river, Labor Outpost by Yavin 4's), so this is two more of a thing the game has always had rather than something new in kind. The bake warns when it happens, `nav.json` carries the list, and `npm run swg -- status assets-private` prints it for every world.

A route is found on a sixteen-metre plane first and on the two-metre cells inside it after, and the plane offers a cell only where a third of it is open, so an alley between two buildings or a yard behind a gate can be a gap in the plane where the ground has a way through. A plan shorter than 64 metres that the plane refuses is therefore searched on the fine cells directly, in a band along the straight line between its ends (`nearFine`, ours; `__debug.nav({ outdoor: { nearFine: 0 } })` is the plan without it, and `outdoor.nearFine` counts the plans it found). Over every leg of the towns' patrol rounds it halves the legs the plane refuses, from 69 of 1,236 to 34; what is left are points the grid's own margin stands on or walls off, where a body steers straight as it always did.

Two rules of the bake decide how a building sits in the grid, and both were changed at once (`rules: 2` in `nav.json`; `status` asks for the bake again over a grid that does not say so, and `--skip-existing` bakes it). **A building's footprint is where its rooms come near the ground and the building rises out of it**: the ground over a cellar, a courtyard over the rooms dug under it (Fort Tusken's) and the yard over a house's basement are ground, where they used to be walled off as the building's floor because a room ceiling lay within three metres under them. **A wall stands off a body by the body's own half-width, not by a whole cell**: an object's cell is grown into a neighbour only across a side its geometry comes within 0.35 m of, where every blocked cell used to be grown a whole two metres on every side, which closed every lane narrower than about six metres. A line between two open cells can then run along the very side an object's cell shares with it, the 0.35 m off its geometry and no more, so a body wider than that is handed only legs that keep the rest of its width off anything the grid does not call open, and where none does, the path's own cell-to-cell steps (`legInset` in `src/world/nav/outdoorGrid.ts`): a creature a metre round is walked down a lane's middle rather than along its wall. The slope and the water still grow by the whole cell. Fort Tusken's seven doors on the ground used to let out onto five separate islands of one to forty-one cells; five of them now share the courtyard. Its gate is a single two-metre cell between the wall's ends and stays closed in the grid, so a body outside the compound still cannot be routed in. The bake prints, for every world, how many cells the first rule left open and in which models.

`__debug.nav()` says which world's grid is loaded, how many searches have been asked for and what each came to, and what the last one cost; `__debug.nav({ outdoor: { … } })` moves any of the invented numbers live.

### Sending a fighter somewhere, and watching how it does

`__debug.send('bestine')` sends the fighter nearest you to that named place and holds it to it: it runs there, takes no target, answers nobody who attacks it on the way and stops when it arrives. `__debug.send(-158, -112)` takes a point instead, in the same coordinates `__debug.teleport` does; `__debug.send()` reads the account back; `__debug.send({ track: 0 })` gives the one-second rows for `console.table`; `__debug.send({ stop: true })` calls every order off; `__debug.send({ fighter: 2 })` beside a destination picks the body rather than taking the nearest. The named places are the world's own, out of its pack, so the towns type as they are spoken — a world whose pack carries none says so and asks for coordinates.

There is nothing clever behind it. The creatures' brain, which a fighter thinks with, already has the rule as its very first one: past its leash, run home, take no target, until you are within two metres of home. So the order is the body's home moved onto the destination, and nothing in the brain is touched. That rule has two halves and the order needs both, which is worth knowing because the second is invisible: the leash half says only "the destination is further off than sixty metres", so an order to something in sight would never fire it at all, and the half that does the carrying there — and that carries the last sixty metres of every long walk — is the body's own "on the way home" state. The order holds that state itself, every step, rather than leaving it to the fact that nothing else in the game happens to move it. When the order ends, for any reason, the home is left **where the body is standing** and never put back where it started — put back, it would be kilometres from home and would run the whole way there.

The account is the point of it, and the one thing to read first is `proved`. The game builds placed-object collision only within 170 m of you and terrain collision only within about 192 m, and a fighter's last floor outdoors is the planet's height, which holds it up and never sideways. **So a body walking alone is stopped by nothing at all**: it walks through mesas and through buildings and arrives having proved nothing, and that arrival looks exactly like a success. The walk therefore samples its distance from you once a second, counts those samples in three buckets — the buildings and rocks solid, the ground only, nothing at all — and says `inconclusive` rather than `arrived` when it was mostly out there. Read the first of those three for exactly what it is: the streamer never builds collision for a prop under a metre and a half across, or for anything a building contains, at any distance whatever, so a body walking through a small crate beside you is the game as it stands and not a steering failure. **Follow it on a speeder**: that is the only way a long route is solid along its whole length. `__debug.advance` never calls `world.update`, so nothing streams under it and a walk driven by it is inconclusive by construction; `__debug.advanceCost(10)` says whether the helper is fast enough to be worth using for the short indoor checks it is still right for.

Beside the verdict the account carries where the body is along the route, how far it really walked against the straight line, where it first spent two seconds getting almost none of the ground it asked for, its stuck count, how often the floor of last resort had to lift it (both must stay 0 outdoors), how many paths it asked for, what its health did, how many terrain blocks were generated on the main thread while it walked (one stutter each — that is the world's one counter, so somebody following on a speeder is in the number too, and it says how stuttery the stretch was rather than what the fighter cost), and how many samples stood in water. Its stuck and lift counts are the walk's own, differenced against what the body had already collected before the order. Four things it cannot help and says so in its own `caveats`: it will not fight back, so an aggressive wild body can kill it; a fighter has no side-step at all, so one that wedges leans until the order's clock ends it; nothing in a fighter knows what water is, so it walks the lake bed — the first long walk belongs on a dry world; and the snapshot's own small props are never solid, while whatever is put down in play solid (a house, a travel terminal, a fitting, a player's prop) is solid at any size. A walk that ends stalled says which of the two stalls it was: it names the missing side-step only when it really did measure the body leaning on something, and says outright that it is not a wedge when it did not. Every number of the account is ours and lives through `__debug.send({ tune: { … } })`, except the two distances the verdict is made of, which are the running game's own and are refused from the console.

### Death and ragdolls

Nothing fades when you die: the body falls where it stood and the camera stays on it, with a card offering **Respawn**. The body is a ragdoll (`src/combat/ragdoll.ts`) built from the skeleton itself: every bone long enough gets a capsule from itself to its children, hinged to its nearest ancestor's by a ball joint, and each frame the bones are posed from the bodies so the skin follows; it starts from the pose the last animation frame left, so a creature plays its incapacitation and then falls on from that pose, and a fighter its death clip. A dead creature's or fighter's body lies ten seconds and is gone. Ragdolls touch only what stands still (the ground, buildings, a ship's rooms when aboard) and never the living, vehicles or one another: a physics hook drops those contacts, and the character controller walks through them. `__debug.kill()`, `kill('creatures')` and `kill('fighters')` are the quick way to see them fall. Every joint is sprung towards the pose it died in (a torque applied each frame, stiffening past a limit; the engine's own joint motors and limits fought one another and never settled), so a body sags and folds under its weight rather than into a heap, and once the whole body has been near still for a second its pieces are stopped and put to sleep, so it lies as it fell. The springs fade out over the first seconds, so they shape the fall and then let the body lie loose. The numbers are at the top of `src/combat/ragdoll.ts` and live in the console: `__debug.ragdoll({ stiffness: 80, limit: 1 })` changes them for the next body to fall (the sleep numbers at once), and `__debug.ragdoll()` reads them with every fallen body's speed and whether it sleeps. A creature stood by hand from the NPC tab lies where it fell and is then taken away, and so does one of a lair's or a person at a post, whose own lair or row stands another on its own clock. A creature or NPC stood from the catalogue lies where it fell and is then taken away; the ragdolls of many deaths at once start a couple a frame, nearest first, the rest holding their death pose meanwhile.

`__debug.physics()` is the engine's own step: how many steps the world has taken, its bodies and colliders, whether it has broken and on what (a panic of the engine's, in its step or on any other call into it, leaves that world unusable for good; see **Common problems**), and whether the game has stopped for it. A break stops the frames at once, saves the character where it stands and reloads the page straight back into it, unless it happened again within two minutes of the last; `__debug.physics({ reload: false })` leaves the game stopped instead (the last picture on the screen, nothing moving, the pointer handed back), which a driven tab wants, since a reload takes its frame pump with it.

### Aboard a ship

Fighting inside a ship's rooms happens in the hull's frame: a bolt fired aboard flies against the room's own walls and is drawn where the hull carries it, a thrown saber flies and comes back in the same frame, the blade's smear stays with the room, and nothing done inside cuts the hull down (the blades, bolts, blasts and Force pushes all leave the ship you are in alone). A hull blown up or removed with people still in its rooms puts them out into the world where it was, with its motion: adrift in space, falling on a planet. `__debug.wreck()` blows up the vehicle you are on or in.

### Skills: Force powers, gadgets, bare hands

The number keys 1 to 6 use whatever is in their slots, and the inventory's **Skills** tab (I) picks it for the class in play: a row per skill with the slot buttons, the choice kept with the character (a Jedi's powers and a Bounty Hunter's gadgets separately). A tap skill fires on the key, a hold skill lasts while it is down, a toggle until the key again.

The Jedi's are the Force powers (`src/combat/forcePowers.ts`): the four the game started with (Jump, Speed, Push, Lightning) and, after Jedi Academy, The Force Unleashed and Jedi: Survivor, Pull (everything ahead dragged to your feet), Grip (hold: the creature under the crosshair lifted and held in the air, choking; let go to throw it), Drain (hold: life drawn out of what is ahead into you), Repulse (a blast in every direction), Slow (the creature under the crosshair at a crawl for five seconds), Heal, Protect (toggle: what hurts you counts for a third) and Rage (ten seconds faster and half again as hard with the blade, paid in health, then a rest). `__debug.powers(['grip', 'pull', null, 'repulse'])` sets the slots from the console, `__debug.creature(8)` stands a creature ahead to try them on.

The Bounty Hunter's are the gadgets (`src/combat/gadgets.ts`): the game's grenades, each after its own flavour text and played the way Jedi Academy plays a thrown charge (an arc from the hand with a little lift, bouncing where it lands, off on its fuse, a blast that hurts and throws everything within its reach, you too when close). The thermal detonator is the strongest, the fragmentation grenade quicker and lighter, the proton grenade made for machines (vehicles and turrets take it three times over), the Imperial detonator the biggest and it stuns what it leaves standing, cryoban freezes everything in it to a crawl for six seconds, glop sticks what it splashes for two seconds and slows it after, the poison grenade and the Kashyyyk bug bomb leave a cloud that hangs for seconds and keeps hurting whoever stands in it. Each flies as the rack's model for it (the weapons pack converts the grenade templates now; before a reconversion, a plain ball). From Jedi Academy come the trip mine (set on the wall or floor under the crosshair within four metres, a red laser out from it; whatever crosses the beam sets it off, you included) and the det pack (stuck where the crosshair is, or thrown when nothing is near; the key again sets every one off; a blast near a mine or a pack sets it off too), and the stim pack mends forty-five health. `__debug.gadgets(['cryoban', 'trip_mine', 'det_pack'])` sets the slots from the console.

Bare Hands is a toggle both classes can carry: the weapon is put away and hidden, the left mouse punches and the right kicks, the game's own brawling clips (`unarmed_standing_ready_jab`, `_lead_uppercut`, `_rear_roundhouse_kic` and kin; the player pack converts them now, so reconvert with `player`) or, without them, Jedi Academy's two punches and its kicks. A punch does a little and shoves a little, a kick more of both, each landing part way into its clip on whatever is within reach ahead of the chest.

### Lightsabers

The game's crafted sabers live under the melee templates (`object/weapon/melee/{sword,2h_sword,polearm}/crafted_saber/`), and the converter sorts them into three classes of their own: lightsabers (one hand: the fast, medium and strong styles), two-hand lightsabers (medium and strong) and double-bladed lightsabers (the staff style, a blade out of each end). Each one's appearance is a blade file (`.lsb`) that names the hilt's model and the blade the client drew from it: its length and width in metres, and the seconds it took to ignite and retract; the converter keeps those in the manifest as `blade`, so a hilt's blade is as long as the game made it, and the tips the hits are swept to move with it.

The blade itself is not the game's sprite: it is drawn as the films have it, a white core inside a coloured glow (two camera-facing ribbons, so it reads the same from every side, additive and never writing depth, so crossing blades add up), and behind it a smear, the surface the blade swept over the last fraction of a second, fading with age, so a blade in motion looks drawn out along its path. A swing sweeps far and the smear is long; walking with the blade out sweeps little, so it shimmers; a still blade has none. `src/combat/saberBlade.ts` has the numbers (the smear's life at rest and in a swing, the core's share of the width, the brightness the bloom catches). The pooled light the blade throws takes its colour, softened toward white.

The rack's Weapons tab starts with **Blade colour**: the game's own 48 (from `palette/wp_lightsaber.pal`, written to the manifest as `saberColors`) as swatches, and a colour picker for any colour at all. The choice goes on every blade at once (the staff's second, the dual style's left, the thrown and orbiting ones) and into the character's record, so it comes back when the character is played again. Other players' blades are not drawn in their colour yet, and hilt styling beyond the game's own hilts is still to come.

## When something fails to draw

A mesh that throws inside the renderer (a broken attribute, a missing uniform) used to abort the whole frame from that pass on, so the water and the doorways flickered whenever it came into view, and since the frame ended before the mouse movement was spent, the view slid on with a momentum of its own. Now the camera spends the movement as it reads it, a frame that throws is caught and logged, and the portal renderer finds the object that fails by drawing the candidates one at a time, hides it, and prints its name, geometry attributes and material (`render: hid an object that fails to draw`); `__debug.passLog()` and the renderer's `broken` list say what was hidden. Paste that line to have the converter fixed for it.

A terminal screen or sign whose flip-book names frames the archives lack draws its first frame, still (the converter logs `flip-book <shader>: base <base>, N of M frames missing`); when the first frame is missing too it draws untextured, as it always did. `npm run swg -- shader @SWG <shader> --retail-only` prints what a shader's forms and its effect say (flip-book timing and frames, the pass's blend and test at its version's offsets, scroll rates, split alpha, how it glows) and what the converter made of it; `materials` prints the same decision per shader as a `surface:` line.

## Vehicles and the garage

Every vehicle is one physics body held up by springs at the corners of its footprint (or by its feet), driven by a spec that says how it handles (`src/vehicles/vehicle.ts`). There are four kinds, and the kind is read from the vehicle's name (`vehicleKindOf`); when a name says nothing the garage guesses and marks it:

- **Podracer** (`podracer`): the fastest thing there is (85 m/s, 125 boosting), a wide drifting turn that redirects its speed rather than scrubbing it, banking into corners, and a **heat** boost after Star Wars: Racer: holding Shift builds heat for about four seconds; at the top the engine burns out and the pod limps at a third of its power for three seconds, and the heat has to fall before it boosts again. The mouse steers it like everything else; **A** and **D** are its air brakes, as in Racer: the nose yaws round hard, the pod banks further and slides wide while they are held. The pods whose animation tables carry them (Gasgano's, Mawhonic's, the light-bending one) spin their engines with their own clips at speed. The prompt shows the heat bar and the speed.
- **Speeder bike** (`speederbike`: swoops, STAPs, BARCs, flash speeders): quick and tight (42 m/s, 60 boosting), bites into turns, banks, a **burst** boost that runs down and recharges, and a hop on Space.
- **Ground** (`ground`: walkers, wheel bikes, tanks, and every animal mount): slow, turns in place, never slides. An animal from the creatures pack walks and runs with its own clips at its own pace under a saddle on its back, and can hop; the creatures pack holds every mount the game sold (the 52 creatures its saddle map lists: banthas, dewbacks, kaadu, cu pa, varactyls, tauntauns, rontos, blurrgs and the rest, `creatures --no-mounts` leaves them out) beside the planets' own creatures, the garage marking which is a mount and which a wild thing. A walker (the AT-ST, the AT-XT, the basilisk, the wheel bike) has an animation table of its own and walks and runs with its clips too; the gallery's `vehicles` conversion keeps them, and the game plays them by speed as it does an animal's. A vehicle's hull collides with everything but the ground: the springs hold it off the terrain from their own rays, and a hull that also met the heightfield dug its underside or its feet into every slope, snagging and jolting there.
- **Flyer** (`flyer`: landspeeders and skiffs as flying cars, gunships and airspeeders as aircraft): looking up climbs and looking down sinks (Space and X do too); the vehicle holds its height over the ground meanwhile and settles back down when nobody drives it. A flying car climbs slowly to 60 m, an aircraft fast to 260 m.
- **Ship** (`ship`: the player ships from the `ships` pack): a first flight model. W builds speed and S bleeds it (no key holds the speed); past a few metres a second the ship lifts off and is flown by hand, free of gravity and of any levelling: the mouse moves a cursor that stays where it is left, and outside the small aim circle in the middle the ship's stick points at it (harder the further out, for as long as it is held there), asking for turning rates about the ship's own axes, and the ship's inertia lets those rates build up and die away over a moment (half a second for a fighter, longer for a freighter: `__debug.vehicleTune({ inertia: 1 })` changes it), so a turn carries on a little after the cursor comes back into the circle; A and D roll it, Space and X pitch it, the flight display draws the aim circle where the ship's nose points and the cursor as a diamond inside it, held at the rim with an arrow behind it once the stick is pushed further out, brightening with how hard the ship is being asked to turn, and it can fly on its back and loop, the camera following it in its own frame so the world turns over with it, a quarter of a second behind so a turn shows the ship swinging and banking against the view; the wheel sets how far back it sits, scaled to the ship, and all the way in is the cockpit view, on the model's cockpit hardpoint when it has one (the hull is hidden meanwhile; Alt looks around from the orbit instead). Near the ground or the ceiling a light hand eases the nose toward the horizon, only while the stick is slack, so a pilot who keeps pushing flies into the ground: the ship stops dead, drops onto its gear, and the rider takes damage by the speed. Below a few metres a second it settles, levels and lands on its gear as a flyer. Fighters are quick, freighters ponderous. The engine glow sits on the hull's engine hardpoints when the model has them (the engines themselves are parts a player fits in the game, so the hulls carry only the sockets), else at the rear. Interiors are converted but not entered yet.

The mouse steers every kind: the vehicle turns toward where the camera looks (A/D steer directly while held), and holding **Alt** frees the camera to look around. Vehicles lean into their turns. A speeder bike rides half a metre off the ground, a flying car the same. Machines float on water at their ride height and mounts swim chest-deep; a vehicle's wake is wider, stronger and less even than a swimmer's, with spray past a brisk pace. Every machine trails dust in the colour of the ground it runs over (the terrain texture's mean colour on a real planet), more the faster it goes, and none over water or in the air, and carries engine glows at its rear that brighten with the throttle and the boost (orange on a podracer, blue on a repulsor drive; dimmer and flickering while burnt out). The rider sits the way the game seats a rider on that vehicle: the riding loop has a branch per rider pose (`loop_riding:vehicle_speeder_bike`, `vehicle_landspeeder`, `vehicle_hover_chair`, the saddle poses of the mounts, `space_sitting` for a pilot's chair), and the converter reads the game's mount tables (`datatables/mount/`: a skeletal appearance to its logical saddle, the saddle to its appearance, the appearance and seat to the pose) into each vehicle's and creature's manifest entry, so a speeder bike's rider crouches over the bars, a landspeeder's sits, a bantha's rides the wide saddle, a ship's pilot sits in the chair; a vehicle the tables lack is tried by its name (`vehicle_<id>`), and the plain `loop_riding` is the game's default saddle. The game's own vehicles seat the rider at the vehicle's origin and the riding clip's root offset puts the pelvis in the seat (a speeder bike's rider hardpoint is exactly its clip's root); a mount wears the saddle the mount tables name, hung on the saddle hardpoint its own mesh carries under one of its joints (the 22 mounts the game let players ride; for the bantha, kaadu, bol and rancor the hardpoint is read from the tables' own `_hue` appearance), and the rider's pelvis sits on the saddle's own `player` point, which is exactly where the game's saddle poses put it, so the saddle and the rider ride the joint together at a walk and a run; a creature the tables list that the game never saddled wears its saddle on the top of its back as its idle stands it (the back, never a neck held up over it), the basilisk seats its rider on its own `player` point, a wild creature's bare back takes the seat, and `__debug.seat()` says which (`seatFrom`) and whether a saddle is worn; a ship's pilot is placed by the cockpit's own camera point: the eyes go there (the B-wing's view takes half of its cockpit file's first-person offset) and the body hangs under them as the chair pose's first frame has it, so in first and third person alike the pilot's head is where the view is. `__debug.seat(right, up, forward)` nudges the ridden vehicle's seat live and reports the pose and its root, for finding a seat by eye; in a ship it moves the body under the eye while the view stays put, and prints a `paste` line for the per-frame table (`COCKPIT_BODY_NUDGE` in `src/vehicles/cockpitSeat.ts`). A fast vehicle can outrun the physics ground being streamed in, so the terrain's own height is a floor under every vehicle. Every vehicle's turning is asked for as an acceleration and scaled by its own inertia (a box of its measured size, never smaller than a bike), so a barge and a swoop right themselves alike, and the springs' ground rays start inside the body so a corner pushed into a slope is lifted out rather than left to tumble. A machine's box is measured from its model when it is spawned, since the pack's bounds can be another mesh's. Both blaster fire and Force Push still shove them.

**B** opens the spawner; its Garage tab lists every vehicle the gallery pack holds (`gallery --only=vehicles`) and every creature in the creatures pack, listed by kind, with a button that stands one in front of you on clear ground (ahead by its own half length and a gap, and further on while an exhibit, a house or another vehicle is in the way, since a box spawned inside something is thrown out of it), a "try it as" choice to drive any model with another kind's handling, and Remove all. A speeder or walker whose shaders take colours (20 models of the gallery's: the landspeeders, swoops, skiffs, the BARC, the AT-PT and AT-RT, the basilisk) has a **paint** button beside it, which opens the ship edit page with its paint alone: its own palettes, and every colour a garment takes through the same picker, kept with the character, put on the speeders already spawned when the page closes and seen by the others while it is ridden; a gallery converted before speeders took paint shows no button until `gallery --only=vehicles` is run again. Walk up to any side of it and press **E** to ride (within 3.6 m of its edge, so a barge is mounted from its side), **E** again to get off. In the console, `__debug.vehicles('speeder')` lists what can be spawned and what stands on the world, `__debug.spawn('speeder_ab1')` or `__debug.spawn('bantha', 'ground')` stands one ahead of you, `__debug.spawnBox(2, 1.5, 5, 'podracer')` a plain box of that size as any kind, `__debug.mount()` rides the nearest, `__debug.vehicleState()` reports where each stands, how level it is (pitch and roll), its spin and speed and how many corners find the ground, `__debug.vehicleTune({ bank: 0, turnRate: 1.2 })` changes the ridden one's handling live, and `__debug.unspawn()` clears them. A vehicle's rider pose shows in the spawn message; `__debug.player()` names the clip the rig plays.

A ship the `ships` pack gave a fit has an **edit** button beside **spawn**. It opens the ship's page, styled like the Appearance tab: the ship turning on the left on its own renderer (drag turns it, right-drag pans, the wheel zooms, a double-click resets, "wings open" opens them at their own pace), and on the right its paint (the pattern, and the two colours from the game's own palettes or any colour a garment takes: each colour is a strip that opens the appearance page's own picker under it, Own the hull's palette, then every garment palette, every creator palette and all the garment colours in one grid, a colour from anywhere but Own carried whole; the pattern stays a choice of the game's; the TIEs and a few others say their paint is fixed), every slot of its chassis with the components the game allowed in it, grouped by the look each shows on this hull ("Look 2 (115)", "No model on this hull"), the droid (an astromech in the socket on the eight hulls that have one, the N-1 wearing its own droid head; a flight computer elsewhere, which shows nothing), and the systems that change nothing to look at. A pick shows on the preview within a second (new parts are compiled a piece at a time and painted before they are swapped in; paint renders off the main thread) and is kept with the character; **Spawn** on the page stands the ship as edited, as does the garage's spawn button afterwards; closing the page refits the ships of that kind already spawned, in place, and, when it is the ship you fly or last flew or stood out, tells the other players once. **Stock** goes back to the game's stock parts, no droid and the shaders' own colours.

## Ships (a start)

`npm run swg -- ships @SWG assets-private --retail-only` converts every ship a player can fly (`object/ship/player/`) into `assets-private/ships/`: the exterior as a model, and for the multi-crew ships the interior the template names (`interiorLayoutFileName`, a portal building) as a second model with its cells, all listed in `manifest.json` by class (starfighters, bombers, freighters, gunships, shuttles). They appear at the bottom of the garage (G) and fly with the first flight model above. Still to come: entering the interiors (the cells are converted, the doors are not wired to a moving ship yet), the game's own cockpit hardpoints for the pilot, weapons, and space itself.

## The gallery (a development world)

`npm run swg -- gallery @SWG assets-private --retail-only --jka=@JKA` builds `assets-private/gallery/`: a flat world under Tatooine's sky with every player house (interiors and all, walk in through the doors), every vehicle and every weapon in rows, each with its name over it, and every animation from both games on a grid of player models six feet apart, sorted by category (SWG's locomotion, pistol, rifle, one-hand and two-hand melee, polearm, unarmed, emotes, reactions and so on; Jedi Academy's stances, each saber style's swings, the specials and katas, parries, jumps and rolls, Force powers, deaths) with the clip's name over each. A clip that moves part of the skeleton only is layered over the idle on its joints, and the game's additive clips (`add_pistol_fire_N` and kin, deltas that would stand as T-poses on their own) are added to the idle the way the game adds them to whatever plays. Play it with `?planet=gallery` or from the galaxy map. Only the mannequins within 12 m of you animate (the rest of the grid is labels), so the grid can hold thousands of clips; `__debug.gallery(30)` widens that to 30 m. `--only=houses,vehicles,weapons,anims,interiors` rebuilds part of it and keeps the rest from the last build, `--limit=N` a sample. A skinned vehicle keeps its mesh's own hardpoints as nodes under their joints, as a mount does, and lists their names in its manifest entry: a pod racer's pilot sits on its own `player` point, and `status` asks for the gallery again while no pod lists one. A vehicle whose shaders take colours is converted as a ship's hull is: its paint shaders baked at their defaults through every pass (so it shows the game's own default colours rather than its uncoloured texture), their recipes in the gallery's own `customize.json` with their pictures under `customize/` (the ships command's own recipe maker, `tools/swg/paintrecipes.mjs`), and its `paint` (`{ shaders, variables }`, the ships pack's shape) on its model's manifest entry, the manifest stamped `paintFormat`; `status` asks for `gallery --only=vehicles` while a gallery with vehicles carries no stamp. Measured on the retail archives: 20 of the 51 vehicle models, 32 recipes, 68 pictures (15.9 MB), about 18 s for the vehicles alone.

The last section, **Interiors**, is every structure with rooms once each: the converter follows every building and ship template to its portal layout and shows one exhibit per layout (the same cantina stands on three planets, a station in every zone), so the world's buildings, the space stations and the ships with interiors (the yacht, the corvette, the gunboats, the Star Destroyer, the Y-8) stand in wide rows with their names over them, walk-in interiors and all. A layout whose rooms include a lift shaft (a cell named `elevator1`, `reactorlift`, `empelevator`) is labelled **(lift)**, which is how the buildings with elevators are found on the ground. The game's lifts were objects the server spawned in those shafts, and the shafts have no floor at their upper levels, so the shaft's doorways are its stops: stand in it and `E` opens the lift menu, the levels the shaft reaches by the rooms they open into (`src/world/lifts.ts`), and a pick steps you out through that level's doorway. A shaft that opens into another shaft, or one split in two by name (`elevator_e3_up` and `elevator_e3_down`, the Star Destroyer's), shares its stops with it. The same works aboard a ship's rooms. Twenty-odd layouts have shafts: the hospitals (three levels), the civilian association halls, the Emperor's retreat, the Lucky Despot, the Warren, the merc bunker (two shafts, three levels), the corvette and Star Destroyer dungeons, the spires and the yacht among them. The dungeons whose way in was a server object (the Old Republic facility, the tombs and caves, the corvette and Star Destroyer dungeons, the chapter 8 station) stand as hidden rooms with nothing to see outside: within their footprint `E` offers to put you inside, in the room their door would have opened into. In the console, `__debug.gallery()` names the animation slot nearest you and `__debug.anim('name')` plays any clip the player's rig has on the player, so a saber style can be assembled by naming its clips.

## Ship interiors (a start: the physics)

A multi-crew ship's interior is a still room of its own. Its meshes hang inside the hull in the world, so it moves, banks and rolls with the ship, but its physics lives in a Rapier world of its own in the hull's frame, never rotated, with gravity straight down (`src/vehicles/interior.ts`). Whoever is aboard has their body in that world and walks, jumps and stands in the still room; each frame the figure is drawn where the hull's transform puts it. The hull can be thrown about and nobody inside feels it, which is how Garry's Mod's Gravity Hull Designator did it. The rooms come one of two ways: a separate interior model the ship's template names (the YT-1300s, when the archives hold it), or the hull model itself when it is a portal building (the Sorosuub yacht), its rooms converted as cells beside the shell; the game finds either without a new conversion, measures the vehicle's collider from the shell alone (the game's rooms are often larger than the hull around them), and shows a hull's own rooms only to whoever is aboard until the windows are portals (while someone is aboard the hull's glass turns clear so those outside see in; otherwise it is the game's own solid pane), a vehicle's collision is its model's own triangles (a portal building's shell alone, cleaned of degenerate triangles) rather than a box, and the figure no longer pushes the vehicles it walks into (the engine's push against a hull's triangles crashed it; `__debug.pushBodies(true)` turns it back on to check), and the glass casts no shadow, so the sun (and the moon, at night) falls into the rooms through the windows. Aboard, the camera's frame is the hull's: the orbit, its up and the mouse's yaw all follow the room, so the view stays upright however the hull banks. Windows are drawn as the game draws them: a hull's window shader is opaque and reflective (its texture's alpha is the reflection mask, not transparency), a room's window opening is an invisible collidable surface, drawn as nothing and kept for the colliders, and from inside the hull's single-sided window is seen from behind, so the outside shows through; the converter only marks glass-named shaders (by the shader's or its texture's name: glass, window, windshield, canopy, viewport, pane) so the sun comes through them and they clear while someone is aboard, a glassy name covering more than a third of a shell is taken for its skin and kept solid, and `ships --glass=<regex>` marks more shaders for a ship whose windows are named otherwise (`materials --ship=<id>` shows the names); `npm run swg -- materials @SWG appearance/sorosuub_space_yacht.pob` lists every shader a hull uses with what the converter made of it, and `ships --glass=<regex>` names more of them as glass, `--match=yacht` redoing that ship alone. Near a ship with an interior, **E** steps in at the room's entry (a hardpoint naming the way in when the rooms carry one, else a clear spot on the floor of its entry room, or its first room, found under a grid of points with a ceiling above and head and elbow room; the console lists every room's hardpoints on spawning). Aboard, the rooms' own lights from the portal file, the nearest three, shine through the effects' light pool (no new lights, so nothing recompiles), and the shell casts no shadow while someone is aboard, since the rooms lie where its shadow would fall and would never see the sun rather than taking the pilot's seat, and E again steps out beside the hull; falling out of the room (through a door while it moves) drops you into the world with the hull's motion. `__debug.shipDrift(2, 0.4)` sets the hull adrift at 2 m/s spinning 0.4 rad/s with no gravity or righting, to try the room while it moves; `__debug.shipDrift(0)` brings it to rest. A ship with a bridge among its rooms is flown from it: at the front of the bridge **E** takes the controls (the figure stands there, still in the room's own physics, while W/S and the mouse fly the ship as from a seat; the view is from the bridge in first person), and **E** again lets go, in flight too (the ship carries on as it was; there is no landing place in space). Still to come: the room's windows as exits in the portal renderer so the world shows through them; the relay carrying who is aboard what; the lifts and terminals the server spawns into the rooms.

## Ship attachments and cockpits

A ship's client data (`clientdata/ship/client_shared_<ship>.cdf`, named by its template) says what hangs on the hull beyond its own mesh, and the `ships` command reads it: the wings (objects of their own, each standing at its own place in its parent's frame and turning about its own Z; a wing's own client data can hold more wings, as the B-wing body holds its two foils); the carriers the client keeps beside its forms (turret bodies and barrels, radar dishes, the Black Sun wing structures, the Hutt wing pieces); "engine on" appearances shown while the drive runs, or while boosting for a booster's; the hardpoints its thruster effects sit at, which place the engine glows, else any hardpoint named for an exhaust; and the effect and smoke of each damage band, kept in the manifest for later. The engines, guns and boosters are attachments of their own (`object/tangible/ship/attachment/engine/shared_xwing_engine_pos_s01.iff` and the like), and which ones a ship carries as sold comes from its chassis tables (`datatables/space/ship_chassis_<chassis>.iff`, each slot's looks as `attachment:hardpoint` lists): each slot's most common look, leaving out the `modification_*` slots (one reward look each, the X-wing's side strakes among them), so the X-wing has one gun per hardpoint and the Jedi starfighter its own engines and guns. Every part's own client data is read in turn, and a hardpoint it names is looked for on that part, then on what already hangs under it (a turret's barrels are on its body), then on the whole ship; the manifest writes the lot as a tree, 596 parts over the 63 ships (`yt1300_engine_none`, the empty engine slot's stand-in, is written but not shown while the slot is filled). Each part is turned the way its hardpoint faces, and its own glow hardpoints place the engine glows (the client data's thruster points, then any exhaust, then any numbered engine hardpoint, when there are none), sized by the hull's height. A ship's hardpoints live in its `.lod` appearance, which the converter now reads through (`materials --ship=<id>` lists them). The cockpit file the template names (`cockpit/<ship>.iff`) gives the frame drawn around the pilot, converted as `<ship>_cockpit.glb`; it is authored in the ship's own space, so it hangs on the model at its origin and lands in the canopy by itself, and its one hardpoint, `camera`, is the game's own first-person eye: that point with the file's first-person offset is where first person looks from, hovering and flying alike, so the view never moves at lift-off. The game never drew a pilot in a fighter, so the body hangs under that eye as the chair pose has it and sits on the cushion one downward ray finds in the frame's triangles when the ship spawns (the console's `garage: <ship> cockpit:` line gives the eye, the seat and how far the body was moved); in first person it rises no more than 0.10 m, which keeps the view clear of the collar. It shows while someone is at the controls, seen from outside through the canopy, which clears like a hull's windows, and in first person the view sits inside it and the hull stays in the picture (`__debug.cockpitFrame(right, up, forward)` nudges the frame and its eye live, for checking). A gun's hardpoint is never taken for a seat or an eye (the gunboats' `pilotmuzzle1` is a gun), and a ship without a frame, which is always one with rooms, is flown from its bridge; seated in one, the rider is not drawn. A ship with rooms has a bridge instead of a frame. Zoomed all the way in, the view is locked to the hull hovering as in flight; **Alt** looks round from the same eye in the ship's frame, starting at the nose (a bridge pilot looks from their own eyes), and the wheel zooms out from there in steps sized to the ship; each engine leaves a ribbon of exhaust behind it, longer the faster it goes.

What a hull can be fitted with comes from the game's own tables, not from names: each slot of its chassis (reactor, engine, shields, armour, capacitor, booster, droid interface, cargo, modifications, guns, launchers, countermeasures) takes the components whose class the chassis names, and the hull's looks table says which model each shows there, so an engine or a gun has up to seven looks on the X-wing. The ship as sold carries each slot's stock component, from the look the most of its components show: the X-wing has its Incom Advanced Blasters, one per hardpoint, and no side strakes, which were a reward. It fires that gun's own bolt. The fighters with an astromech socket (the X-wings, the Y-wings, the Jedi starfighter, the N-1, the ARC-170 and the V-wing) take R2 to R5, the N-1 as its own droid head; the rest take a flight computer. 41 hulls have paint the game let players change, a pattern and two colours from the game's palettes. The models wear each shader's default pattern and colours, every pass baked, as the client drew a ship nobody painted.

A ship converted with its fit is built from the fit its character keeps (the stock one until it is edited): the manifest's tree without its stock components, then the fitted components' own parts, hung by hardpoint name over the whole ship in as many passes as they need (the Hutt heavy's booster hangs on its engine part; a part nothing carries waits and is named in the console, never put at the origin), each with the parts its own client data hangs on it (the ones it does not carry itself hung by name after every fitted part is on), and the droid in the astromech socket as its own model at rest, sunk so only its dome and the top of its body show (the N-1 wears the game's droid head); an empty slot's stand-in (the YT-1300's engine cover) shows while the slot is empty and comes off when it is filled, on a spawned ship too. A ship already spawned changes in place when its fit changes, one change at a time: the new parts are loaded, compiled in their own materials and then in the ship's paint, and painted, in a group of their own first, then swapped in one step, and the guns, the engine glows, their trails and the heat shimmer follow the new parts; the colliders stay as spawned. Paint is the game's own: until a ship is first given custom paint it wears its model's materials (the stock bake); then it gets copies of its paint materials, compiled before they go on, and each paint shader's texture is rendered from the game's recipe in a worker, off the main thread (a big freighter's takes most of a second), and swapped onto the copies when it lands; a glowing shader's glow is split from the painted colour as the converter splits it. Going back to stock puts the model's materials back. `src/vehicles/shipFit.ts` resolves a saved fit against the hull (anything the pack no longer has, or a slot does not take, falls back to stock with a note), `shipMounts.ts` hangs and swaps the parts, `shipPaint.ts` and `paintRender.ts` (a worker of its own, `src/player/recipeWorker.ts`) paint.

## The map (M)

**M** opens the map window, on whichever of its four tabs was used last: **Here**, **Galaxy** (every world, and the places on each to jump to), **Waypoints** (Y) and **Group** (the full stop, and only while a server holds your group). Its first tab is where you are. On a planet it is the client's own map of it (`npm run swg -- maps @SWG assets-private --retail-only` writes `texture/ui_map_<planet>.dds` into every converted planet pack as `map.png`, with `map.json` giving the ground it covers and the point under its middle: the terrain's whole width about the origin on the ten launch worlds, and on the expansion zones the buildout area's composite rectangle the client drew them over, moved by the client's map adjustments (Kachirho's picture is 2048 m about a point 112 m north of the origin, Mustafar's 8000 m about (-2880, 2976), the hunting grounds' 2844 m and the dead forest's 1000 m; a `map.json` written before the frame was read draws about the origin as it always did, and `status` asks for `maps` again); the map window, the galaxy map's thumbnails and the travel terminal all place a point on a picture through that one frame (`mapFrame` in `src/ui/spaceMapLayers.ts`); without a picture the ground's extent is a grid; the same command also writes `galaxy.json` beside the packs, every planet's ground width and the game's own thirty shuttle routes with the price of each, which the galaxy tab draws as the lines between the systems), with the cities as rings the size of their reach, the starports and shuttleports, and every other place once the map is close enough to read them, and you as an arrow the way you face, with your height under it when flying. Drag pans, the wheel zooms about the cursor, a double click centres on you, and a click on a place jumps to it. A right-click sets a waypoint where it fell, named for the place nearest it ("Near Mos Eisley") or by its number: the character keeps up to a hundred in its story book, each in one of the palette's thirteen colours, and both maps draw the ones switched on as a layer of their own, a diamond of our own drawing in the waypoint's colour with its name, the tracked one ringed (the client's own waypoint icon is never used). With a server that holds the story the book is the server's and a waypoint set here is asked of it; with none it is this browser's, kept apart from the character list, and settled with a server the next time the character meets one exactly as the rest of the character is. In the world each one switched on is a mark of our own where it stands -- a diamond, a diamond with a dot for a quest's, a diamond notched at its foot for one set in a room, which from outside its building stands at the building's way in -- with a caret when it is well above or below you, the distance under the tracked one and the nearest in view, and an arrow at the edge of the screen pointing at the tracked one when it is out of sight; walking up to one of your own says so on the message line. A minimap at the top left is the planet's own picture in a circle round you, north up (or turning with you: the Interface page's **North up** switch, on to begin with), with your arrow, every waypoint switched on and a mark for north on its rim; the planet's name stands over the top of the circle and your coordinates over its foot -- the same two numbers the map window reads out, the hull's place while you ride, fly or stand aboard as the map's arrow has it, written only when they change -- and walking into a city fades its name in under it. The corner wears no plate: in space, in the dungeon copies and on a world with no picture, where the minimap stands aside, the planet's name and the `/loc` line stand bare there on a dark halo, `/loc` saying on a planet the same two numbers as the minimap and the map window and in space the game's own frame, which a story's place, a waypoint and `__debug.teleport` take out there (the space map's own line mirrors x, so the two differ in that sign alone), and with the plate over a head switched off the nearest living thing's name is a line of its own at the corner's foot. **Y** opens the map on its Waypoints tab: this world's first by distance and then every other world's, each to switch, track, rename, recolour, take away or show on the map (which turns to the Here tab at that point), and "Mark here" at the top; Y on that tab shuts the map, and M opens it on whichever tab was used last. The Interface page's **Map, waypoints and jobs** group switches all of it, and the jobs followed down the right edge as well. A job's own waypoint stands beside yours, on the minimap and on the map, switched on unless its step says otherwise. `__debug.waypoints` sets, lists and changes them from the console, and `__debug.minimap` the minimap. In space the tab is the zone in three axes: a grid at the zone's plane, every rock as a dot, and from the zone's own pack its stations by the game's own names, its hyperspace points, its launch point, its asteroid fields and its nebulae as faint shells in their own colours, each kind a layer with a box to switch it off and the client's own zone map icon beside it; the ships are cones the way they point, yours in orange with a dashed line down to the plane. Left-drag turns, right- or middle-drag slides, the wheel zooms toward the cursor and a double click centres on a mark. The view follows your ship until you slide it, and **F** or the Follow button brings it back. Resting the cursor on anything names it, and a click picks it: the panel beside the picture gives the game's own description, how far off it is, how big it is, how many docking lanes its model has, a Centre button and, at a ship's controls, **Hyperspace…**, which opens the System Map on this system. Other ships will need a ping to show later; for now every ship in the zone is on it.

The window's other tab is the **galaxy**: a disc of stars with every system we have standing at its canon grid square, turned, slid and zoomed with the mouse exactly as the space map is, with **Reset view** to frame it again. Where the systems hang is ours and not the game's — the archives hold a picture of the galaxy but nothing that places a world on it, and only the shuttle fares say which worlds are joined — so a system placed by us carries a star beside its name and says so when it is picked; the lines between them are the game's own shuttle routes, read out of `galaxy.json`. Each system wears the picture of its world that its own orbit's pack carries, or a tinted globe where the archives have none. A ring marks the system you are in. Click one and the panel beside the map gives each of its worlds: what it is, its own ground map with its cities and ports dotted on it, its cities, ports and landmarks to jump straight to (a dot jumps as its chip does), **Travel** to go there, and, at a ship's controls in space, **Hyperspace to orbit**, which starts the same jump the System Map does (Talus and Rori arrive at their own stations in their neighbour's system; Mustafar has no orbit and says so). **List** puts the old card list back for reading and for the keyboard.

## Adrift: on foot in space

Out of a ship in space (E beside it, carrying its motion) there is no down. The mouse turns the body itself, yawing and pitching it in its own frame through any angle, **Z** and **V** roll it, and the view rides behind however it is turned (Alt looks round without turning it). The keys are thrusters: **W** and **S** ahead and back, **A** and **D** sideways, **Space** and **Ctrl** up and down, each adding to a momentum that is kept until **Q** brakes it off, capped at 60 m/s; a hull or a rock met on the way stops the drift into it. E beside a ship boards it. For now anyone can do this; it will want a jetpack to move and a helmet to live.

## Speeders on the ground

A speeder's springs are stiff and well damped and the ground under it is a hard floor: a corner that has got under the ground is lifted out at once, and the speed it went in at is a hit, so a hull cannot sink into a slope the way it used to. A speeder bike or a landspeeder does 65 km/h flat out and 75 on the boost (a pod racer keeps its 300). Off the ground, past its cushion, the repulsor still carries half the machine's weight and holds the fall to a walking pace within a few dozen metres of the ground, so a ramp gives a glide; it never carries more than the weight, so nothing flies.
