A machine on its back for more than a moment throws its rider (a little hurt), and **E** beside a machine on its back turns it upright rather than climbing on. The pod racers' seats: the game seats a rider at a pod's authored origin with the riding pose's own offset, and on most pods that origin is nowhere near the cockpit, while on the three that carry clips the cockpit itself stands four metres higher in the run than in the idle. So a pod's seat names where the pilot's pelvis goes -- the owner's own place for that pod where there is one, else the game's own `player` point, which every skinned pod's mesh carries on its cockpit joint (a gallery converted before that point was kept has none, and `status` asks for the gallery again), else that joint's own origin -- and hangs under the cockpit's joint, so the pilot rises and sinks with the cockpit through the pod's own idle and run; another player's pod plays the same clips by its speed, so their pilot sits in the cockpit drawn here too. The console says for each pod which rule placed its pilot and where, and `__debug.seat(dx, dy, dz)` nudges the seat live on every pod and prints the line for the table.

## Pods and speeder bikes

A pod racer, and a speeder bike in racing mode, is driven by the race drive (`src/vehicles/raceDrive.ts`), not by the springs. **W** climbs a speed curve toward the pod's top and **S** is the air brake (a crawl reverses); **Shift** with W, once past three-fifths of the plain top, charges the boost for most of a second and then burns the engines' heat for up to six seconds -- run them dry and the boost is refused until they cool, which the message line says. The mouse moves a heading target that never leads the nose by more than a third of a right angle, and the pod turns toward it at its own most (95 degrees a second on a pod), so no flick turns it round; **A** and **D** steer directly; **Space** slides it wide and loose through a turn; **Z** and **V** lean it hard on its side once it is really moving. Its travel lags its nose, so it drifts wide at speed and grips as it slows. It rides a set height over the ground, read half a second ahead so that a rise is climbed in time and climbed as fast as before, but the ground never pulls it down harder than the world's own gravity (the loose hold, after the owner's first ride): the upward speed a slope gives it is kept, and only gravity takes it back, so off a crest or a lip it flies and comes down where a thrown thing would, over a dip or a crevasse wider than the glide it carries on its momentum and comes down where it comes down, on gently rolling ground it rides its height without bobbing, and down a long descent it stays close to the ground once it is on it, though a descent that begins at a crest is jumped first (on the steepest hills, Dantooine's, a full pod spends about two fifths of the way in the air); a kerb or a pit's far rim is a nudge, not a launch, and a lift toward a rise ahead never carries it past where the rise will be. A pit narrower than half a second of travel (the same metres a quarter second covered before the speeds were halved) is glided over. `race({ hold: 'firm' })` puts back the first ride's rule at the halved speeds, which held the hull onto every hollow and dropped it hard after a crest. A wall met at a glance is a scrape that costs some hull and a stumble; head on, or at 45 degrees or more at speed, it is a crash and the pod is gone, the rider thrown no harder than off a speeder; the hull wears no further than a quarter of itself by scrapes and bumps. A steep terrain face is a wall too (`race({ offCourse: 'stop' })` makes it only a scrape). While racing a pod collides as one shared box (7 × 3 × 15 m) and a bike as a small one (1 × 1.3 × 3.6 m, or its own box held to the pod's), so every pod handles alike whichever was picked.

The speeds are the owner's, halved after his first ride: a full pod reaches 600 km/h with the boost (476 without; a stock one about 490, 357 without), a full bike 350 (278 without; a stock one about 287, 208 without); `race({ tune: { ceiling } })` moves the pod's ceiling up to 1,368 km/h, the bike's following at 350/600 of it, and upgrades (seven of them, `race({ upgrades: 'max' })`, kept with the character in that vehicle's fit by its garage id, and never sent to anybody) move a pod from stock toward full. **U** switches a speeder bike between traversal (today's bike, the orbit and its own hull) and racing, at any speed going up and under its traversal top coming down; a pod has no modes. What a vehicle is spawned as decides which drive it gets: every pod and every bike races, a landspeeder and a skiff are bikes, the Koro-2 and Bail Organa's speeder stay hover cars (spawned "as speeder bike" they race too), the snowspeeder is an aircraft, the walkers walk (`src/vehicles/vehicleKinds.ts`, the gallery's 57 and Sebulba's pod by name). While racing the camera is the race view: behind the hull, leaning into its turns, pulled out and widened as the speed nears the ceiling, **Alt** looking round as ever; zoomed all the way in it is the pilot's own eyes, turning, banking and leaning with the pod. The flight display's arcs show the speed against the ceiling, the engines' heat and the boost's charge. `localStorage['swg.race'] = '0'` and a reload start the session with everything exactly as before; `__debug.race({ on: false })` does it live -- every vehicle back on its springs, U doing nothing on a bike, the gallery sorted by the old names again for the next spawn -- except that a vehicle standing keeps the kind it was spawned as (a landspeeder spawned while the pass was on stays a speeder bike, on its springs, until it is spawned again). `__debug.race()` is the console.

A raced pod has two engines of three parts each, a raced bike one (by the kind it is spawned as). A scrape damages a part on the side it struck and a hard strike from below damages any of them; running the boost dry sets one part, any of them alike, on fire, and a part that burns burns out in about eight seconds. Parts badly damaged on one side pull the pod toward that side, a part out costs a share of the top and refuses the boost, and so does a fire. Holding **R** while racing with nothing in hand mends the worst part (puts its fire out first, brings an out part back), at the cost of speed meanwhile; the bar offers it while anything is damaged. Each engine's glows stand on its own engine points and dim as it loses thrust, smoke and fire stand at a side as its worst part gets worse (the ships pack's own three effects), and the game's own damaged-engine sounds play: the vehicle's damaged thruster set for the parts' mean damage, its fire over the engine, a spark as a part crosses a band, and at the last a silent engine with only its fire. The flight display's condition block names each part (pips red while it burns) and its heat arc carries a notch for each part out. A scrape costs the hull a third of what it did before the engines had parts, the rest going into the parts, and the upgrade levels are kept with the character. Sebulba's pod, the one pod the archives hold that no template names, is in the gallery once its vehicles are converted again (`npm run swg -- gallery '@SWG' assets-private --only=vehicles --retail-only`, which `status` asks for), on Gasgano's clips and Gasgano's seat. `__debug.engines()` is the console.

**Pods put together from parts.** The seven skinned pods (Anakin's, Gasgano's, the light-bending one, Mawhonical's, Balta's, the IPG and Sebulba's; the static FG 8T8 has no skeleton to split by) stand on one skeleton, and nearly every triangle of each belongs wholly to its cockpit or to its engines, so any pod's cockpit can be spawned on any other's engines: in the garage panel each skinned pod has an engines choice beside its spawn button (its own, or any other skinned pod's, kept in this browser), and `__debug.spawn('pod_racer_one', undefined, { engines: 'podracer_anakin' })` does the same from the console. It is built when it is spawned, from the two converted pods, with no conversion: the cockpit, the seat, the clips (a Gasgano, light-bending, Mawhonical or Sebulba cockpit plays its idle and run, and the other pod's engines ride them) and the rider's pose are the first pod's, and the engines, their glows, the engine parts, the smoke and fire and the engine sound are the second's; it races on the shared pod box like every pod, and spawned as a speeder bike it races as one. The few triangles of the engine pod that reach into its own cockpit (16 to 32 a pod: the control cables) go with the engines and end about where the other pod's cockpit stands. Other players see it built the same way from its id, which names both pods (`pod_racer_one.podracer_anakin`); a browser built before draws nothing for it. `__debug.pods()` is the console.

## Tracks

A track is a row of gates on a world: rectangles standing in the air that a pod or a racing bike flies through in order, as a circuit of laps or as a sprint from a grid to a last gate. A world's tracks are one plain file, `public/tracks/<world>.json` (the world's pack id; a real-elevation world's is its planet's), which ships with the build exactly as the permanent placements do: nothing reads them from a server, a track reaches players once its file is committed and merged to main, and a file left there uncommitted stops a local release pack. On a planet a gate's place is in the raw frame `/loc` reads; in a space zone in the game's own.

**Running one.** Spawn a pod (or a speeder bike, which a track that is raced as one takes in racing mode), then `__debug.track({ start: 'test_dune_loop' })` stands it at the grid behind a short loading screen, which also compiles what stands along the course, and counts down three seconds. **W** pressed within three tenths of a second of go, and not held from before, earns a start boost that climbs the curve three times as fast until the pod reaches eight tenths of its plain top. The gate to fly through next is drawn bright (with an arrow at the screen's edge when it is out of view), the one after it dim; one flown the wrong way says so. The race block at the top of the screen shows the lap, the run's time, the split at each gate against the best lap there (coloured for ahead or behind), the last lap and the best. A crash puts the pod back after two and a half seconds at the last gate crossed, facing the next, as a fresh spawn of the same vehicle as the kind it was raced as; the clock runs all the while. A run is timed on the physics' own steps, so it stands still with a panel or the map open, and a gate is timed to the instant the hull's middle passed through its window, whatever the frame rate. The best lap and the best total are kept with the character, per world, track and the kind it was raced as, with the upgrade levels and the splits they were set at. `track({ stop: true })` ends a run, and getting off, dying, leaving racing mode, travelling or the vehicle being taken away rather than wrecked (the garage's clear) ends it too; a run over keeps its block, saying why, until you get off. A bike that starts a run is put in racing mode and the message line says so. While a run stands nobody new is stood or put away beside the course, whatever the speed, so nothing is compiled as you pass. With the race pass off (`race({ on: false })` before an arrival, or `localStorage['swg.race'] = '0'`) no track file is read and no corridor kept clear. The block and the gates' outlines are one switch on the Interface page (The race block, under Flying).

**The TEST course.** `TEST: Dune Loop` (`test_dune_loop`) on Tatooine is a three-lap circuit of 24 gates, about six and a quarter kilometres a lap, on open desert over a kilometre from the nearest town, for a pod or a bike: chosen where no stretch of 32 m is steeper than 0.08 and nothing stands in its corridor, which `raceTrack.test.ts` checks against the converted packs. `track({ go: 'test_dune_loop' })` takes you to its grid.

**Laying one** (the dev server only, as the placements' editor is). Ride a pod or a racing bike to where the track begins and `track({ begin: 'my_loop' })` takes a new one in hand, its grid where you are; then ride the course and press **Numpad0** (`trackGate` on the Controls page, a dev server's only) wherever a gate should stand: each is dropped where the hull is, facing the way it is going, at the default size for the kind. `track({ save: true })` writes it into the world's file (the first save to a file each session asks a second time), and the file is read again on every page at once. Pick `track` in the debug menu and its **Gates** view lists every track of the world (go to its grid, start it, check its corridor, edit it) and, with one in hand, its name, kind, laps, who may race it and its corridor's half-width as fields, and every gate with buttons to go to it, move it to where you stand, put one in after it, nudge it a metre (or five degrees) in its own frame, resize it and take it out; its **Sliders** view moves the countdown, the start window and boost, the put-back's wait, the default gate sizes and the nudge. A saved track's corridor (its `clear`, 12 m by default, either side of every leg) keeps the procedural trees and plants off at the next arrival. `track({ check: 'my_loop' })` names anything placed, planted, laid or posted in that corridor.

**Racing together.** A group on one world can race one track together, through a server started from this build (its greeting says `race: 1`; restart it with `npm run relay` if it is older, and every member must be on this build). Whoever leads the group calls the race: `/race test_dune_loop` in the chat (or `/race test_dune_loop 30` for thirty seconds to go), the debug menu's Gates view's **Race the group** beside a track, or `__debug.raceTogether({ start: 'test_dune_loop' })`. Every member hears it on the message line with the track, the laps and how long until go, and joins with `/race`, which stands what they ride (a pod, or a bike put in racing mode) at a slot of their own behind the track's grid, two abreast in the leader's order, and counts down to the one moment the leader set on the shared clock, so every member's go is the same instant whatever their machine. A member who is riding nothing that races the track, or rides the wrong kind, is told so as the race is offered, and can still get on something that races it and join before go; one on another world or with another copy of the track is told why and sits it out. From go each member's own browser times its own run on the physics' own steps, exactly as a run alone is timed, and says each lap and its finish to the group; every member's race block then shows the standings below the times (the finished first by their time from go, then those still racing by laps done and the time at their last lap, then whoever dropped out), and the message line says each lap, each finish and anyone who drops out. `/race leave` drops out, and getting off, a crash that cannot be put back, dying or travelling does too; leaving the group takes you out of the race with your run going on alone, and so does the leader calling a new one. Nothing of it is judged by the server: it only carries the words to the group, and to nobody else. Another player's pod drawn within twenty metres of yours nudges your turn away from it rather than colliding, since theirs is a picture here and passes through yours (`race({ tune: { nudge: 'off' } })`, and the Racing together dials with `race` picked). A best set in a group race is kept as a best of your own, like any other.

## Hovering, and the way into space

A ship not yet in flight hovers as a VTOL: it holds still until the throttle opens, the view's tilt does not lift it, **Space** and **Ctrl** (or **X**) raise and lower it, and **A** and **D** slide it sideways; the mouse still turns it, and zoomed into the cockpit the view turns with the hull (up and down do nothing there; **Alt** looks round). At a bridge's controls, hovering, the mouse's sideways movement turns the hull. **W** builds speed and past a few metres a second it is in flight, where the mouse's cursor flies it (above) and A and D roll. Flown into something solid (a building, an asteroid, a station) the hull bounces off with most of its speed gone and is hurt by what it lost, and for a moment the contacts, not the throttle, have it, so it comes clear rather than being driven deeper in; the ground ahead on the course counts as ground coming up, so the nose eases over a slope, and a hull that ends up inside the ground (flown sideways into a hillside) is a crash, lifted back onto the surface. The way into space and back is the ship menu, **P**, for whoever is in a ship (at its controls, riding a fighter, or aboard as a passenger), since E aboard a multi-crew ship takes and leaves the controls and steps out. Near the top of a planet's sky (above 1100 m over the ground) the prompt says the ship is at altitude, and the menu's **Go to space** takes it into the planet's space zone in flight; in space the pilot's **Land on …** brings it back 700 m over the planet's spawn, flying, and anyone's **Eject to …** goes down on foot, the ship left behind. A ship with rooms crosses with its crew: whoever is aboard stands in the new hull where they stood in the old, at the controls if they were there (the relay does not carry this yet, so other players make their own crossing). **Dock** flies the ship in along a station's own lane and the station puts it right, free (below). The galaxy map lists each orbit too, arrived at in the ship last flown (else an X-wing). Every panel (the menus, the map, the inventory and spawner) drags by its header and stays where it is put, and the screen round it is clear, so a graphics setting is seen as it moves.

**Hyperspace.** In space the ship menu's **Hyperspace…** row (the pilot's) opens the **System Map**, a page of the ship menu closed by the same key: every space system down the left (the nine orbits, Kessel, Ord Mantell and Deep Space; the one flown in marked, and one not yet converted for hyperspace greyed with the command that converts it), the picked system's hyperspace points by the game's own names, its stations and, for a planet's orbit, its launch point (where a ship climbing out of the planet's sky comes out) in the middle with how far each is from the ship, and on the right the one picked, with the game's description, how far it is and **Hyperspace**. The arrows pick (up and down the points, left and right the systems) and Enter jumps. A jump counts down from five while the ship flies on (the menu's row reads **Cancel the jump** meanwhile, the Escape menu holds the count, and the inventory and spawner keys wait until the jump is over); then the ship vanishes for everyone else, the view goes behind the ship, P, M and N do nothing and the prompt says "jumping" while the game's own streaks play and a tunnel of streaking light closes round the ship; E works only inside the tunnel, aboard a ship with rooms (see below). Inside it the ship is carried there, within the system or to another one, whose world loads behind the tunnel while it runs (at least four seconds); aboard a ship with rooms you can let go of the controls with E and walk about meanwhile, ride its lifts and take the controls again, but not step out (whoever walks out of the door is put back at the entry). When the far side is ready the tunnel opens ahead, the others see the ship arrive, it brakes onto the arrival facing the nearest station or landmark, and control comes back. A place in the same system whose arrival is less than a kilometre off is refused with the game's own "You are already at that point." Points marked "made up" are ours (Kessel's and Deep Space's, whose places the game kept on its servers). In Kessel, Ord Mantell and Deep Space the menu says "In Kessel System" and so on, and Land and Eject are greyed: there is no planet below. Arriving in a space zone from the galaxy map comes out at its arrival point facing its landmark (a system with no pack yet says so on its card, with the command), and a character saved in space comes back where it was.

**Docking.** In space the ship menu's **Dock** row (the pilot's) lights up within 1.5 km of a station, or of the capital ship in Deep Space. Press it, close the menu, and the ship flies itself in along one of the station's own docking lanes — the hardpoints the hull carries, converted into the zone's pack — easing down through each point of the lane and coming to rest at the dock, faced the way the hull's designers drew it. The station puts the hull, its shields and its components back to full, free, over four seconds, and the row then says **Launch**, which flies the lane back out and hands the ship over at its last point. Touching the throttle, the roll keys, **Space** or **Ctrl** breaks the approach off wherever it has got to, and the row does the same. While the ship is at a dock it cannot jump or cross ("undock first"), and nothing can ram it. Which lane a hull offers, how fast one is flown, how long the repair takes and how a parked ship is faced are ours: `__debug.dock` shows them all and tunes them live. A dock hardpoint's own turn is where a ship parks, never the way in — on the five hulls that carry lanes it stands between 9 and 100 degrees from the direction its lane is flown — so the way in is read from the lane's points, and a lane's letter is not whose corridor it is: one station files a third dock's corridors under the letters of the two beside it, so the points are chained by geometry and each corridor is put back on the dock it is nearest. A lane that cannot be reached from where the ship stands without crossing the hull is not offered: off the nose of the capital ship, whose four lanes are all at its tail, the row says to come round to the docks.

**Docking onto another ship.** In space a small ship can also be carried on a bigger one. Fly within 50 m of a hull at least twice your ship's length, slow to a crawl, and the same **Dock** row offers **Dock onto** it; press it and your ship eases onto the other's back and rides there. From then on the carrier's pilot flies both — your own controls do nothing, and your row says **Undock**, which lets the ship go with the carrier's own speed and a push clear of it. While the two are clamped together *neither* ship can jump, go up to orbit or down to a planet, or land ("undock first"): nothing carries two ships across a zone change yet, so the carrier waits too. If both ships have rooms and you are aboard yours, **E** at the room's own way in crosses you into the other ship's rooms, and **E** there crosses back; **E** anywhere else in the room still steps you out. That works between two hulls of this world and between yours and another player's alike: the first crossing into a hull somebody else flies builds its rooms here, which takes a second or two and is done once, and while it happens the ship menu's own **Cross to** row says so. Standing anywhere but the way in, that row tells you where to stand. A crew already aboard either ship rides along with no interruption. Docking onto another player's ship asks their pilot first: their **Dock** row becomes **Let *you* dock** for twenty seconds of flying, and `__debug.dock({ allow: false })` turns you away; a pilot who already has somebody on their hull turns you away at once, and an answer that comes back after you have flown off or opened the throttle is dropped rather than snatching your ship onto their back. Both browsers must be on a relay started from this build. None of this is in the game's own files — the client had no ship-to-ship docking at all — so every rule and every number is ours: how big a carrier must be, where on its back a ship rests, how fast you may be going to ask, and how hard an undock pushes off. `__debug.dock` shows and moves all of them.

`npm run swg -- space @SWG all assets-private --retail-only` converts the space zones (`space_tatooine`, `space_naboo`, `space_corellia`, `space_dantooine`, `space_lok`, `space_endor`, `space_dathomir`, `space_yavin4`, `space_kashyyyk`, and three systems that are no planet's orbit: `space_light1` Kessel, `space_heavy1` Deep Space and `space_ord_mantell` Ord Mantell; one at a time by name), each into `assets-private/<zone>/`: the station its station table places (drawn as the faction station the client has for that system), every asteroid of its asteroid fields (each field scattered from its own seed through its style table, along a spline where the table gives one, capped at 400 a field), the planets and moons its terrain file hangs in the sky (`space.json`, with each one's surface texture; the game draws them as lit spheres far out, following the camera), and its sky from the same terrain file: the six-sided nebula skybox it names, its two parallel lights and ambient (the main light's direction is fixed; there is no day in space), its clear colour, its star field (tens of thousands, all around), its star sprites (the suns and their glows, hung where the file turns them) and its dust (motes fixed in space within a few dozen metres of the camera, so speed shows against nothing). In the zone there is no ground at all, no gravity and no fog; placed objects load out to three times the setting's reach; ships fly at twice their planetary speeds with no ceiling and never land. `__debug.sky()` lists the sky's parts and lighting, and `__debug.sky('skybox')` (or `'dome'`, `'stars'`, `'dust'`, `'sprites'`) toggles one to see what it contributes. The zone's stars hang fixed in its sky (they are quads turned once towards the camera, not screen-upright sprites, so a roll no longer rolls their glows), and the ground sky its environment file carries — a sun, two moons and two planet pictures, the same 348-byte file copied into all 23 of them, and none at all for the orbit added last — is never built: `__debug.sky()` counts it as `envBodiesLeftOut`. Which star is the zone's sun is ours to choose, not the game's, since the file's own light points as much as 119 degrees away from any star: the brightest by the lens flare's ranking, with a star within six degrees of it folded in as a companion that flares beside it and takes no light of its own. The main light, the shadows, the lit side of every planet, the flare and the god rays (new in space, and stopped by planets and by stations kilometres off) all come from that one star. `__debug.suns()` shows the choice and `__debug.suns({ rule: 'disc' })` compares it with the largest disc. Each zone's `space.json` also carries its hyperspace points, its stations' names, its arrival and the jump scene, which the Hyperspace paragraph above flies. Made up here, because the game kept them on its servers: Kessel's and Deep Space's hyperspace points, Deep Space's asteroids and wreckage, and a Star Destroyer hanging broadside a couple of kilometres from Deep Space's first point (the exterior only: scenery with collision, never boarded); Ord Mantell's one point is the Nova Orion point the game gave the same sector. NPC starships patrol near the stations, the hyperspace points and the arrival lane and fight by side (Imperial, Rebel, Black Sun, pirates), and the NPC tab (B) stands them by hand; every number of their fight is invented, kept in `src/space/shipStats.ts`, `loadout.ts`, `npcBrain.ts` and `roster.ts`. The pack also carries the zone's nebulae with their colours and their lightning, its asteroid fields' shapes, its stations' docking lanes, drydocks and hangar mouths, and the sound a dock plays, which the space map draws. The nebulae are drawn as well: each row becomes a cloud of glowing or misty sheets in its own colours, the far ones pulled in toward the camera so they show past the far plane and fade before it, half of them turning to face you and the rest fixed in the sky. Fly into one and a haze comes up around you, the lens flare and the god rays dim with how deep in you are, and in the rough ones the view shakes by the row's own jitter. Lightning follows the game's own rule, read off the clock the weather reads (the server's while one answers) so everyone on the relay sees the same strikes at the same moments: a nebula strikes by the game's own chance for its rate, each bolt standing between two points in the middle of the cloud for half to all of the row's longest, drawn with the game's own lightning appearance and its two end effects. A bolt strikes every ship it passes, yours or an NPC's, whether or not it is near enough to be drawn, and drains only the shield on the side it passed, never the armour, the hull or a part, at a quarter of the tables' numbers; each ship rests two seconds between strikes, and the Space section of the settings turns the damage off. The game's bolts are short and its nebulae large, so a ship is almost never struck: `__debug.nebulae({ bendWithin: 900 })` puts back this game's old aim, which moved a bolt ending near your ship onto it. And a nebula weakens a ship inside it, as the game's rule did: its engines, its reactor and its shield recharge are reduced by the nebula's density and come back exactly as it leaves, the message line saying so as you fly in; the speed arc's top tick moves down while you are in one and back up as you leave (the arc is scaled to the hull whole, so a part knocked down moves it too), and a ship faster than its new top slows to it at the brake whether W is held or not. How many sheets there are, how big they are, the pull-in, the haze and how strongly a nebula weakens a ship (no row of the game's tables sets that) are ours, not the game's, and `__debug.nebulae()` moves every one of those numbers live. Not done: the stations' interiors, landing at a station.

**The sandbox.** `npm run swg -- sandbox @SWG assets-private --retail-only` writes one more system, **Sandbox**, which is not a place in the game at all: a sun, four to six planets 40 to 200 km out that you can fly toward, asteroid fields between them and three jump points, 250 km across, all drawn from a seed (`--seed=N` for a different one). It borrows a converted zone's sky and the client's own planet surfaces and asteroids to be drawn with, and nothing else; every place and size in it is ours and the pack says so. It is reached like any other system, from the Galaxy tab or the System Map. There, and only there, the ship menu's **Ultra cruise** row (and **O**) runs the ship straight ahead at 10 km/s: no turning, no damage, nothing can hit you, the others on the relay stop seeing you until you are back, and it stops itself two kilometres short of a planet or at the edge of the system, waiting a moment at the stop for what is around you to load before it hands the controls back. Let go with the same key or the same row.

## Ship guns, targets and wings

A ship with weapon hardpoints has guns: **click** fires them in turn (a gun part's own muzzle hardpoint when it names one, else the mount it hangs on). The bolts are the game's own. The `ships` command reads the projectile table (`datatables/projectile/projectile.iff`: each projectile's particle effect, the effect played when it fires and the ones played where it strikes, per surface, through client effect files) and the weapon table (`datatables/space/ship_weapon_components.iff`: each gun's projectile, speed and range), converts every bolt effect and hit effect into the pack's `particles/`, writes the tables as `ships/projectiles.json`, and names in each ship's manifest entry the gun it fires (the blaster named for its family, `wpn_awing_blaster`, `wpn_tiefighter_basic`, `wpn_z95_blaster`; else the light blaster, green for the Empire's ships). Nearly every blaster fires at 600 m/s to 512 m. A bolt carries the ship's own velocity on top of its muzzle speed, so it never lags the ship that fired it, and lives its range over its speed. Its look is the projectile's effect (a streak 19 m long in red, green or blue), a particle effect carried along with the bolt: the particle player now honours the client's local-space emitters (particles that live in the emitter's frame and are put into the world only when drawn) and can place passing effects (a bolt, a hit) that play from their start when placed, are never put to sleep for distance, and are dropped once they have played out; where the bolt strikes, the table's hit effect (armour panels and sparks on metal) is stood on the surface. A pack converted before this has no `projectiles.json`, and the bolts are drawn as a blaster's. A ship converted with its fit fires from each gun what that gun's chassis slot holds (the component's own row of the weapon table: its bolt, speed and range), so a gun fitted with a green-bolt blaster fires green bolts beside red ones; a gun on the hull itself fires the first weapon slot that fires bolts, and a missile launcher or countermeasures slot fires no bolt. The lead is worked out with the speed of the gun that fires next.

Bolts hurt ships: a vehicle's colliders are known to the bolts, a hit takes from its hull (the ship's condition under the middle of the screen shows the hull and every layer over it as they go, and a pilot whose ship is hit feels a jolt), and a ship with no hull left blows up as a vehicle does. In flight the guns lead a target. **Tab** cycles the ships ahead (within 70° of the nose and 2.5 km) by how far off the nose they sit, and with none chosen the nearest to the nose is taken; the flight display puts four corners on the target (an arrow on a ring inside the screen's edge when it is off it) with its name, range, side and the three layers of the face turned toward you under them, and draws the lead marker where the guns must point for a bolt fired now to meet it: the meeting is worked out in the ship's own frame (the target's place and velocity relative to the ship, the bolt's speed over the ship's), the earliest time a bolt can be where the target will be. In flight the guns swing toward the cursor inside the aim circle, every gun's bolt crossing under it at the target's range, and take the lead exactly when the cursor sits on the reticle (the circle and the reticle turn green), never more than 12° off the nose; hovering, or with Alt held, they fire at the lead while it sits within 12° of the nose, else along it.

The game's fighters carry wings that open in flight. Each wing stands where its client data places it (the PSOR place, mirrored as the model is, or a hardpoint of the part that lists it: the B-wing's foils hang on its body) and turns about its own Z by its angle over its own time, so the X-wing's foils take three seconds, not a quarter of that. Everything hung on a wing hangs under it: the engines, boosters and guns ride the foils, the glows and exhaust ribbons leave from the engines wherever they stand, and the bolts leave from the muzzles as they stand at the shot; the hull's colliders under a wing move with it. Wings open whenever a ship flies with room under them (on a planet a wing that swings below the belly waits for that much air and half a metre, with a metre's band: the B-wing's 15 m) and close on landing, and a chassis's `wing_open_speed_factor` (0.95 on the X-wing, advanced X-wing, B-wing and V-wing) is taken as the top speed's share while they are open; `__debug.wings('threshold')` shows the other reading (open only under that share of top speed). The pilot opens and closes them with **U** at any speed; the choice holds until **U** again or leaving the seat, and a wing that swings below the belly still folds on landing. NPC fighters fly with their wings shut and open them the moment they take a target to fight, shutting them again three seconds after the last one is lost (the game shut them at once; the three seconds are ours and keep a fighter between two targets from flapping); a dock keeps your own ship's wings shut from the first leg of its approach until the launch has flown it clear, and while it is clamped onto another hull, then gives them back to your choice or the flight rule, as the game did for a docking ship. Firing with them shut is allowed. `__debug.wings({ auto: { npc: 'flight' } })` has the NPCs fly open as they did before. A ship is centred side to side on the meshes that do not ride a wing, so it turns about its hull: the B-wing about its cockpit pod, not a point out along its flat wing. A large ship takes a few seconds to appear, since everything it draws is compiled before it is shown (the console says how long when it is over a second). Other players' ships open and close their wings as their pilots' do. `npm run swg -- dump @SWG clientdata/ship/client_shared_xwing.cdf --hex` shows the layout.

On a planet a ship can be set down. Stopped with its engines running it holds the height it is at, and **Ctrl** and **Space** fly it down and up from there a metre and a half a second; hold **Ctrl** once it is at the bottom of its hover band, or press **J** to cut the engines, and it comes down, tilts to the ground under it and rests on the point the game marks beneath half the hulls (never more than 0.3 m off the ground, and on its underside for the hulls that carry no such point), held still with its springs off and its wings folded. A slope steeper than 20 degrees is refused, with the reason in the prompt; arriving faster than 8 m/s is a crash, which hurts the hull and the pilot as flying into the ground does, and a ship whose engines are out is measured by its whole speed, so cutting them in a fast cruise is a crash rather than a landing. **W** or **Space** lifts it off again. Standing inside a hangar room (the Theed and Massassi hangars, the Imperial outposts' bays), a ship flown from a cockpit is stood on that room's floor rather than on the ground under the building, turned along the room, and while it is in a room it takes the floor and the collision the room's own bodies take, so it can hover about inside without snagging on the shell. Ships with rooms of their own still spawn outside. How high a ship rests, the slope it will take, how long it settles and what counts as a hard landing are ours, and `__debug.landing` tunes all of them live; `__debug.landing({ rule: 'springs' })` puts the older hover-only ride back. Other players see a landed ship standing still.

Out in space there is no ground and no up, so a ship sets down on whatever it has come to a stop over: bring it to a halt within forty metres of an asteroid, a station's hull or a wreck, on any side of it, and **J** puts it down there, turned so its own up is that face's. **W** lifts it off again along the same face, carrying whatever it was standing on was doing. Step out of a ship that is set down and the gravity boots take hold of the surface under it: you walk on it with "up" the face beneath your feet, and the view turns with you as you go round the curve. Adrift with nothing to climb into, **E** puts the boots on wherever you are looking, out to forty metres, and jumping takes them off and leaves you floating again. The boots hold to anything solid, and to something that is moving as readily as to something that is not, because the ground you walk on is read from whatever you are standing on every step. How far round you the surface is copied, how quickly the view turns onto it and how high a jump has to be to let go are all ours; `__debug.boots` tunes them live.

## Playing together (a start)

`npm run relay` starts a relay (`server/relay.mjs`, port 8787, no dependencies: a WebSocket server on Node's own http module) that passes every player's place and pose to every other. In the game, the Escape menu's **Multiplayer** page takes the relay's address (`ws://host:8787`, or `?server=ws://host:8787` in the page's address) and connects; each other player is shown as their own character, of their species, standing where they stand and playing what their rig plays (walking, running, crouching, seated on a vehicle, an emote or a dance), with their name over their head, gliding between the relay's ten updates a second, and only while on the same world. The vehicle or ship a player is on goes with them: the others draw it (the model, with no physics) where it is and how it is turned, with the player seated on it in the pose it seats them, flying it from its rooms, or aboard as a passenger, and a figure aboard a banked hull or adrift in space is turned as it is there. Through a server started from this build (its greeting says `veh: 2`) each state also carries the vehicle's own velocity, its turn and the moment its place was true on the shared clock, so the others draw it where it is now rather than where it was: glided toward each message, a pod at full speed was drawn some twenty to forty metres behind itself, and now it is drawn where it is while it holds its line (to the centimetre in the node simulation; between two real machines it is off by its speed times however far apart their copies of the shared clock still are, which is milliseconds, and a turn or a change of speed is eased in over a tenth of a second), the rider riding it there; against an older relay, with no clock shared or with `__debug.reckon({ tune: { on: false } })` it glides as before. A winged ship's wings open and close for the others as its pilot's do (a relay started before this drops that, and the wings then follow the ship's speed). A ship in a hyperspace jump is hidden from the others from the jump's start until its tunnel opens, and then shown where it comes out without gliding across (a relay started before this drops that, and the others then see the ship vanish only when it changes zone). A ship's components, droid and paint go with its pilot's hello when the Edit page closes or they change ship (one hello, never while a colour is being dragged), and the others hang the same parts and paint it the same, in place, without rebuilding it; a relay started before this drops the fit, and the others then see the stock ship. A player's look goes with their hello (the shape sliders, height, colours and outfit), so the others draw them as they are. The relay has grown into a small server that holds the few things nobody can be trusted to hold alone. It keeps **one clock**, so everyone sees the same time of day rather than each browser running its own twelve-minute day from wherever it happened to start. It knows **who you are**: the first time it runs, your browser makes itself a key and keeps it, and from then on you come back as the same player with the same characters, with no account and no password — and nobody else can appear as one of your characters, because answering the server's challenge needs the key. Opening the same character in a second browser gives it to the newer one and tells the older it was taken over. A character played with no server and then brought to one is settled by a change counter, the higher one winning, and a real tie is put to you. What it keeps is written to a file you can read (`server/data/world.json`, plus a log of the changes since it was last written), so stopping the server and starting it again picks up where it left off. It holds **groups**: invite whoever you are looking at (the game's own 90 m, from its radial menu table), accept or decline, leave, hand the lead on, put someone out, and a roster with each member's world and how far off they are, plus a chevron over one who is too far to make out; the full stop (`.`) opens the map on its Group tab, which is there only while the server holds your group (the roster is a tab of the map, so play stops under it as under the rest of the map, and its Trade button shuts the map first so the trade's window can come up); the full stop is a binding like any other on the Controls page. Everyone in it also stands in a strip down the side of the screen and is marked on the planet's map and on the space map, the leader's mark larger and ringed. And it carries a **chat line**: Enter opens it, plain words are said to everyone on your world and `/g <words>` to your group, and what anybody says floats over their head for a few seconds and stands in the message line; `/invite`, `/accept`, `/decline`, `/leave`, `/promote`, `/kick` and `/who` are typed there too. It carries **the fight**: a shot fired on one screen is flown again on every other as a picture of a bolt that hurts nothing, and is cut short where the one who fired it says it landed, so the mark and the burst are in the same place on both screens. Whether it hit is the shooter's screen to decide, and whoever was hit is the only place health is ever taken off — their own health then goes back out, which is what fills the health bars on the group's roster, and a death is announced by the one who died, with whoever struck the blow, and plays as a clip on the other screens. A lit blade turning one of these bolts away is a word rather than a second bolt: that shot stops on every screen, the shooter's own included, and the one who blocked it fires their own from the blade. Damage between players is off unless the server was started with `--friendly-fire`; with it off, two of you can still agree to a duel, which is the game's own word at the game's own 128 m (`COMBAT_DUEL`, and `COMBAT_PEACE` to call it off). Everyone on your world is something a bolt, a blade and a sweep stop against, and nothing else: walking into somebody does not shove or lift you, a ship set down beside one does not rest on them and a speeder does not ride over them. Another player's lightsaber is drawn as well, in the colour they chose, lighting the ground and the walls round them as your own does, with both ends of a staff and one in each hand, and it spins through the air when they throw it. It also lets you **stand in a friend's ship**: walk up to a ship another player has parked and press **E** within a few metres of its side, and that hull's rooms are built here — once, a second or two the first time, and nothing at all is built for the ships you merely watch go past — and you are put inside them, with their own lights on and their floor under you while your friend flies. **E** anywhere in the rooms steps you back outside beside the hull, and the second time you board it is instant. Their ship stays theirs while you are in it: there are no controls for you to take and the ship menu opens nothing there. When their hull goes — they fly off, or close their browser — you are put out into the world rather than left standing in a ship that is no longer there. And it carries **the group's travelling**: when whoever leads a group crosses to another world — up into space, down to a planet, across the galaxy map or through a hyperspace jump — everybody else in the group is offered the same trip, with a countdown on it, and nobody is moved unless they say yes. Whoever says yes makes the crossing their own way: a jump if they are flying in space and the leader jumped, which keeps their hull, its rooms and whoever is walking them, and otherwise the ordinary travel with the ship they are flying carried across. They come out beside the leader rather than at their own world's spawn, each a fixed step to one side so that two ships never arrive inside each other, and a member who is on another planet when the offer is made crosses from wherever they are. A ship at a dock or on a station's lane refuses the trip as it refuses every other crossing, and somebody standing in a hull they are not flying is not moved at all: nothing carries a passenger out of another player's rooms, so they are asked to step out first and the offer stands until they have. A relay started before this drops the word that carries where the leader came out, and the group then arrives at the world's own arrival point instead. It keeps **each character's story book** as well (for now, the waypoints): the server's copy is the one that stands while you play there, a copy changed while you played alone is handed up and taken when the character settles your browser's way, the book it replaces is archived whole rather than merged, and a copy the server does not take is set aside in your browser rather than thrown away, which the message line says. Against a relay started before this a browser keeps its book itself, as it does with no server at all. It keeps **what you put down**: a house, and now a prop, is the server's while it keeps them, so everybody on that world sees everybody's and only the one who put it down (or the server's admin) can pick it up, move it or take it away, and O picks up only your own; with no server both your props and your houses are kept on this machine, per world and shared by every character played on it, and stand again the next time you come back with no server. Which of the two keeps them changes only when a server takes your claim (props: when its hail says it keeps them), when you put the line down, when a line that dropped comes back to a far end that keeps none of them, or when an arrival with an address saved hears nothing for six seconds: a line that merely drops keeps the server's things standing, and putting down or picking up one of yours meanwhile is refused in words until it is back. The server answers every word about a prop it will not act on, in its own words, and a move or a removal it refuses stands the prop again where it stood. The things this machine keeps wait while a server keeps yours, and are never handed up to it on their own. A relay started before this keeps no props, and against it yours stay on this machine. A house this machine keeps clears the plants off its ground from the moment you arrive only when there is no server to wait for; arriving with a server address saved, the ground waits with the question, and a house stood once a wait runs out clears the plants only off ground built after it, as a house put down in a wood does. It still does not check physics: what the server holds is what is decided, not what is observed. Behind a reverse proxy with TLS the address is `wss://`.

It also sends each player's news only to the world they are standing on, so a browser is never asked to fetch a rig, dress a stranger and build their ship for somebody it cannot see on another planet. Someone you have already met and who then walks off your world is different: they stay built and simply stop being drawn, and when they come back they are drawn again with nothing rebuilt. A player is taken apart on your machine only when they actually leave.

A browser built before any of this plays exactly as it always has, and a browser built for the server that finds an older relay at the other end says so and turns everything new off.

What each character owns is kept a row per thing: two of one shirt are two things, each under the name its browser gave it when it was got, which can be held, shown, traded and coloured apart, and a thing's colour goes with it when it changes hands. The server says so in its greeting (`items: 2`); against a server that does not, which folds two of one item into one, the game hands up one of each and says on the message line to restart it (`npm run relay`). A world a server from before this wrote is read as it stands, every row kept and named on read, and nothing in it is rewritten. A ship's or a painted speeder's colour carried whole (any colour a garment takes) goes to the others only through a server whose greeting says `paint: 2`; it travels beside the nearest colour of the hull's own palette, which is what the others see through a server that does not pass it on or in a browser built before, and the message line says once to restart the server (`npm run relay`).

To play across a home network from the machine with the assets: run the relay (`npm run relay`, port 8787) and the dev server listening on the network (`npx vite --host`) on that machine, find its address (`ipconfig` on Windows, the IPv4 of the wireless or ethernet adapter, say `192.168.1.20`), let Node through the firewall when Windows asks (both ports), and on the other machine open `http://192.168.1.20:5173/?server=ws://192.168.1.20:8787` in Chrome. That machine downloads the converted assets from the first as it plays (a few hundred megabytes the first time; the browser caches them), and the two see each other once both are on the same world. The first machine's own game connects from the Escape menu's Multiplayer page with `ws://localhost:8787`.

`npm run relay` takes a few switches, all optional: `--port=8787`, `--word=<something>` (a join word, which a browser must carry in its address — one line of work, and it stops a stranger who guesses your port from walking into your world), `--data=<folder>` (where the world is kept; `server/data`, beside the server itself, by default), `--friendly-fire` (damage between players, off unless you ask for it; with it off two players can still agree to a duel, the game's own word at the game's own 128 m), `--day=<seconds>` (how long a day is; 720 by default, the game's own), `--story=<folder>` (your own story set, which the server runs; `story-private/` beside the checkout by default, when it is there, and never from `.env`), `--story-tests` (run the committed test set's jobs as well, which are labelled TEST and are off unless asked for), `--assets=<folder>` (the converted content, read for each planet's layout centre so an arrival at a job's place is checked against where the server holds the player; `assets-private/` beside the checkout by default, and with none a planet's arrivals are taken on the browser's word, as every position already is), and `--set <name>=<number>` for any of the server's own numbers. `npm run relay -- --help` lists them. It says what it is doing as it does it: who connected, who said who they were, who went where, who was turned away and why, who is not reading fast enough, and what it wrote to disk. `http://localhost:8787/` in a browser prints the same as a page: how many are connected, which worlds have whom, the clock, the world on disk, what each browser is behind by, and every number in force.

Stop it with Ctrl-C and it writes the world out before it goes. If it is killed instead, nothing is lost: every change is in `server/data/world.log` as it happens, and the next start reads the snapshot and plays the log over it. If the snapshot cannot be put in place — `world.json` open in an editor is the usual reason — it says so and writes at the next turn instead; nothing is lost while it waits.

The address may carry a join word as well as a server: `?server=ws://192.168.1.20:8787&word=mos-eisley`.
The word is kept in this browser afterwards, and the Multiplayer page under Escape has a field for it;
it is never sent — only a hash of it with the server's own challenge crosses.

That page also says who this browser is. It makes itself a key the first time you open the page or
connect, and a server knows you by it for ever after: your characters are yours and nobody else can
appear as one of them. **Copy my key** writes it out; **Use a key** in another browser makes that
browser the same player, which is also how a browser whose data you cleared gets its player back. There
are no accounts and no passwords, and anyone you give the key to can be you.

Open the same character in two browsers and the newer one has it: the older says so on the message line
and stops, and does not take it back. Press Connect on that older one if you want it after all.

## Story

The game will carry a story of your own writing: quests, conversations, documents and the rest, kept in **`story-private/`** beside the checkout. Git ignores that folder, the release pack refuses any path through it, and a release build never copies it; the dev server serves it at `/story-private/` (with `/story-private/index.json`, the list of its files, made fresh on every ask) and tells the page whenever anything in it changes, so an edit reloads without a restart. A local `npm run build` copies it into `dist/` with its index, exactly as it does `assets-private`. The rules that run a story are written and tested (`src/story/`, the same files in the browser and on the server), and with no server they run in the game: this browser holds the jobs (`src/story/localHost.ts`) and pays what they pay out of the character's own purse and backpack. On a server whose greeting says it runs jobs, the server does (`server/stories.mjs`, over the sets `--story` and `--story-tests` name): it reads the character's book in when the character settles -- every revision carried and every deadline that fell while the character was away settled in the order it fell -- sweeps the deadlines of everyone in play on its own clock, and is the only payer, credits through the purse and things through the ledger's own word, never through the browser's. The browser (`src/story/remoteHost.ts`) sends up what its detectors see with where the player stood, and is sent back the view the tracker, the marks and the maps read, and the message line's facts. The server checks what it can before it believes anything: an arrival is believed only where a step waits for it, on the world the player stands on, and -- when it was told the converted content and so knows each planet's layout centre -- within the step's radius and 25 m of where it holds the player; a use within the thing's reach of it, and only of a thing a step waits on or that gives a job the character may take; a room only when a step waits on it, near the place a goto names (or on the browser's word for a step that waits on the room alone, which names no place for the server to measure); an area entered only from within it; a world only the one stood on; a kill of one of the world's shared creatures only on the server's own word of that death naming the player among those who struck it (or as its keeper), a report that came first waiting two seconds for the word; a console signal only from the admin. Where the player stands is the place the browser's last state carried, aboard one's own hull as much as on foot; standing in another player's hull, in a dungeon's copies, or on a planet whose centre it was not told, the server cannot say and takes the browser's word. The game hour a condition reads is the server's own, worked out from the clock it hands out and the planet's own sun exactly as every browser's sky is, so it is known at a claim, during an offline settle and on every sweep; only on a world the game's list of planets does not know is the browser's hour taken, and only while it is fresh. A server that reads no story set at all says so in every view and the tracker says the jobs wait (its heralds still speak and mark their places, since the game's own conversations need no set); the first set its admin's reload reads brings everyone in play into the world as a claim would. A book played alone that the server takes has the rewards the browser paid alone paid again here, once, up to 50,000 credits a settle, the rest owed at the next, since neither the browser's purse nor its backpack crosses to a server. A counter moving (a kill counted) is written to the server's log lazily and flushed every few seconds, so a fight is not a disk flush a kill. While the line is held (the book being settled, or the server not answering) nothing is worked out and the tracker says so; against a server that holds the story and runs no jobs (one from the previous build) the jobs wait, in words. Nor does this browser work them out against a server that keeps the character's credits but holds no story (one from before the stories): only its admin may put credits in there, so a reward worked out here would be recorded as paid and never arrive, and the jobs wait instead, with the tracker saying why; the waypoints are this browser's there as they are with no server. The launcher's **Your story** field names the folder its server runs and its game reads (served at `/story-private/`).

```
story-private/            yours, git-ignored                     ids prefixed own:
  story.jsonc             { "prefix": "own", "title": "..." }
  quests/*.jsonc          one quest a file
  areas/*.jsonc           one area a file
  objects/*.jsonc         one story object a file
  talk/*.jsonc            one conversation a file
  cast/*.jsonc            one of the story's named people a file
  docs/*.doc.txt          one document a file, in its own format (below)
  file.jsonc              the ISB's file on the player: its levels, and how long an entry waits
  calendar.jsonc          what a document's {date} reads
  ladders.jsonc           each track's rungs, and the five words Trust is shown in
src/story/testSet/        committed, every text "TEST: ..."     ids prefixed test:
  fixtures/broken-*.jsonc never loaded: each is built to fail the checker
```

Every file is JSONC: JSON with `//` and `/* */` comments and a comma allowed after the last entry. The prefix `test` is the committed test set's alone: a folder of yours that declares it is refused, since only the test set may use relative places and console signals. Ids are written plain and the loader puts the set's prefix in front (`goto` is `own:goto`, an object `obj/desk` is `own:obj/desk`, an area `area/yard`); a reference into another set carries its prefix. A text is the words themselves, `"@table:key"` for one of the client's own strings, or `{ "en": "..." }` for words that would otherwise begin with `@` or `:`. A place on a planet is `{ "world": "tatooine", "raw": [x, z] }` in the raw frame the client's own waypoints and `pois.json` use (height is never written: it is the ground's, or the room's floor), in a space zone `{ "world": "space_tatooine", "game": [x, z] }` (on foot, the corner's `/loc` reads out these very two numbers in either frame, and `__debug.storyHere()` writes the whole place), and either may add `"room": { "cell": "cantina", "template": "object/building/..." }`; only the test set may write a place relative to where the quest was taken (`{ "rel": "start", "dx": 30, "dz": 0 }`). A span of time is `{ "seconds": 30 }`, `realHours`, `gameHours` or `gameDays` (a game day is the game's own 720 seconds), on the `world` clock (runs while you are logged off; the default) or the `played` one.

**A quest** has an `id`, a `title`, `start` (the steps it begins with, side by side) and `steps` by name, and may say `rev`, `client`, `verb`, `track`, `harsh` (no restart, and abandoning is a failure), `abandon` (`true`, `false`, or the outcome abandoning ends it as), `restart` (`never`, `start` or `checkpoint`), `repeat` (`{ "every": "never" | "always" | "cooldown" | "gameDay" | "realDay", "cooldown": span, "limit": n, "standingPerRealDay": 500 }`), `needs` (a condition), `givers` (`debug`, `use` an object, or `chain` after another quest's outcome), `card` (a document of kind `workorder` or `notice`: offered, the job opens as that page with Accept and Decline at its foot), `stakes`, and `outcomes` by name (`done` and `failed` always exist; each may carry a `reward`, actions in `do`, `failure: true` and `pressure: true`). A step has a `type` and may say `objective`, `visible` (`true`, `false`, or `"after"`: shown once done), `after` and `unless` (steps it waits on, and steps that skip it), `chance`, `timeLimit`, `next` (step names, or `{ "to": step, "when": condition }`; every edge whose condition holds is followed), `nextOne` (only the first), `onFail`, `signalsOut`, `grant` (`{ "done": [...], "fail": [...] }`), `ends` (the outcome it ends the quest with, or `cleared`), `do` (`{ "start", "done", "fail" }` lists of actions), `waypoint` (`{ "on": false }` to start its waypoint switched off), `checkpoint`, `onStuck` (`fail`, `skip` or a step) and `failOn: ["death"]`; each maps to one of the client's quest-task columns, so an importer has somewhere to write. The step types: `nothing`, `join` and `end` (done at once), `reward` (`{ credits, xp, items: [{ kind, id, n }], standing: [{ track, add }] }`, paid once for each time the quest is done: a restart, a drop and a fresh grant, or a retry after a failure pay nothing it already paid), `timer` and `wait` (`for`: a span), `goto` (`at`: a place, `radius`), `signal` (`signal`, counted to `n`), `kill` (`who` catalogue ids, `group`, `social`, `tag`, counted to `n`), `use` (`object`), `observe` (`area`, `seconds`, `continuous`), `talk` (`who`, a cast member, and `node`: done when their conversation reaches that node, or any node with none named) and `choice` (`options`, each `{ "id", "label", "when", "stakes", "pressure", "do", "next", "ends" }`: done when an answer in a conversation chooses one with `choose(q, s, option)`, and going on by that option's own `next` or `ends`, or the step's own when it names neither; with a `doc` the options are chosen at that document's foot instead); `message` and `document` (`doc`: handed over as the step begins, done once it is read to its last page) and `comm` (`doc` and `who`, a cast member: a call played line by line in the conversation band, done once heard to its end); and `collect`, `give`, `carry`, `killLoot`, `pay`, `encounter`, `spawn`, `waves`, `escort`, `perform`, `craft` and `space` are kept for a later pass. Areas are `{ "id", "world", "shape": { "kind": "circle", "c": [x, z], "r": 12 } }` (or a `rect` with `min` and `max`, or a `poly` with `pts`); story objects are `{ "id", "world", "template", "near": [x, z], "reach", "label" }`, a thing the world already stands, picked by its template nearest that point (looked for less than 30 m from `near`); a thing placed for good (**Permanent placements**, below) is named by its key, `"template": "place/<id>"`, with `near` its own place, which `__debug.storyHere()` and the placement editor's list write out for you.

**Conditions and actions** are one vocabulary for everything, written as a short expression (`"questDone(goto) && !flag(seen) || gameHour() >= 18"`, `"grant(next)"`) or as the JSON each reads into, which is what an importer writes; nothing is ever evaluated as code. The conditions built so far are `questNone/Offered/Active/Failed(q)`, `questDone(q[, outcome])`, `closed(q)`, `stepActive(q, s)`, `stepDone(q, s)`, `completions(q) >= n`, `flag(f)` and `flag(f) == v` (and `!= < <= > >=`), `world(id)`, `inArea(a)`, `inRoom(cell[, template])`, `gameHour() >= h`, `inGroup()`, `species(s)`, `roll(p)`, `call(name, ...)`, `credits() >= n`, `has(kind, id[, n])`, `standing(track) >= n` and `trust(track) >= n`, and the conversations' `choiceOf(q, s) == "option"`, `heard(talk/<id>#<node>)`, `chosen(talk/<id>#<node>.<reply>)` (inside a conversation's own file the node alone, `heard(hello)`), `met(cast/<id>)` and `named(cast/<id>)`, and the documents' `witnessed(doc/<id>[, variant])` (it is in the journal, as that version), `fileLevel(isb) >= n` and `fileHas(isb, tag)`; the actions are `offer`, `grant`, `signal`, `complete(q, s)`, `failStep(q, s)`, `end(q[, outcome])`, `fail(q[, outcome])`, `drop`, `restart`, `close(q, ...)`, `flag(f, v)`, `unflag(f)`, `call(name, ...)`, `pay(n)`, `give(kind, id[, n])`, `xp(n)`, `standing(track, n)`, `waypoint(name, world, x, z[, cell])` (one of the character's own, marked as the story's and set once), `waypointGone(id or name)` (only one a story set: a story never takes away a waypoint the player set), `say(text)`, and the conversations' `trust(track, n)` (only on an answer, an option or an outcome marked `pressure`), `charge(n)` (a price, taken every time, on an answer whose condition asks `credits() >= n`), `introduce(cast/<id>)` (they give their name), `gesture(clip)` and `choose(q, s, option)`, and the documents' `doc(doc/<id>)` (handed over, to read), `note(text)` (a line written into the journal where the player is) and `file(isb, kind, weight[, doc/<id>][, tag, ...])` (an entry in the ISB's file: `kind` one of `intercept`, `sighting`, `associate`, `incident`, `classification`, `note`, a weight of nought or more, the entry's page and its tags). `pay`, `give`, `xp`, `standing` and `trust` are paid once each, keyed by where they are written, exactly as a reward is (in a conversation, once to a character for each answer). The tracks, the named people, what is owed and the companion have words of their own (below). `impound`, `licence`, `district`, `take` and `misfile` are kept for a later pass and refused. Signals are personal; the engine raises `used:<object>`, `entered:<area>`, `left:<area>`, `room:<template>#<cell>`, `died:<creature>` and `world:<id>`, a cast member's conversation raises `talked:<who>` and `talked:<who>#<node>` at each node it reaches, a document read to its last page raises `read:<doc>`, and only the test set may wait on `debug:<name>`, which the console raises.

**A conversation** (`talk/<id>.jsonc`) is `{ "format": 1, "id", "rev", "speaker": "cast/<id>", "entry": [...], "nodes": { ... } }`. `entry` is tried in order and the first whose `when` holds is where it starts; the last has no `when`, so nobody is ever mute. A node says its lines in turn (`say`: a line is its words, or `{ "text", "tone", "gesture", "shot", "wait", "fill" }`), runs its actions (`do`), and then offers its answers (`replies`), goes on by itself (`next`) or ends (`end: true`). An answer is `{ "id", "text", "said", "when", "show": "hide" | "disable", "why", "stakes", "pressure", "once", "do", "to", "gesture", "shot" }`: a failed `when` hides it, or greys it with `why`; `stakes` says what is at risk before it is chosen (never the outcome); `once` offers it once; `said` is what the player says aloud in other words (`null`: nothing); with no `to` the conversation ends after it. A line's and an answer's `gesture` (`emt_nod`, an alias the game's scripts used such as `explain`, a family `@agree`, `mood:<name>` to change the speaker's idle for the rest of the talk, or `null` for none) and `shot` (`over-player`, `over-npc`, `close-npc`, `close-player`, `two`, `wide` or `hold`, with an optional `side`) are the only fields a cutscene editor would write: left out, the game fills them as the line plays, and never writes them into the file. `cues` are kept and not played yet. Where a conversation stands is never in the book: it starts at its entry again after a reload, and so does one whose file was edited under you. **The cast** (`cast/<id>.jsonc`) is `{ "id", "name", "unknownAs", "body": <creature catalogue entry>, "world", "at": [x, z], "room", "heading", "mood", "side", "tree": "talk/<id>", "stand": condition, "mortal", "companion" }`: each is stood where the file says, for you alone, while you are within 120 m and their `stand` holds, known by `unknownAs` until they `introduce` themselves, essential unless `mortal`. **E** at one with a conversation of their own plays it: each line in turn for as long as its words take (a click or any number moves on), every line with a gesture -- chosen by the game from its tone, its words and the speaker's mood at the rates the game's own scripts show, or stillness on purpose -- and the camera cutting between you and them, over your shoulder as they speak and over theirs as your answer is said aloud, cuts held at least 1.2 s, on the side of the line chosen as it opens. The answers are numbered after the last line, a greyed one with its reason and what is at stake under any that names it, with the game's own way out after them. With a server, the server plays the conversation and hands the browser each node as it is reached, never the file.

**A document** (`docs/<id>.doc.txt`) is a format of its own, plain text: a header of `key: value` lines -- `kind` (`workorder`, `memo`, `log`, `intercept`, `letter`, `notice`, `declaration` or `dossier`), `id`, `title`, `issuer`, `client` -- then a blank line and the body (a header's value is everything after its colon, a `#` included; the only comment is a line beginning `//`). Paragraphs are separated by a blank line; `[field] LABEL: value` is a form's field, `[table]` with `| a | b |` rows up to `[/table]` a table, `[stamp] WORDS` and `[sign] WORDS` a stamp and a signature, `[redact] ... [/redact]` words that are a black bar on the page (`[redact when met(cast/x)] ... [/redact]`: lifted for a reader for whom the condition holds), `[slot name]` a place a version fills, and `[page]` turns the page. `{player}`, `{player.species}`, `{npc:cast/<id>}` (the name the player knows them by) and `{date}` (what `calendar.jsonc` says: `{ "text": "..." }`, or `{ "epoch", "day": "game" | "real", "yearDays", "firstYear", "format": "Day {day}, year {year}" }`) are filled as it is read. After the body, `--- variant <id> when <condition>` lines each begin a version, whose `[slot name] words` lines fill the body's slots; the first version whose condition holds is the one read, and a version may not call itself the true one (rule 10). The first time a character opens a document the page is worked out and **frozen**: written into their journal as it was read and read back from there for ever after, whatever the file says later, so two characters who made different choices hold two different letters and neither is the real one. Read to its last page, a document is put away and every step waiting on it is done -- except a page whose foot still asks something (a card whose job is on offer, a choice still open on it), which stays to read until it is answered. A page whose frozen words were lost (a cleared browser store, a server past its cap) says so and can still be read to its end and answered; one the journal is too full to keep is shown as it reads now and never frozen. A job may be offered as its **card** (a `workorder` or a `notice` named by the quest's `card`): offered, it opens as that page with the job's stakes and Accept and Decline at its foot, after any conversation that offered it has closed. **The journal** (**J**, its second tab) holds what the character witnessed and nothing else: a page read, a call heard, a conversation's transcript (written when its window closes, every line in turn and every answer said aloud, a client line as this browser read it out), a `note` the story writes where the player is, and notes of the player's own on any entry. It only ever grows; it can be filtered by world, by person and by job, a page or a call opens again as it was, and its words are kept by their hash (in this browser's IndexedDB, and once for everybody on a server, which asks a browser for any it has not got when it takes a book played alone). **The ISB's file** (`file.jsonc`: `{ "isb": { "levels": [{ "at": 10, "name": "...", "reacts": { "empire": "mean" } }], "reveal": span, "revealBy": { "<kind>": span } } }`) is what the agency has on the character, written by the story's `file()` action and never shown on the message line or in the journal. It only ever grows, and the level its exposure reaches works at once -- a condition reads it, and a level that says so makes a track's people greet the character no warmer than `mid` or `mean` -- while the player sees an entry on the File tab only once its reveal time has passed (six game hours by default) or the story has handed over its page.

**Standing, Trust and rank** are kept on three tracks, `rebellion`, `empire` and `freelance`. Standing is how much work a track has had out of the character, from nought up; it can be ground out, but a repeatable job pays only its `standingPerRealDay` of it a real day. Trust is a small number (-5 to 10) that moves only on an answer, an option or an outcome marked `pressure` (rule 5, which holds `trust()` and `npc()` alike). The player sees Standing as a bar to the next rung with no numbers on it and Trust only as words, on the journal's **Standing** tab; a rank changing is said on the message line in words, and Trust moving never is. **`ladders.jsonc`** is `{ "trustWords": [five, lowest first], "<track>": { "floor": n, "rungs": [{ "id", "name", "standing", "trust", "promote": "beat" | "auto", "pays", "brief", "exposure", "closes": [quest...] }] } }`: a rung is taken when the story says `promote(t)` once its Standing and Trust are reached (`rankReady(t)` is the condition the beat is written behind), or by itself with `"auto"`, and only while the track has taken the character on -- `useTrack(t)` marks it `used`, `activate(t[, cell])` takes them on, and there is no recruiter but the story. A rank is written down, never worked out, so it can be lost: `demote(t[, n])` steps down n rungs and closes the jobs each rung left names in `closes`, `suspend(t, gameHours)` lifts itself on the shared clock, `burn(t)` takes the rank and the cell, puts Standing back to the ladder's `floor`, Trust to nought and refuses the track's own named people, and only `activate` takes it back, and `assign(t, division)` names a division. `exposure` multiplies over every rung held to make each entry in the ISB's file heavier. Conditions: `rank(t) == "id"`, `rankAtLeast(t, id)`, `rankReady(t)`, `trackIs(t, status)` (`none`, `used`, `active`, `suspended`, `burned`) and `division(t) == "name"`.

**Named people** are the cast, and the game's own people a cast file promotes (`{ "id", "row": "row:<key>", "name", "unknownAs", "side", "tree" }`: kept under that row, given a name and a side, and stood by the world rather than the story). Only they keep a record with the character -- their own Standing (-1000 to 1000) and Trust toward them, whether they will speak (`access`), whether they are alive, and what the character knows of it -- and the journal's **People** tab shows them by the name the character knows, where and when they were last seen, and gone only once the character has been told. `npc(who, standing, trust)` moves their regard (its Trust under pressure only), `refuse(who)` stops them speaking (they say so and nothing else), `vouch(by, who, cost)` opens a refusal at the cost of the voucher's own Standing, and `kill(who[, how][, doc/<id>])` kills them for good for this character alone: never stood or spoken to again, and known only through the page named, or because the companion falls beside them. Conditions: `npcStanding(who) >= n`, `npcTrust(who) >= n`, `alive(who)` and `access(who) == "refused"`. **What is owed**: `fine(n, reason[, to])` takes what the purse holds, never below nought, and writes the rest owed to `to` (`fines` when it names nobody), said on the message line; `debt(to, n)` moves a debt either way and `debt(to) >= n` reads one.

**The companion** is one cast member written `"companion": true`, taken on with `recruit(who)` (one at a time), told `waitHere()` (stays where they stand when told, kept as where the player stood, and is there when the player comes back) or `dismiss()`. They follow as a follower does, stood beside the player at every arrival (behind them where there is a floor and nothing in the way, else the next clear spot round them), never frozen and never put down for their distance; taken on, told to wait or let go, they are the same body where they stand, so the conversation that did it goes on. Struck to nothing they go **down** rather than dying, out of everything's list of targets, and get up when **E** is held beside them for three seconds or by themselves after twenty with nothing near still in the fight. They die only through the story's `kill()`. Their kills are credited to nobody, and they are this browser's alone: a server keeps the record and believes the browser about down and up, and nobody else sees them. Conditions: `companion(who) == "active"` (`waiting`, `downed`, `dead`, `released`) and `companionUp()`.

**In the game.** The test set is read under `npm run dev` (and never in a release unless `__debug.story({ tests: true })` asks for it), and `story-private/` whenever there is one; an edit to either reloads it.
