Four times a second the detectors look for what the running jobs wait on -- arriving at a place (in its room when it names one), stepping into a room, into or out of an area, onto a world, a kill the player is credited with (a blow of their own, or the server's word of a death that names them among those who struck a creature another browser keeps), the player's own death -- and raise each again every two seconds while it stays true, so nothing lost is lost for good. **E** works a story object (`use`, after a port's own things and before a person). Down the right edge under the group's roster the tracker shows up to three jobs followed (with none followed, the newest job taken), each objective with what it counts, how far off its place is and how long it has left, and under them up to three documents waiting to be read; the message line says, in the story's own colour, when a job is taken, an objective set or done, a place reached, a reward paid, a job done or failed, and when a time limit comes under ten minutes. A job's own waypoint stands in the world, on the minimap and on the map like the character's own, switched on unless its step says otherwise. Every time a job keeps is the shared clock (this machine's with no server), so a world-clock timer runs on while a panel is open and while the character is logged off, and what fell due meanwhile is settled in order when the book is next read.

**`npm run story:check [folder]`** reads a set and says everything wrong with it before anybody plays it, as `file:line: error: ... (rule n)`, and fails when there is an error. With no folder named it checks `story-private/` (when there is one) and then the test set. It refuses what would leave a player stuck or the rules broken: an id that does not exist (rule 1), a step that cannot be reached or can be reached and never finish, an edge back to an earlier step from one not marked `"loop": true`, a step waiting `after` one that may be skipped or after two only different branches of one `nextOne` or choice lead to (unless its `onStuck` says what happens then; two different conditional `next` edges are warned about), or a circle of quests that would give itself for ever from one grant (2), a signal a step waits on that nothing raises (3), a word not in the vocabulary or kept for a later pass (4), Trust moved outside a choice or outcome marked `pressure`, or in a repeatable quest, and a repeatable quest that closes anything (5), a journal `note` where the player might not be present (6), a world this game does not have (7), a relative place outside the test set (8), a `charge` not guarded by `credits() >= ` that much (9), anything marked canonical or "true" (10, a document's version included), a document a card, a step, a `doc()` or `file()` action or a `witnessed` condition names that no set holds, or a version of one it has not got (11), and a conversation whose last entry has a condition, which links to a node it has not got, or which can reach a node that neither ends, goes on nor offers an answer (12); a `choice` step nothing ever chooses is a signal nothing raises (3), Trust on a node's own actions or an answer not marked `pressure` is rule 5, and a gesture that is not one is rule 4. It warns about a job's card that is not a work order or a notice, a kill target or a cast member's body not in the creature catalogue on this machine (13), a gesture clip not on the player's own body there, a node nothing reaches, counts every `call` that names no registered script (14), and warns about a step that goes nowhere (the quest then ends done by itself). It reads the permanent placements of **this checkout's own** `public/placements/` (whatever `--root` names, since the placements a quest names are the ones its build carries; `--placements=<folder>` names another folder holding `story/` and `world/`), and refuses an object whose `place/<id>` no row on its world carries, names a row the game never stands (anything in the dungeons' world of copies, a building in a space zone), or whose `near` is 30 m or more from where that row stands (1: the game finds a thing only nearer than that, counting the height too); an id both files hold on one world is an error once (the world edit's row stands and the story's never does), a row a file cannot read or the game never stands is a warning naming it, and an object written with the template of a thing a permanent row stands near its point is warned about, since the game never finds that row by its template. Every number and word of it is ours; the step types and their fields are the client's quest-task columns.

## Progress

A character learns by doing (the owner's *Character Progression* document, its first slice built a wave at a time). The rules are **`src/progress/`**, pure and its own folder only, so the server runs the very files the browser does, as it runs the story's: one record a character (`record.ts`), which only ever moves by a closed list of changes, every number in them whole and already clamped; one table of every number progression can change (`stats.ts`, the document's Table A), each stat with today's literal as its base and worked out through the document's layers (proficiency, the core skills, the disciplines, gear, buffs) only when something grants to it; the seven core skills, the four built (`core.ts`: Marksmanship, Melee, Fortitude and Acrobatics, rated 0 to 100 from their XP, each steadying something a little along a curve and with a milestone every 25 points); Demolitions as data (`demolitions.ts`, `disciplines.ts`: a Novice, four branches of four and a Master, bought with its own XP and Skill Points, 250 of them from creation and never earned); and the challenge level, `1 + Skill Points spent x 89 / 250`, read against the game's own 90-level table (`challenge.ts`). Every number is one of three tables (`tune.ts`: `PROGRESS_RULES`, `PROGRESS_LIMITS`, `PROGRESS_TUNE`), each marked where it came from, and every one moves live through `__debug.progress({ rules })`.

**Nothing changes until something is earned.** An empty record grants nothing, so every stat the game reads is the very number it was (the player's health, a gun's spread, a grenade's throw, a blast on yourself, a fall, the aim's blend); `tools/swg/tests/progressIdentity.test.ts` holds every one of them to the bit. The record is kept in this browser (`swg.progress.<character>`) and counted in the character's change mark as the story book is.

**With a server, the server holds it and is the only one that pays** (`server/progress.mjs`, its hail saying `progress: 1`). The record settles the way the story book does once the claim is answered: what was made alone is carried up whole on first contact, after that the browser's copy is taken only when the character settled its way and changed alone, and a copy the server did not take is set aside in storage with the player told once. From then on the browser only claims what happened -- what was fought, at what level, how it ended, which skills it used -- and the server works every amount out itself by the same rules, under its own limits and rate (`--set progress.rules.<name>=n`, `--set progress.limits.<name>=n`, never past the backstop), and sends the batch back; a record it wrote is read back -- on its next start, by the browser, out of the browser's storage -- under fixed backstops, so a cap or a limit moved up is never cut. A shared body (a lair's creature, a person at a post) counts dead only on its keeper's own word of the death, which the relay already hears, once to each character and only while the body stays down (a lair's creature comes back under the same name); a claim that names one dead before that word has come is parked for up to three seconds and paid on what was heard. A body nobody shares is believed, up to sixty a minute. The buckets that hold each pool to so much a minute are kept by character for the life of the server. Giving a node back takes its fee through the server's purse and gives the node back in the same step. A quest's bonus is paid by the server's own story host, or owed until the character's progress settles and paid then, once. A browser against an old relay, or a server whose hail says nothing of progress, holds it itself exactly as with no server.

**Fights are noticed for you, and only the ones you win pay** (`encounter.ts`, `payout.ts`). A fight opens on the first blow you land on, or take from, anything that could be in one -- a bite, a blade, a bolt -- and closes won when everything in it is dead (however it died: your blow, a burn, a follower's, the companion's, a group member's), lost when you die, and broken off when it goes quiet with foes still in it, or on a travel or a jump to another system. It pays every skill your own blows used the whole fight's worth: a gun Marksmanship, a launcher, a grenade or a charge Demolitions, a blade, a kick, a fist or a bolt your blade turned Melee, a roll that beat a blow Acrobatics; Fortitude is paid for living through a fight that took half your health in blows (never a fall, the lava, drowning, a fire's tick, a crash or your own blast, none of which a roll dodges either). A fight's worth is each foe that fought and died, at 25 times its band against your challenge level, held to 400; the same creature again pays less, a first creature and a first planet pay more, a lost fight with the foes near falling pays a tenth, and coming back after four hours away doubles what fights pay for a while (the rested bonus). Something that would never fight (a grazing herd beast) pays nothing. A landing roll that keeps some of a fall off pays Acrobatics on its own, at most 120 in any fifteen minutes. A quest's experience is paid as a bonus into the skills you used while it ran, never into a general pool (by this browser, or by a server that holds both the story and progress; a server from before progress that holds the story pays none). The four core skills rise 0 to 100 and steady a little as they do -- spread, the saber's swing, health and burns, falls, air control and a roll's dodge -- with a milestone every 25 (Marksmanship 25 halves the aimed spread once you have aimed still for a second; Acrobatics 25 rolls you out of falls up to 8 m for nothing; Fortitude 75 leaves you one point of health once a fight). Every rule number is `PROGRESS_RULES` and every number of the fights `ENCOUNTER_TUNE`, all ours.

**Demolitions is learnt at a commando trainer** (the emulator's own, on Corellia, Rori, Talus and Tatooine). E and Train opens the inventory's Skills tab (I) on its **Progress** section at that trainer: the four skills with their milestones, Skill Points spent of 250 and your challenge level, the Demolitions tree with what each node costs and who teaches it, the experience banked, and where the nearest trainer stands (with a waypoint on offer). A node costs its tier's Skill Points and XP; the Novice, III, IV and the Master want a trainer, I and II are learnt anywhere; a node is given back at a trainer for 500 credits a tier step, from the bottom of a branch up, one at a time (with a server keeping the purse, the next waits until the server has answered for the last fee), and learnt again within thirty days for half its XP. Every payout, milestone, node learnt or given back and quest bonus is one line on the message line. The class switch on C is the developer's.

**The challenge table** is `datatables/player/level_con_table.iff`, converted by the `customization` command into `assets-private/characters/levels.json` and never committed; without it a fight is banded by a fallback of ours by the level gap. Its colours are the client's interface and are never read.

## Permanent placements

Beside what a player puts down for themselves, a world stands things **for good, for every player**: the rows of two plain JSON files that ship with the build. `public/placements/story/<world>.json` is the story's dressing -- the yard a quest sends you to, the locker it asks you to open -- and `public/placements/world/<world>.json` the map's own edits, a house or a wall the game's own worlds did not place; `<world>` is the pack id (`tatooine`, `kashyyyk_main`, `space_tatooine`). Every browser reads both out of the build it runs, with or without a server and whether or not it plays the story, so a row reaches players with a launcher update once its file is committed and merged to main, and nothing about it goes through a server. A file left uncommitted there stops a local release pack: commit it or delete it before packing.

```json
{
  "version": 1,
  "world": "tatooine",
  "frame": "raw",
  "rows": [
    { "id": "eisley-bay22-locker", "kind": "prop", "pack": "props", "thing": "<props catalogue id>", "x": 3448.12, "y": 5.03, "z": -4688.4, "q": [0.92388, 0, 0.38268, 0], "inside": true, "name": "Locker", "note": "the drawer and the coat hooks" },
    { "id": "anchorhead-house-1", "kind": "building", "pack": "gallery", "model": "ply_tato_house_sm_s01_fp1", "deed": "<deed id>", "x": 0, "y": 12.4, "z": 0, "q": [0.70711, 0, 0.70711, 0], "clear": 14.5 }
  ]
}
```

A row's `id` is a-z, 0-9, `_` and `-` (64 at most) and is unique on its world across both files: where both hold one, the world edit's row stands and the story's is skipped. `kind` is `prop` or `building`; `pack` is `props` (with the catalogue's `thing`), `gallery` (a `model`, with the `deed` that makes it when there is one) or `world` (a `model` out of that world's own pack). **On a planet a row is written in the raw frame**, the one `/loc`, a story's `near`, an area and a waypoint are written in, so numbers copy straight across; in a space zone (`"frame": "game"`) it is the game's own. `y` is absolute and never measured again, and `q` is the turn as `[w, x, y, z]` in the file's frame. A row may also say `solid` (every row is solid unless it says `false`, except a seat or a table out of the props pack, which is passable unless it says `true`, since the person the world sits on it would be pushed off), `inside` (it stands in a room), `clear` (metres of plants kept off round it), `guard` (metres past its own footprint in which a player's own prop is refused as part of this world: 1.5 on a story row that says nothing, none on a world edit), `name` and `note` (words, since JSON has no comments) and `at`; `fill`, `locked` and `effectsOf` are kept and do nothing yet. A row that does not read is skipped and named on the console, never the whole file, and a file whose header is not its own world's is refused whole. A file holds 2,000 rows at most.

In the game a permanent thing stands like anything the world places: solid where it is solid, part of the world -- **O** beside one says so and never picks it up -- and nobody's own prop goes down inside a story row's guard. **A quest names one** as a story object whose template is the row's key: `{ "id": "bay22-locker", "world": "tatooine", "template": "place/eisley-bay22-locker", "near": [3448.1, -4688.4], "reach": 2, "label": "LOCKER" }`. Nothing else in the story changes for it; `npm run story:check` checks that a row of that id stands on that world, less than 30 m from `near`, and `__debug.storyHere()` beside one hands over that object and an area round it, ready to paste. `__debug.placements({ snippet: 'example' })` prints a ready-made world-edit file round where you stand, to try the files without the editor (save it as the file it names, and commit it only if it is meant to ship). `npm run swg -- status assets-private` says which pack the rows need that this install has not got, and asks for nothing because of them.

**The editor, on the dev server alone** (`npm run dev`; a release carries none of it). The Props and Housing tabs each have a switch between **Yours** and **Permanent**, which starts on Yours every session and is never kept across a reload. On Permanent, a thing taken from either tab goes down into one of the two files, the story's dressing the first time; **L** with it in hand moves it round yours, the story's dressing and the world edits (a permanent row picked up goes between the two files only: a permanent thing never becomes yours). A label at the top of the screen and the placing bar say where it is going, and the ghost is drawn in the accent colour where it may stand. The first save to a file in a session waits for a second click, as does putting one down on a spot the ghost refused; every save names its file and its id on the message line and stands at once. **O** beside a permanent prop picks it up exactly where it stands (the nearer of yours and the permanent ones), and with neither near, your own doorstep's house, then a permanent building's origin; Escape puts it back as its file has it, and a click saves it where it is now. **Delete**, O again or the bar's Put away takes a permanent row out of its file, and asks first. On Permanent the Housing tab lists every gallery building with rooms beside the deeds, any of which a double-click takes in hand by its model, deed or not, to be raised or sunk up to 30 m; and each tab lists what this world stands for good of its kind, nearest first, with Go to, Pick up, Delete (asked first), Rename, a name and a note, Solid, and a story object or an area naming it to paste. The editor writes one row at a time: it reads the file as it stands, writes every other row back exactly as it read it (fields written by hand that this build does not read included), and takes a file away when its last row goes. **The dev server loads the editor's own code once, at its first save, and runs that code until it is restarted**, so a change to that code needs `npm run dev` started again. Nothing the editor writes is committed by it.

## Vehicle damage

A vehicle's hull takes damage from a hard hit (the velocity lost in one step beyond what braking or a slope can take off) and from bolts, a blaster's or a ship's: sparks and a jolt to the rider on the hit, smoke below two thirds of the hull, heavy smoke, sparks and a limp (about half the power) below a third, and at nothing it blows up and throws the rider off. The prompt shows the hull once it is dented. Hover kinds pitch and roll to the ground a length ahead and behind, so a rise ahead lifts the nose before the hull meets it, and speeder bikes ride a little higher than they did.

## The picture's effects

The Graphics menu's **Effects** page is one master switch (on by default) with a switch, and where it means anything a strength, for each effect under it. With it on, the frame is drawn into a target of the game's own — half floats, so light brighter than white survives, a stencil for the portal renderer and a depth buffer the effects can read — and reaches the screen through a chain of passes in a fixed order: the picture is **cleaned** (a pixel that is not a number becomes black before any blur can spread it), the sun's **god rays** are scattered into it, the camera's movement **smears** it, the bright parts **spill over** (the engine glows, the bolts, the suns), the **output** pass applies the exposure, the tone curve and the colour space exactly once, and the **edges are smoothed** at the end. With it off the frame is drawn straight to the screen as before, multisampled, and none of the passes run.

Nothing in the chain ever writes back into the scene's own target, and that is the whole point of it: a pass may read the frame's depth only while it is drawing somewhere else, and before this the god rays and the speed blur each sampled the depth of the very buffer they were writing into, so the browser threw the draw away and said nothing — the rays never appeared at all, and the blur and the bloom went missing whenever the sun was off the screen. `__debug.fxCheck()` draws one frame asking the driver for an error after every step and names the step at fault; it should answer `{ clean: true }` both with the sun on screen and with it behind you.

The blur is the camera's: each pixel's point is found in the world from the frame's depth, projected with the last frame's camera to see where it was on the screen, and the colour averaged along that movement, so the ground rushing under a ship at speed and a wall sweeping past in a turn smear in proportion, the far sky hardly at all, and nothing within a few dozen metres of the camera (the hull flown, a cockpit) at all. Its strength is a slider (0.35 by default, a film's shutter at 60 frames a second; the smear is scaled so a slow frame is not a longer one), and it is capped at a few percent of the screen. Things that move on their own smear too: the game lists them each frame (the player and what they ride, vehicles, creatures, fighters, mobiles, other players) and a velocity picture of their moving parts is merged with the camera's reprojection of everything else; what the camera follows stays sharp at any size.

**God rays** come from the same depth: the sun's place on the screen is found by projecting its direction, and each pixel marches towards it summing the open sky along the way (depth past two and a half kilometres counts as sky) with a decay, so light streams past a tree, a wall or a hull that stands against the sun and nothing is drawn twice. They come before the blur and the bloom, so the light smears and spills as the sun's does, take the day's sun colour, fade towards the horizon and as the sun leaves the screen, and are off at night and in space. Their toggle and strength (0.6 by default) are on the Effects page.

The edges are smoothed by FXAA rather than by multisampling: the renderer resolves a multisampled target at the end of every render call, and the portal renderer makes a dozen of those a frame, which took the frame rate from 144 to 11. A pixel that is not a number (a material that divided by zero somewhere) is black on the screen, and the bloom's blur used to spread one into a black box the size of its coarsest level whenever the object was in view; the first pass of the chain makes such pixels black before anything else sees them, and `__debug.blackBox()` names the object under one so the material can be fixed.

Turning Effects on or off no longer costs a pause. Tone mapping is applied by a different program depending on whether the frame is drawn to the screen or to a target, so every material has a variant for each, and moving the switch compiles the other set in the background while the frames keep drawing the old way — a notice over the picture counts the objects — and the picture changes over only once the programs are linked. Settings kept from before the switch existed are carried over: a speed blur left off stays a motion blur left off, at the strength it had.

From the console, `__debug.postfx()` lists every pass with its setting, whether it drew last frame and why not, what it cost and which pass drew to the canvas; `__debug.postfx({ godRays: false })` forces one on or off (`null` hands it back to the settings); `__debug.fxTiming(true)` and then `__debug.fxTiming()` times every step on the GPU against its budget; `__debug.fxView('linearDepthHalf')` shows one of the pictures the effects work from — the distance to each pixel, the direction each surface faces, the mask of the player — instead of the picture, and `__debug.fxView()` puts it back; `__debug.fxWarm()` compiles every pass's shaders again, and a second call should say none were needed.

**Wet ground.** Wetness rises while it rains and falls slowly in sunshine and wind, and every world material, the ground and the lakes read it: surfaces darken by how porous they look, turn glossy and reflect the sky at a low angle, flat ground near you gathers puddles that the rain rings, lakes ring too, and snow settles on what faces up. The rays that keep the rain off covered ground also tell the shading what is sheltered, so a floor under a roof stays dry and a wall is judged by the open air beside it. Rooms, ships, people and what they hold are never weathered. It is all compiled in once and moved by uniforms, so the weather never recompiles a shader; "Wet ground" in the weather settings turns it off.

**Flora.** The Graphics page's **Flora** section holds every number the plants have, scene content like the weather and none of it an effect: the grass on or off, its distance (1 is the world's own 20 to 32 m; the window of ground built round you follows it) and its density; the far tree cards on or off and their distance (1 is the world's own end, 500 to 1000 m); the small plants' distance (times the world's own, never under 64 m); the trees' distance, past which the far tree cards stand in for them, lifted over the ground they stand on so no bare ring opens between the two; and what casts and takes the sun's shadow -- trees (on), small plants (off, the dearest switch there, and at their finest only when on), the grass near you (off: the original's never cast), shadows falling on the grass and the far cards (on), the far cards casting (off, and only where they stand inside Shadow distance), and whether a leaf takes its own tree's shadow (no, as the owner asked). The Shadows section's **Smallest shadow caster** now really governs placed buildings and props, what players put down included, live; before, it reached almost nothing. Every switch is a uniform or a `castShadow` and builds no shader program in play: the depth programs a caster turned on for the first time needs are drawn behind the first loading screen. Every one is also a console knob (`__debug.grass`, `farCards`, `flora`, `floraShadows`, `shadows`) that moves the same setting live and never saves it: only the page saves.

**Volumetric clouds — unfinished, and off by default.** It draws, and everything below is true of it, but how it *looks* has not been settled: the size of a cloud, the depth of the deck, how hard the detail eats an edge and how it is lit are all numbers of ours that nobody has judged by eye yet. It is on the shelf rather than finished, and switching it off is the flat sheets exactly as they always were. It is also by far the most expensive thing in the chain. What it does: instead of the flat sheets the client drew its sky with, a ray is marched through a slab of air overhead, a step at a time, with a few more steps toward the sun from each one, and what comes back is cloud you fly into rather than a picture painted across the sky. It stands in exactly the slab the sheets hang in and takes them down while it draws, and switching it off puts them back on the next frame with nothing rebuilt. Nothing about how it is lit is invented: the sun, the ambient and the haze are the very ones the rest of the scene is lit by, so each world keeps its own colour with no table of its own and the lava world's cloud is red because its sky already is. What *is* read off the client is how much of the sky a world covers and how dark that cover is, measured by `npm run swg -- clouds assets-private` from the very pictures the sheets are drawn with — the storm world 85% covered at brightness 0.30, the lava world the darkest at 0.17, the desert 12% and bright. The noise it marches through is ours, eight megabytes written by the same command, the same on every world, and so is the table beside it that says what each threshold through that noise really covers: the same command marches the volume it has just written and writes down what it found, because what a share of sky costs in threshold is three separate things at once and none of them is arithmetic. Measured afterwards a different way, every world gets within two points of the sky its own art asks for. It marches at half the screen by default (a quarter and full are in the menu) and comes back through an upsample that weighs each tap by how near its own depth is, since a plain one haloes every ridge; it writes no depth and asks for no light, so nothing else in the chain has to know it ran and no material anywhere recompiles when it is switched on. It draws nothing in space, nothing indoors, and nothing while a dust storm has the sky already. `__debug.clouds()` reports what the world measured, what the march is being asked for and what it cost, and every number of it moves live — the deck's floor and ceiling, the step counts and the look at once, the rest by rebuilding the program.

**Effects to do**, roughly in the order they would pay off:

1. Shoreline foam from the water mask; a lit blade's glow on the ground is the `bladeGlow` pass now, and the bolts' light still comes from the pooled lights.
2. Limbs that blur on their own: moving things smear by their root's motion only, so a swinging arm, a ragdoll's fall and a peer's roll do not (a previous-bone texture per skeleton is the sketched fix).
3. The sunlit patch where a doorway's beam lands: the light shafts already work it out, and its gain waits at 0 until it has been judged by eye.

## Normal maps

Every converted model carries its normal map, **decoded by the slot it sits in**, exactly as the client's own pixel programs decode it: a `CNRM` map is the game's compressed layout (x in the alpha, y in the green, z rebuilt), an `NRML` map plain signed RGB, and a map is written only where the shader's program reads one (`src/swg/normalDecode.ts`, which the converter and the game's recolour both run). Before material format 6 the layout was guessed from the picture, and an ordinary map keeps the height it was drawn from in its alpha, so 1,157 of the shaders the game uses -- most of Tatooine's trims, tiles and filler walls among them -- were lit about 33 degrees off; the recolour of a worn piece (`recipeNormal`) made the same guess over 554 of its 873 plain maps and now decodes by the tag it already knew. A normal map the shader reads on its second coordinate set is drawn on it. The green is read as it stands, which is what looks right on a wall's edges by eye and what the maps' own heights say (`__debug.normals(1, 1)` is that; `(1, -1)` flips every normal map in the world and on the character the other way, which is how the game had them for years, to compare), and the Graphics menu has a strength slider for them (1 as the game has them). From the same format every textured material also says what highlight the client drew -- the mask its program multiplies its specular by (in the metal-rough image's red), the MATL's specular colour and power, the band where the program bends it into one -- and whether its shader has a reflection cube (`extras.swgSpec`, `extras.swgCube`; `__debug.shine` shows them). **The game draws that highlight in place of three's own** (`src/world/specular.ts`, its arithmetic mirrored in `src/world/specMath.ts`): the specular colour times the mask times (N·H) to the power, with no Fresnel term and no N·L factor, or on the client's fake-anisotropic programs (hair, fur, most of the two-tone cloth) its band of N·H, a ring 37 degrees off the mirror direction, times N·L. It is measured against a white surface facing the light, as the client's was, so a shader's colour of 0.5 is a highlight half as bright as a white wall in the sun, about 15 degrees wide at the median power of 20, where three's GGX had drawn the same shaders a median 2.6 times too bright and a quarter as wide (21 times on the two-tone cloth, which is why shirts glinted in stripes). One line of three's lighting is replaced, the mode, colour and power are uniforms of each material and the knobs shared ones, so every converted material shares one program text and switching back (`__debug.spec({ use: 'three' })`) compiles nothing; a wet or snowed surface keeps three's GGX by how wet or snowed it is, since the rain's glint and the snow's matt both ride on roughness. The select screen, the creator and the ship edit page draw it too, on what the world has not yet seen. The term is capped at a white surface facing the light (ours: the client clamped every pixel to white, and without it glass's colour of 10 blooms into a blob), and `localStorage['swg.spec'] = 'three'` leaves it off at the next load. And a surface whose shader has no reflection cube takes none of the sky's reflection, nor the sky-coloured fill three's environment adds to that same material's diffuse light; the client gave it neither (`__debug.reflections({ plain: 1 })` puts both back to compare). What is worn, held, flown and ridden, and the creatures, reflect the same sky where their shader has a cube: before, only a planet's own models had a reflection at all, so every piece of chrome armour, every hilt and every ship's metal drew dark. They join as the world prepares them, so their programs are built with the reflection in them and nothing compiles in play, and the wardrobe's doll, the select screen, the creator and the ship edit page, which draw the same materials in contexts of their own, give them a studio of their own for the length of each of their own draws (`__debug.reflections({ models: false })` is the look before, live; `localStorage['swg.reflections.models'] = '0'` keeps them out at the next load). A pack converted before format 6 says nothing of either and is drawn exactly as before. Packs converted before carry the old guesses: `status` asks for every model command again (the planets' packs, `species`, `parts`, `player`, `wardrobe`, `weapons`, `ships`, `creatures`, `gallery`, `space`, `spawns`, `travel`), and a converter change needs that reconversion. A character's head and body take theirs from the live recipes as well, since the age slider picks among them.

## Debugging

**Checks before pushing.** `npm run typecheck` (or `npx tsc --noEmit -p .`), `npm run build`, `npm run test:all` (which runs the converter, terrain, effects, weather, first-person head, close combat, inventory, audio, display, network, story and frame-report suites; plain node scripts that print `ok` per check, there is no test framework), and `node --check tools/swg/<file>.mjs` or `node --check server/<file>.mjs` for converter and server modules. The network suite on its own is `npm run test:net`.

**The server's own numbers.** The server invents a handful of rates, caps and patiences — how fast a browser may talk, how much may be queued for one that has stopped reading, how long it has to say who it is, how often the world is written. None of them is from the game, they all live in one `TUNING` object at the top of `server/relay.mjs`, and `http://localhost:8787/` prints every one of them back along with what each connected browser is behind by. `npm run relay -- --set <name>=<number>` moves one for a run without a rebuild (`--set wire.<name>=<number>` for the caps on the message fields, and `--set combat.<name>=<number>` for the caps on a shot, a blow and a duel's patience, printed back under `combat`), and a name that is not one of them says so rather than being quietly ignored. The group's own numbers are the same: how many fit in a group, how long an invitation stands, how many invitations one person may have out, how long a place is held for somebody who is reloading, how long a leader's offer of a trip lasts, how many lines of chat one browser may send in a second and how many things it may ask its group for in a second are all invented here, all printed back by that page under `group`, and all moved by `--set group.<name>=<number>`. The distances beside them under `ranges` are not ours and are not tunable: an invitation reaching 90 m, a trade 8 m, a duel 128 m and a death blow 10 m are the client's own, read off its radial menu table, and the server enforces the invitation's.

**The console.** The game logs what it does as it does it, and the messages are meant to be read: a vehicle's hardpoints on spawning (`garage: xwing hardpoints: …`), a portal building's shell and rooms, a ship interior's colliders and entry, where a cockpit frame and the seat landed, `shaders: N compiled during play` when a shader compiled on a live frame (a stall; everything should compile behind the loading screen or in the background), and `hid an object` with the material names when something failed to draw. When something is wrong, the first thing to paste is the console from spawning or entering the thing that is wrong.

**`__debug` in the browser console** is the toolbox, on `window`; each helper returns a plain object or string. Every one of them has a row in the table below, and `tools/swg/tests/debugDocs.test.ts` fails, naming it, if one is added without.

**The debug menu** runs all of them without the console: the key left of 1 (the backquote to begin with, **Debug menu** on the Controls page) or **Debug** in the Escape menu opens a window listing every helper, grouped and searchable, with its rows from this table, an arguments box filled from its first example, and the answer folded under it with whatever it printed. It leaves the game running, so a measurement can be taken with it open. Its own row is below.

| Helper | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| `teleport(x, z)`, `time(0.5)`, `fog(1)`, `advance(seconds, ['KeyW'])` | Move (x, z in the game's own frame: on a planet that is not the pair `/loc`, the minimap and the map window read out, which goes through `teleportSwg`; in space `/loc` is this frame), set the time of day, scale the fog, simulate play with keys held |
| `look(0, -0.2)`, `zoom(6)`, `mouse(40, 0)`, `mouse(0, 0, 1)`, `swg()`, `advanceCost(10)`, `breakFrames(true)` | The view from a tab that cannot lock the mouse: `look(yaw, pitch)` aims the camera, in radians, and `zoom(metres)` sets how far back it sits; `mouse(dx, dy, wheel)` feeds mouse movement and wheel notches to the real frame loop as if the pointer were locked, and answers with the camera's yaw, pitch, distance, place and look (with a placing key held through `advance(t, ['KeyR'], true)` the movement turns or raises what is in hand instead, and the camera's yaw stays where it was; it lands whole in the next frame, so a slow hand is fed a little a frame). `swg()` is where you stand in the original game's own coordinates, with the ground's height there, which is what `terrain-check --at` and the game's `/way` take. `advanceCost(10)` simulates ten seconds through `advance` and says how long of the wall clock that took, with a verdict on whether it is cheap enough to stand in for a long walk — and it says as well that `advance` never streams anything, so a walk driven by it proves nothing about collision. `breakFrames(true)` makes every frame throw after the camera has moved, which is how to check that a failing frame cannot make the view drift; `false` puts it back |
| `hud()`, `hud({ overlay: false })`, `hud({ scale: 1.25 })`, `hud({ dpr: 2 })`, `hud({ boxes: true })`, `say('hit you', 'test')`, `hurt(180)`, `hit(12, true)` | What the head-up display costs, what it drew, and what it is showing. `overlay.ms` is the overlay canvas's own milliseconds averaged over the last sixty frames it drew, `overlay.ops` the canvas operations it made last frame and `overlay.shapes` the flight display's own count of the calls behind them. `writes` is the number to watch: the DOM writes of the last full second that were not by design, 0 in a steady frame, with the 4 Hz clock, the `/loc` line and the frame-rate line counted apart as `byDesign`, and the message line's, the action bar's and the nameplates' counted apart again, since a line said, an action offered or a plate over a moving head is a write that is meant to happen. `actions.list` is the bar as it stands, a key-cap and its words per slot shown, and `actions.state` the struct behind it, gathered eight times a second; `onFoot.keys` is the number row's key-caps, which is how a rebind is checked, and `onFoot.hands` what is in each hand; `feedback` counts the damage arcs standing and the numbers rising and says how long ago a shot of yours told and killed; `plates` is the name over a head. `{ overlay: false }` turns the canvas off for a baseline and `{ overlay: true }` puts it back; `{ scale: 1.25 }` and `{ dpr: 2 }` try the size and the sharper canvas without opening the menu, both clamped to the settings' own ends; `{ boxes: true }` outlines the region the overlay clears each frame. Every piece is a switch of its own — `{ condition, target, arcs, line, lines, fullPrompts, damageArc, damageNumbers, nameplate, jediCrosshair }` — and every invented number is reachable from the same call: `{ flight: { arcRadius: 150 } }` for the reticle and the arcs, `{ tune: { barPixels: 200 } }` for the on-foot write steps, `{ messages: { seconds: 3 } }` for how long a line stays, `{ feedback: { arcAlpha: 1.4 } }` for the damage arc and the tick, `{ plates: { pickHz: 20 } }` for the name over a head, `{ prompt: { slots: 3 } }` for how many actions the bar shows, and `{ wiring: { promptHz: 60, nearbyHz: 60, hurtRange: 400 } }` for the rates the game gathers at — set either rate to 60 to put the old per-frame cost back and measure what gathering at 8 and 4 saves. `__debug.say('hit you', 'a test line')` puts a line on the message line by hand; `__debug.hurt(180)` puts a damage arc up (0 ahead, 90 right, 180 behind, 270 left, `null` for a blow with no direction) and `__debug.hit(12, true)` ticks the crosshair as one of your own shots landing or killing, both without being shot at. `hud().actions` is what the bar along the bottom is saying: `list` is its key-and-words pairs in order, `shown` how many are up, `changed` how many times the words have moved and `rebinds` how many times a cap was rewritten because the key under it had been rebound, which is the one figure that says a rebind reached the bar from a tab nobody can see. Its `writes` are counted apart from `writes` for the same reason the message line's are: walking up to a vehicle is a write that is meant to happen. `{ prompt: { slots: 3, maxLabel: 22 } }` are the bar's own two numbers (`PROMPT` in `src/ui/promptRules.ts`; `slots` can be lowered but not raised past the cells the bar was built with), and how often a second the state behind it is gathered is `{ wiring: { promptHz: 4 } }`, which lives with the frame loop's other rates because that is where it is read. It also gives the scale and backing store in use, where each block sits, how many part squares the ship's condition has up, what the message line is holding, and which pieces the settings are showing. `overlay.attached` says whether the flight display has the overlay to draw on. Every colour is one line in `src/core/palette.ts` and every size one line in `src/ui/hudMath.ts`. |
| `roster()`, `roster({ standIn: 4 })`, `roster({ showAlone: 1, metreStep: 5 })` | The group's roster down the side of the display: `rows` standing, `over` (members the group has that there was no row for), `writes` in the last full second (0 in a steady second) and `writesNow`, `styled` (whether the block's own rules reached the page), and `lines` — every row as it reads, `Riva (leader) 100% 12 m \| Tem (you) 100% \| Osk 100% Kashyyyk: Rryatt Trail`, which is how a name, a health bar and a distance are checked from a tab nobody is looking at. The right-hand column is one of four things: how far off they are; where they are, named out of the game's own table of worlds, when that is genuinely not this world; one plain word while their browser has gone quiet or nothing has been heard of them, which is not the same thing as having travelled; and nothing at all on your own row. A health nothing carries yet shows an empty bar and a dash. `{ standIn: 4 }` puts a made-up group up so the block can be judged with nobody else connected — its healths reach all three bands at any size, one member stands on another world and one has gone quiet — and holds it there until `{ standIn: 0 }` hands the block back to the real group; `{ showAlone: 1 }` keeps it up while you are the only one in the group. Every other name is one of its invented steps — `barPixels` (how finely a health bar is compared), `metreStep`, `coarseFrom` and `farStep` (a metre close in, 25 m past 200 m), `kmFrom` (where it reads in kilometres), and `top`, `side` and `width` (where the block sits, in pixels; the two offsets take a zero, so it can go flush into the corner) — and a name it does not have is said out loud rather than swallowed |
| `hud().messages`, `hud({ messages: { kept: 5, seconds: 12, merge: 2, steps: 8, maxChars: 140, maxCount: 99, countEvery: 0.25 } })` | The message line bottom left: how many lines are up, how many are fading, and how many were said, merged into one already there and pushed off before their time. Its DOM writes are in `hud().writes` with the rest of the display's — sample that a second apart, and with nothing fading the rate is 0. The second form sets its numbers live: how many lines are kept, how long one stays, the window in which the same words bump a count instead of adding a line, how coarsely the fade is written, where a long line is cut, where the count stops counting and reads "×99+", and how often at most one line's count is written however fast the same words come |
| `hud().onFoot`, `hud().plates`, `hud({ tune: { tickAlpha: 0 } })`, `hud({ plates: { liftPx: 18, pickHz: 4, hold: 1, barStep: 0.05 } })` | The display on foot. `onFoot.keys` is the number row's key-caps as one string, which is how a rebind is checked without squinting, `onFoot.icons` how many of our own glyphs the page holds (31 when `src/ui/hud.svg` has arrived, 0 while it is on its way or if it failed), `onFoot.hands` what each hand is showing, and `onFoot.crosshair` whether one is drawn for the class in play. `plates` is the name and health over whatever the crosshair rests on: what it is showing and its own writes in the last full second, which is a rate and not 0 — a plate that is up writes its place on every frame the head moves on the screen. The second and third forms set the invented numbers live: the crosshair's tick alphas at rest and while aiming (0 is allowed, and takes them off, which is the way to ask whether they are wanted), how far over the head the plate sits in pixels, how many times a second the living are walked for it, how long it is held after you look away, and the smallest change in its bar worth a write. A cooldown's wedge is the page's own and is drawn from `--cool` on the cell in steps of `HUD_TUNE.cooldownSteps`, so a second with a cooldown running in it writes about fifty times and is not meant to read 0 |
| Esc → **Interface**, and `hud({ damageArc: false, damageNumbers: true, nameplate: false, jediCrosshair: false, fullPrompts: true })` | Every switch and number the display has, in one page beside Graphics and Sound: the scale (0.75 to 1.5) and the sharper canvas, what is shown while flying, the message line and how many lines it keeps (3 to 8), the long prompt line, and four switches for a fight — the direction of a blow (on), damage numbers (**off**, because the game said its results in words and those words go to the message line), the name over what you look at (on) and a crosshair as a Jedi (on). Every one of those defaults is ours and invented, and they sit together in `DEFAULT_SETTINGS` in `src/core/settings.ts`; the long prompt line starts **off**, since the action bar now puts your own key on everything it offers, and turning it on is how to tell what the short bar has hidden. The page's two sliders end exactly where `__debug.hud` clamps (`HUD_SCALE_RANGE`, `HUD_LINES_RANGE`), so the page and the console cannot give different answers, and "Reset the display to defaults" puts the lot back. Every colour a panel wears is one of the eighteen declared in `:root` in `src/style.css`, either directly or as a tint of one at a fixed alpha; `tools/swg/tests/hudPage.test.ts` fails if a colour is ever typed into a panel by hand. |
| `hud().feedback`, `hud({ damageArc: false })`, `hud({ damageNumbers: true })`, `hud({ feedback: { numberSeconds: 1.5 } })`, `hurt(180)`, `hit(12, true)` | Damage feedback: the arc on the side a blow came from, the tick on the crosshair when one of your shots tells and the red tick and ring when it kills, and the numbers over heads. `hud().feedback` says how many arcs and numbers are up, what the last frame drew (`ops`), what it wrote to the page in the last full second (`writes`, which is inside the headline `writes` and is 0 in a steady frame) and this second so far (`writesNow`), how long ago your last blow landed and your last kill in seconds of play (`sinceHit`, `sinceKill`; -1 for neither since the world loaded), whether it has the overlay and a projector, and its switches. The arc and the numbers are the Interface page's two settings and are set from here as `hud({ damageArc, damageNumbers })`; the tick is always on, and what it says in words rides with the message line. Every invented number is `{ feedback: { … } }`: `arcAlpha` and `followCamera` (the arc keeps pointing at the blow as you turn; false freezes it where it landed), `numberSeconds`, `numberRise`, `numberMerge` (a second blow on one body inside this is added to the number already rising rather than thrown beside it), `numberSteps` (how finely the fade is written), `numberMin`, `lineEvery` and `lineMin` (a body's damage is said at most this often, and a kill at once), and the tick's and ring's widths and alphas. The shapes' own sizes — the arc's 70 degrees, its three radii and alphas, the tick's reach, the kill ring's two radii, the 1.2 s, 0.12 s and 0.3 s — are in `HUD_SIZES` with the rest of the geometry (`hud({ sizes: { damageSpan: 90 } })`), except the two pool sizes, which are read at boot. `__debug.hurt(deg)` shows the arc at an angle without being shot (0 ahead, 90 the right, 180 behind, 270 the left; `hurt(null)` is a blow with no direction) and `__debug.hit(12, true)` the kill's tick and ring, which is how to judge both from a still room |
| Esc → **Debug**, `debugMenu()`, `debugMenu({ open: true })`, `debugMenu({ pick: 'perf', args: '{ frames: 240 }', run: true })`, `debugMenu({ pick: 'reach', args: '{ objects: 6 }', pin: true, atStart: true })`, `debugMenu({ start: true })`, `debugMenu({ pick: 'race', mode: 'sliders' })`, `debugMenu({ dial: { key: 'tune.looseFall', value: 12 } })`, `debugMenu({ resetDials: true })`, `debugMenu({ pinDials: true })` | The debug menu: every helper on `__debug`, and the window's own knobs (`__sharedDay` and the rest), in one window with this table's words beside each. The knobs -- `race` and `engines` (whose numbers are sliders, below), `npcShips` (whose ladder above tier 5 is sliders too), `reach`, `lod`, `flora`, `god`, `difficulty`, `time`, `day`, `fog`, `bladeGlow`, `torch`, `hover`, `nebulae`, `arm` and `seats` -- are a group of their own at the top, **Tuning** (`TUNING_HELPERS`), so a new one is a line there. What a knob sets is never saved by itself: a pin keeps the call, not its effect, and a pin's **▷** ticks it to run again once the first world is up after a reload (in this browser only, beside the pins), in the order pinned, each waited on in turn and every one of them said on the message line as it runs, so god mode or a server's day is never put back silently. The key left of 1 opens and shuts it (the backquote to begin with; it is **Debug menu** on the Controls page), and so does **Debug** in the Escape menu. The list is read off `__debug` itself each time it opens, so a helper added later is there (under *Other* until it is filed in `DEBUG_GROUPS` in `src/ui/debugModel.ts`), grouped and searchable by its name or by anything its row here says. Picking one shows its rows from this table and its examples; the box is the inside of the call's parentheses, prefilled from the first example and read as JavaScript in the page's own scope (where that example is this table's placeholder, `teleport(x, z)`, Run says the names are yours to fill), and **Run** (or Enter in the box) calls it, waits on a promise with a button that stops waiting (the call itself runs on), and shows the answer folded where it is large — circular references named, three's vectors on one line, a three object as the handful of things worth knowing about it, a Map by its keys, a function by its name — with the console lines it printed while it ran (a table, or a report of several lines like `perf()`'s, keeps its columns and scrolls sideways rather than wrapping); **Copy** puts the answer on the clipboard as JSON. The last thirty calls are kept and re-run in one click, and so are the ones you pin, in this browser only; one is not re-run while another is still being waited on. It does not pause the game: it frees the mouse and the world goes on, so `perf()` and its `ab` runs measure with it open, and while it has the keyboard nothing pressed reaches the player; clicking the world takes the mouse back and shuts it. It may be opened over a panel or the map, and shutting it leaves the mouse with them. Nothing of it runs in a frame, open or shut. `debugMenu()` says what it holds: the `helpers` it lists and in which `groups`, the ones filed under `other`, the helpers with no row in this table (`undocumented`, which a node test keeps empty) and the names this table has that `__debug` does not have now (`stale`: a few are hung only once their part of the game has run, the map's the first time it opens and the nebulae's in space), what is pinned and kept, which pins run at start (`atStart`) and what the last run at start came to (`ranAtStart`), and the last call; `{ open }` opens or shuts it, `{ pick }` picks a helper, `{ args }` fills the box and `{ run: true }` presses Run, which is how a driven tab works it; `{ pin }` pins the call in the box (or unpins it with false), `{ atStart }` ticks that pin to run at start (or clears it), and `{ start: true }` runs every ticked pin now, as the first world after a reload does. **Sliders**: a helper whose numbers are a matter of feel has dials (`src/ui/dials.ts`; today `race` and `engines`, `src/vehicles/raceDials.ts`, and `npcShips`, `src/space/tierDials.ts`), and picked it shows them as sliders in place of the box, in groups, each with a box to type a number past the slider's ends (as far as the helper itself allows), its unit, a line saying what it does and a ↺ that puts it back where the game shipped it; **Console** beside them gives the box back. A slider sets its number through the helper's own setter as it moves, so a slider and a typed call never mean two things, and it sets it in memory only: the bar over the sliders counts what has moved and writes the console line that sets it all again, **Copy** puts that line on the clipboard (paste it to keep the numbers in the code), **Pin at start** pins it and ticks it to run at start, and **Reset all** puts every dial back. Opening the window reads the numbers again, so one moved from the console meanwhile shows. With sliders picked, `{ mode: 'sliders' }` or `'console'` switches, `{ dial: { key, value } }` moves one as its slider would, on its step, and `{ resetDials: true }` and `{ pinDials: true }` are the bar's buttons; the report's `dials` names what has moved and the line |
| `windows()`, `windows({ set: { id: 'map', x: 40, y: 30, w: 900, h: 600 } })`, `windows({ reset: true })`, `windows({ reset: 'map' })` | Every window that moves and sizes (`src/ui/drag.ts`): its box, its minimum (`WINDOW_MIN` in `src/ui/windowBox.ts`), what storage holds for it, whether it is wholly on the screen and whether anything in it would scroll sideways, with the browser's own size and pixel ratio. `{ set }` moves and sizes one as a drag would (with no `w` and `h` it only moves it), `{ reset: true }` forgets every window's place and size and `{ reset: 'map' }` one window's |
| `select()` | The character select screen (`src/ui/characterSelect.ts`): its slots and which is chosen, the figure standing and whose it is, the loads started and dropped, every box's size, and `stretch`, which must read 1.000 in a visible window |
| `teleportSwg(x, z)`, `animTex()`, `animTex('mission')`, `animTex({ freeze: true, speed: 4 })` | Move to a point in the original game's coordinates (the inverse of `swg()`), which on a planet are the pair `/loc`, the minimap and the map window read out, so a `/loc` line pastes straight in; the animated surfaces: flip-book screens (frame, mode, seconds) and scrolling falls (rate, offset) whose material name holds the string, the counts, uploads still pending and the renderer's program count; freeze or speed up their clock |
| `near(30)`, `find('name')`, `passes()`, `passLog()`, `drawCalls()`, `interiors()` | What is placed around you (`near` answers each one's key, tier, region state and `colliders`, how many it has this instant: 0 for anything the collider sweep has not made solid, or never will), what the portal renderer drew this frame and what it cost |
| `buildings('cantina')`, `await enter('cantina')`, `await enter()`, `doorless()`, `gates()`, `gates({ reach: 20 })`, `await show('weapons/eqp_ent_baton_mustafar_s01.glb')`, `gallery()`, `gallery(10)` | Getting at the buildings and the models. `buildings(match, limit)` is every building this world places whose model or template holds the text, nearest first with how far off each is — the whole world's list, not what has streamed in; `enter(match)` goes into the nearest such building (walking you over first when it is far) and puts you in the room a doorway opens into, which is the call E makes beside a building with no way in on foot, with that rule taken off; `doorless()` lists the buildings round you and whether each can be walked into. `gates()` is the gates a world's zones are walked between: what the pack carries, which one is in reach and what the key would do there, with `{ reach, calm }` moving its two numbers (`GATE_TUNE` in `zoneGates.ts`). `show(file, clip)` stands a converted model from under `assets-private/` beside you, playing the named clip or its first, and says its clips, joints, size and place. `gallery()` is the gallery world's mannequins (how many are up and the animation slot nearest you), and `gallery(10)` widens the range they wake in |
| `late()` | What each menu holds from SOE's final files (publishes 55 to 59, read through the names list the converter ships; `src/ui/lateReport.ts`): every pack the game has already fetched with its late stamp (`applied` when the list was in the mount, `final`, `none`, `refused`, or `before` for a pack converted before the list, which `convert` asks for again); each menu's late thing asked of that menu's own function over what it was fetched with -- the Clothes view lists the infiltrator chest plate and the Life Day wings, the crafting headband and the Dathomir bracelet under Back, Head and Left bracer by their slots, a Wookiee man's hair grid has his own group, the rack lists the Dathomir scythe, the garage lists the single-pod airspeeder's three templates, the two walker templates and the four new mounts, the garage holds the T-wing and the NPC tab an X-wing at tier 10 (false where the ships pack has no `combat.json`, null while it has not landed), the NPC tab holds the Dug ready, the props view lists the Dathomir paintings, the Housing tab offers the YT-1300 house, the emote list holds the undead emote and dances 33 to 36 with every dance labelled (`shows` true or false, or null while its pack has not been fetched: open that menu once); `missing`, the menus whose own pack says the late files were in the mount and still do not show them, which should be empty; and `line`, what the converter's `status` would print as far as these packs can say it. It fetches nothing. `npm run test:converter`'s `lateMenus.test.ts` asks the very same of the packs on disk |
| `doors()`, `doors({ list: true })`, `doors({ list: 60 })`, `doors({ hold: 'open' })`, `doors({ hold: 'shut' })`, `doors({ hold: 'auto' })`, `doors({ tune: { release: 0.5, linger: 0.3, spring: 5, minDepth: 0.12, reach: 1, speed: 1 } })`, `doors({ tune: { solid: false } })`, `doors({ tune: { shadows: true } })`, `doors({ tune: { on: false } })` | The doors in the buildings' doorways (`src/world/doors.ts`, the converter's `doors` pass): how many stand in the buildings whose rooms are built, how many are `ready` (their programs built and shown), `open` and `moving`, the kinematic `bodies` their leaves hang on, how many bodies were told this step (`openers`), the sounds played, any opening the locks' seam refused (`refused`: a door into one of a corvette copy's locked rooms, until its keypad or panel is used; `instances()` names them), what became of the shared door models (`models`, with `missingModels`), and the door `nearest` the player: its building, portal, style, the cells on its two sides, whether it is an `exit`, how `open` it is (0 to 1), its trigger radius, open and close `times`, its slide, its sounds and how far off it is. `list` gives every door within 30 m (or that many metres). `hold` holds the nearest door open or shut whoever is near, and `'auto'` gives it back to the bodies near it. `tune` moves `DOORS_TUNE` live, every number of it ours: `release` the metres past its radius an open door stays open by, `linger` the seconds it waits with nobody near before it shuts, `spring` how hard a style's springiness bends the end of a move (0 eases every door alike), `minDepth` the least half thickness of a leaf's box, `reach` and `speed` multiply every style's radius and times (the radius alone, never how far a door stands from its building's middle), `solid: false` takes every leaf's box out of the way (switched, never removed), `shadows` lets the doors on exits cast, `sound` silences them, and `on: false` stops every door where it stands. A world converted before the pass has no `doors.json` and its doorways stand open as before; `npm run swg -- doors @SWG assets-private --retail-only` writes them in seconds |
| `instances()`, `await instances({ go: 'stardestroyer' })`, `await instances({ go: 'exarkun' })`, `await instances({ leave: true })`, `instances({ unlock: true })`, `instances({ tune: { useReach: 2.5, exitReach: 6, thingReach: 4, takerStep: 3 } })` | The dungeons the game stood a copy of for each group (`src/world/instances.ts`): the copy the player is in (`here`: which dungeon, where the copy stands, the arrival room and spot, the rooms still locked and every fitting of it that does anything when used, with where each stands and how far off it is), what E would do where the player stands (`use`: `keypad`, `pod`, `out`, `in` or nothing), the way back that is kept (`wayBack`: the world and spot, or the ship left docked), how many copies of each dungeon this world holds, and this world's ways in with how many of each it places and where the nearest one stands and how far off (`ours` where the link is ours). A player who comes out in the world of copies standing in none of them (a character saved between copies) is sent back the way they came at once, or by the dungeon's own way in. `go` goes into a dungeon by its kind (`corvette_rebel`, `corvette_imperial`, `corvette_neutral`, `stardestroyer`, `exarkun`, `axkva`, `ig88`, `meatlump`, `myyydril`, `avatar`, `uplink`, `crystal`, `sherkar`, `republic`, `decrepit`, `working`), to the copy the group's id (or the character's) picks, with where the player stands now as the way back, and the ship they fly or are docked in, which is stood there again on the way out; `leave` goes back out as E at the way out does; `unlock` opens every locked room of the copy (for this browser alone: a keypad's room opened at the keypad is also sent to the group, `unlock` on the relay, which must be restarted for it); `tune` moves `INSTANCE_TUNE`, every number of it ours. The ways in in play: a corvette's ticket taker's own answer, **Go aboard** in the ship menu docked at the Deep Space Star Destroyer or Kashyyyk orbit's Avatar platform station, and E at each dungeon's way in out in the worlds (the Exar Kun entrance on Yavin 4, the meatlump hideout's ladder under Coronet, the Myyydril cave door in the dead forest, Mustafar's dungeon doors, and ours for Axkva Min on Dathomir and IG-88 on Lok). Needs `dungeon1` converted (`snapshot`, `sky`, `water`, `doors`, `fittings`, `spawns`) |
| `await corvette()`, `await corvette({ go: 'rebel' })`, `await corvette({ go: 'imperial' })`, `await corvette({ go: 'neutral' })` | The Corellian corvette, as its ticket taker sends a player: `go` names the faction whose run it is (the Alliance's against an Imperial-held ship, the Empire's against a Rebel blockade runner, the Hutts' against CorSec) and goes aboard that faction's copy, back to where the player stands now; the answer and the call with nothing are what `instances()` says. A copy's crew is the emulator's own (`readCorvette`), stood only in the copy the player is in; seven rooms stay locked until a keypad or panel opens them, the escape pods' consoles and the airlock are the way off, and its lifts are the lift menu |
| `fountains()`, `fountains({ color: '#5d7a70', opacity: 0.45, ripple: 0.8, drift: 0.5, spray: 0.45 })` | The water standing in fountains' and pools' basins and the fountains' own spray: the basins' colour, how much of the floor they hide and how their fine ripples move, and the share of its alpha a fountain's spray is drawn at. Every basin standing takes a change at once; nothing compiles, and every number is ours |
| `ribbons()`, `ribbons({ width: 1.5, length: 2, tailFade: 0.3 })` | The ribbons the entertainers' sticks and the glow sticks trail: `width` and `length` multiply what each ribbon's file asks, `tailFade` is the share of the length that fades out (0, solid to the end, as the files colour them), and `rate`, `samples` and `subdivisions` are for the files that say none of it; `status` says how many are playing |
| `cloning()`, `cloning(0)` | Every facility on this world where the dead come back, nearest first with the distance from where you stand and the name of the place each stands in — the whole list, where the card shows its first few — and with a row number, come back at that one now without dying |
| `capture('theed-terrace')`, `await goToShot('tyrena')`, `captureShip('tyrena')`, `await place('tyrena')`, `await place(null)`, `await place()`, `placeView({ zoom: 0.7, pan: 0.3, spin: 3.14 })`, `placeClock()`, `placeClock({ hour: 21.5 })`, `placeClock({ play: true })` | The places the creator and the select screen stand a character in (`src/data/scenes.ts`). `capture(name)` writes down where you stand, the camera, the hour and a ship parked in view, as one line of JSON to paste back; `goToShot(key)` travels to a captured place's world and stands you on its spot with the view aimed as the shot was framed (no key lists them, world by world), and `captureShip(key)` prints the line for `SCENE_SHIPS` for the ship parked in front of that shot's own camera, with where it would sit on the screen. `place(key)` stands the character in a baked place in three dimensions with the world's own sun, sky and weather (after `npm run swg -- scenes assets-private`), `place(null)` comes out and `place()` lists what is built; `placeView` moves the creator's camera while a place is up. `placeClock` is the creator's hour slider from the console: a place opens with the day stopped on its captured hour (`DayCycle.paused`, cleared when the place goes and on every arrival), `{ hour: 21.5 }` stops it at half past nine at night as a drag does and takes the reflections again at once, `{ play: true }` lets it run at the day's own rate and `{ play: false }` stops it, and `{ tune: { stepMinutes, settleMs } }` moves `PLACE_CLOCK_TUNE` (the slider's step, five minutes, and how long a drag must rest before the reflections are retaken, 250 ms). It reports the clock, whether the day is stopped and `programs`, the renderer's live program count, which should not rise across a first scrub into the night |
| `gloss()`, `gloss(0)`, `gloss(2)`, `reflections()`, `reflections({ source: 'game' })`, `reflections({ source: 'sky' })`, `reflections({ plain: 1 })`, `reflections({ plain: 0 })`, `reflections({ models: false })`, `reflections({ models: true })`, `reflections({ preview: 0.5 })`, `reflections({ capture: true })`, `reflections({ dome: 'scene' })`, `reflections({ dome: 'kept' })` | The ground's own gloss, out of the client's specular masks: with no number, how many families brought one; `0` is the matt ground of before and `2` twice the shine, a uniform the material already holds, so nothing recompiles. `reflections()` says where shiny surfaces and the water take their reflection from: our own sky dome by default, captured every few seconds, or with `{ source: 'game' }` the planets' own cube maps out of the client's files; the choice is kept in this browser (`localStorage['swg.reflections']`) and takes effect at once. `plain` is how much of it a surface whose shader has no reflection cube takes (material format 6's `swgCube: false`, `plainRegistered` of the registered materials): nought by default, which takes away both the faint mirror and the sky-coloured fill three's environment adds to that material's own diffuse light, neither of which the client drew; `{ plain: 1 }` puts both back to compare, indoors as out (a room's own copy of a wall is registered with its original's strength), a uniform, so nothing compiles, and it is not remembered. `models` is whether what is worn, held, flown and ridden and the creatures reflect where their shader has a cube (`modelsRegistered` of them, joined as the world adopted them): `{ models: false }` writes every one at nought, which takes away the reflection and its fill and keeps the program, so the look before they had one is compared live and nothing compiles; `localStorage['swg.reflections.models'] = '0'` keeps them out of the registry altogether at the next load (`modelsAtLoad`), the way back if anything about it goes wrong. `preview` is how much of their own studio the wardrobe's doll, the select screen, the creator and the ship edit page give those same materials (ours, 0.5, what the ship edit page's room was always given), read at every draw; those previews swap it on around their own draws and put the world's back after, and each says how many materials its last draw gave it (`reflecting` in `select()`, `preview()` and `shipEdit()`). `standIn` says the world has no environment of its own just now and every registered material carries the black stand-in, which keeps their programs as they are between worlds. `capture` is the sky's own capture (`DomeCapture`, `src/world/envmap.ts`, the performance pass's fifth wave): the dome is rendered into one kept 128 px cube and filtered into one kept target in place, through a generator of its own, so `targetsMade` stays 1 for the session however many `captures` there are, `height` is the kept target's (512, the one in every reflective program's key), `refused` counts cubes of another size it would not filter, and `held` says the surfaces hold its texture now (with `{ source: 'game' }` on an area with a cube of its own they hold that cube's, filtered through the shared generator at its own size). `{ capture: true }` takes the sky again on the next frame rather than when its four-second clock comes round. The capture takes none of `fromScene`'s 0.04 rad blur, so the mirror-sharp level of the sky's reflection is a few texels sharper than before it; `{ dome: 'scene' }` captures the way before (a target made every capture, with the blur) to compare the look by, and `{ dome: 'kept' }` goes back, neither remembered and nothing compiled either way |
| `spec()`, `spec({ use: 'three' })`, `spec({ use: 'client' })`, `spec({ gain: 1, cap: 1, film: 1, gate: 0 })`, `specSelfTest()` | The client's own highlight (`src/world/specular.ts`, `SPEC_TUNE`), drawn in place of three's GGX on every material converted at format 6: the specular colour times the mask (the metal-rough image's red) times (N·H) to the power, or on hair, fur and the two-tone cloth the client's band of N·H times N·L. `spec()` says whether the wrap is on, the knobs, and how many materials draw no highlight (`none`, the leaves and every shader whose program has no specular), the plain one (`phong`) or the band (`aniso`), with `missingAnchor` (a shader three handed us without the line it replaces, which keeps three's own; it should be 0). `{ use: 'three' }` goes back to three's GGX on all of them and `{ use: 'client' }` returns; `gain` is the client's strength at 1, `cap` holds the term under a white surface facing the light times that (ours, standing in for the client's clamp to white: glass's colour of 10 would bloom past it), `film` how much of three's GGX a wet or snowed surface keeps by the largest of how wet it is, its puddle and its snow (the rain's glint, the snow's matt), `gate` 1 takes the highlight off faces turned from the light, which the client never did (0). All uniforms: nothing compiles. `localStorage['swg.spec'] = 'three'` leaves the wrap off at the next load, the way back if a program will not compile. `specSelfTest()` draws a lit black sphere off-screen with the injected program (the plain highlight, the band, a colour past the cap, a mask, none) and compares every pixel with `src/world/specMath.ts`, which the node test sweeps; then the switch to GGX against an unwrapped standard material, which must match it and build no program (`threeSwitch`), and the film under rain, a puddle and snow through a stand-in for the wet chunk (`weather`): `ok` false means the shader and the arithmetic have parted; it builds four programs of its own, so run it after checking `shaders().builtInPlay` |

`names` a flora's cut-outs met while neither had them), what it switched this session, how many glass cards it made leaves and colours it dropped, each card's class and where it came from (`classFrom`: the GLB's, the pack's table, or the name's suffix while a pack carries neither), what the mirror did (sheets turned, vertices and megabytes added, milliseconds) and `missingAnchor`, which should be 0. `{ backs: 'stored' }` lights every card by the client's one normal again (today's dead face), `{ mipScale: 0 }` shows far crowns thinning as they did, `{ wetGloss: 1 }` the gloss in rain; all uniforms. `{ rule: 'three' }` puts the converter's double-sided materials back and `{ rule: 'card' }` the rule again, recompiling every card (a stall, which it says). `localStorage['swg.cards'] = 'three'` skips the rule at the next load and `localStorage['swg.cardBacks'] = '0'` the mirror alone. `cardsSelfTest()` draws a back-to-back pair on one set of vertices, a lone card and its shadow off-screen: `ok` false means a card is drawn from the wrong side, casts nothing, or the mirror has not turned a face |
| `cardLight()`, `cardLight({ curve: 'scene' })`, `cardLight({ curve: 'client' })`, `cardLight({ on: false })`, `cardLight({ sunSide: 'stored' })`, `cardLight({ sunSide: 'face' })`, `cardLight({ pointFlat: 0 })`, `cardLight({ pointFlat: 1 })`, `cardLight({ fills: 'none' })`, `cardLight({ shadow: 'all' })`, `cardLight({ shadow: 'none' })`, `cardLight({ shadowLook: 'row7' })`, `cardLight({ rampScale: 'alpha' })`, `cardLightSelfTest()`, `leafShadowSelfTest()` | The leaf cards lit by the client's own light (`src/world/cardLight.ts`, `CARD_LIGHT_TUNE`, `src/world/cardLightMath.ts` its mirror): the front, tangent and back colours of the ramp about the sun's axis, the two plain parallel lights and the emissive inside one saturate, times the texel, carried back through our tone curve so a card shows the client's displayed bytes (`curve: 'client'`; `'scene'` keeps our curve), and no shadow on a leaf from its own tree (`shadow: 'self'`, the owner's; `'all'` every shadow as before, `'none'` no shadow on the cards of a tree that casts). `cardLight()` says the colours in bytes and the shadow row's alpha, the sun, the fill's direction, the cards given the light and any left with three's for a missing anchor (which should be none), and how many card meshes do not receive. `sunSide: 'face'` lights each face by the sun on its own side, `'stored'` both faces by the client's one normal as the original did; `pointFlat` is how much the torch and our other lights take a leaf's own face (0.5); `shadowLook: 'row7'` blends a shaded leaf toward ramp row 7 instead of dropping it to the tangent colour. | `sway()`, `sway({ on: false })`, `sway({ on: true })`, `sway({ trunk: 'bend' })`, `sway({ wind: 1 })`, `sway({ wind: 'weather' })`, `sway({ freeze: true })`, `sway({ shadow: false })`, `sway({ clock: 'sim' })`, `sway({ fadeFar: 150 })`, `sway({ model: 'ever_eucalyptus_med' })`, `swaySelfTest()` | The flora's sway (`src/world/floraSway.ts`, `SWAY_TUNE`, all ours; `src/world/swayMath.ts` the chunk and its mirror): one bend over every primitive of a swaying model from its own canopy base up, so the trunk below the crown stands still and no leaf leaves its branch, a flutter off the trunk, downwind with the weather's wind and a calm floor of its own, its shadow swaying with it through a depth material warmed behind the loading screen. `sway()` says how many models sway of those weighed, the materials wrapped and the depth materials made, the programs (colour and depth, and `depthWarm`: the stand-ins the loading screen drew into the shadow maps -- the scene's own combinations and the twelve every swaying material can cast with -- and the programs that built), the clock, the wind's heading, strength and its two factors, the eye and the largest excursion; any setting changes live (`shadow` hangs or takes off the depth materials); `{ trunk: 'bend' }` bends whole trees from the ground to compare, `{ wind: 1 }` holds a storm's strength for looking and `{ wind: 'weather' }` gives it back, `{ clock: 'sim' }` stops it with the creatures under an open panel (the default runs with the water). `{ model }` prints one model's height, canopy base, trunk axis and radius and periods. `localStorage['swg.sway'] = '0'` skips the wrap at the next load. `swaySelfTest()` renders a probe built from the same chunk into a float target and holds it to `swayOffset` to a millimetre |

Any setting changes at once (uniforms) or builds the window again (the knobs that place: `perTile`, `jitter`, `jitterMin`, `water`, `density`, the sway's periods and stiff names); `on`, `reachScale` and `density` are the Graphics page's own settings (Grass, Grass distance, Grass density), written into them in memory and never saved, the page drawn again if it shows (past the page's range a value is in force now and the page keeps its own, which the answer's `notes` say); `reach` in metres stands over the page's distance and `reach: null` hands it back to the file's own near tiling's end times the page's scale; the window of chunks built round the eye follows the reach in force (three at the worlds' own, `window` in the answer) unless `window` holds one, which `window: null` hands back; the answer's `settings` and `overrides` say which is which, and moving a setting a console value stands over says so on the message line. `perTile: false` is the old one roll a pole, `jitter: 0` stands every tuft on its tile's middle, `tint: 'ground'` (the default, the owner's) follows the ground's own colour rule and `tint: 'colourMap'` is the client's raw colour map, `water: 'keep'` plants under the drawn water as well; `casting` counts the chunks casting now (`floraShadows` has the shadows). `{ roads: false }` stops roads and rivers clearing the flora (`TERRAIN_GROUND_TUNE.roadsClearFlora`), which the terrain's workers take at the next world load. `localStorage['swg.grass'] = '0'` keeps it off from the next load |
