Where a short Tree distance hides a chunk's trees the cards over it are shown, lifted onto the coarse height (the far tile they were dropped for is cut away there), so no bare ring opens between the trees and the cards; they follow the trees within one plant sweep. `farCards()` says whether they are on and their escape hatch, the far tiles with cards built and drawn, the cards, those hidden over a near chunk, those shown over one (`overChunks`, inside the detailed ground), those shown in reach, the tiles casting, what the placing dropped and the research's own count for this world (the research counted the far tiling's whole ring, ours starts where our trees stop); any setting changes live, `on` and `reachScale` are the Graphics page's own (Far tree cards, Far tree card distance), written in memory and never saved, `reach` in metres stands over the page's and `reach: null` is the far tiling's own end times its scale, `perFrame` is how many far tiles are built a frame. `localStorage['swg.farCards'] = '0'` keeps them off from the next load |

Trees cast (the page's Trees cast shadows), small plants do not (Small plants cast shadows, and at their finest level only while `plantsFinest`), the line between them `floraMinRadius` (1.2 m, a model's radius times its planting's scale), and a placed piece that is flora follows the same switches -- every piece of a model its pack files as flora, and otherwise a piece that is itself a leaf card, so a crystal rock's or a village tree's solid pieces stay on the slider; the page's Smallest shadow caster governs every other placed thing, what players put down included. The grass takes the sun's shadow by reading the cascades itself, one lookup a fragment, by the ambient occlusion's own arithmetic (its pick, its far fade, its bounds), each lookup lifted toward the sun by `lift` of the tuft's larger side so a tuft never shades itself, and its front light falls toward the tangent as a shaded leaf's does; it casts (off) through one depth material of the session, near you only, into each cascade whose near edge its reach reaches (`castCascades` at most), facing the eye as the drawn quad does (`castFacing: 'sun'` to compare), cut at `castCut`. `floraShadows()` says the tunes, the page's settings, the console values standing over them, what casts and takes the shadow by kind, the cascades the grass casts into now (`grassCascades`), the depth warm-up's report (`depthWarm`: the sway's stand-ins, the plain combinations a light set, whether the grass's was drawn, the programs) and the grass's programs (one `grass` and one `grass depth`). `trees`, `plants`, `grassCast`, `grassReceive`, `farCast` and `leaves` are the page's own settings, written in memory and never saved; the rest move their tunes live. Nothing compiles. `grassShadowSelfTest()` draws the grass's own program, its fragment stage writing the shade it reads, into a float target against stand-in maps of known depth, each cascade in turn the only one able to shade, and holds the shade to the mirror at each cascade's boundaries on both sides, through the last cascade's fade and with the lift and the bias on and off, then runs a real shadow pass off-screen (a tuft under a box darker only while receiving, one behind a casting tuft darker only while casting); it builds a few programs of its own, so read `shaders().builtInPlay` first. The frame report's `grassCast`, `grassReceive`, `plantsCast` and `farCardsByTrees` (the far cards hidden by the trees or by the chunk, the same at the default Tree distance) flip each live for `perf({ ab })` |
| `ground()`, `ground({ at: true })`, `ground({ colour: 'client' })`, `ground({ colour: 'normalised' })`, `ground({ colour: 'channels' })`, `ground({ colour: 'off' })`, `ground({ gainCap: 1.6 })`, `ground({ shine: 'flat' })`, `ground({ shine: 'highlight' })`, `ground({ shine: 'off' })`, `ground({ flat: 1 })`, `ground({ mirror: false })`, `ground({ masks: false })`, `ground({ opposite: 'add' })`, `ground({ order: 'last' })`, `ground({ children: false })`, `ground({ childCorner: ['ne', 'nw', 'se', 'sw'] })`, `ground({ clouds: true })`, `ground({ clouds: { on: true, strength: 1 } })`, `ground({ view: 'colour' })`, `ground({ view: 'bump' })`, `groundSelfTest()` | How the ground is drawn and what the terrain generator lays on it. Under `drawn`: its program (`tiles`, the client's way, which a pack at terrain shaders version 4 is drawn with, or the families of before, for a pack from before or `localStorage['swg.ground.tiles'] = '0'`), the knobs (`GROUND_DRAW_TUNE` in `src/world/groundTiles.ts`), its pictures and how many alternates each family brought (`localStorage['swg.ground.children'] = '1'` loads the first only), the share of the near ground's 2 m cells whose tile holds one, two, three or four families, the world's colour reference, what its pictures hold on the card in megabytes, and the shift that puts its mesh on the client's height points. `colour` is the colour map's tint: `'normalised'` (ours, the default) the world's median brightness taken to today's with the hue kept, `'client'` the byte as it stands in the client's gamma, `'channels'` each channel to its own median, `'off'` none; `gainCap` holds every pole under that brightening (ours, 1.6). `shine` is `'highlight'` (the gloss mask lowers the roughness, as before), `'flat'` (the client's specular power of nought read as a flat add of the gloss under each light, at `flat` times its strength) or `'off'`; a family whose shader's specular colour is black takes none either way. `mirror: false` draws the pictures the way round they were drawn before (along the game's x, mirrored against the terrain's), `masks: false` steps every border at a half to compare with the client's painted masks, `opposite: 'add'` takes two opposite corners' quarters summed rather than the stronger, `clouds` the client's cloud shadows over the lit ground (built and off until looked at; strength 1 is the client's, grey 0.2 laid over the shown colour at the tile's alpha squared, drifting 1.4 m a second), `view: 'colour'` (or `'clouds'`, or `'lit'` back) shows the ground's colour before the light, and `view: 'bump'` the lean its blended bump gives as a colour (before the rain's rings). All of those are uniforms of the one ground material every planet shares: nothing compiles. `order: 'last'` (the other end of the family list at the bottom of a tile), `children: false` (every layer its family's first child) and `childCorner` (which corner's choice a layer takes, the client's SW, SE, NW, NE by default, ours) change what each chunk is built with, so they build the ground round the player again at once, chunks and far tiles, about two thirds of a second. With nothing else, the rules in force: the families laid on the client's 8 m pattern (each pole taking the family of the 8 m chunk corner nearest it, a tie going to the corner above, as the engine does at the end of every chunk and before any layer that reads the family map), each pole's own choice among its family's alternates drawn from its place (`hash`: the engine's rule on Mustafar, ours on every world whose file says legacy), the colour affectors (constants, fractal ramps, height ramps) and how many of the colour ramps they name the pack carried (`terrain/colorramps.json`, which the terrain command writes from terrain shaders version 4 on; a ramp the pack lacks does nothing, as in the client, so on a pack from before it the colour map is the constants' alone). `{ at: true }` adds the pole under the player's feet, worked out afresh on this thread (its block twice, 15 to 40 ms): its family as laid on the pattern and as it was painted, its choice byte and the alternate it picks, its colour, and every colour affector that wrote there, in order (the colour it asked for, how strongly, the colour before and after), beside what the cached blocks the game reads hold there. `groundSelfTest()` draws a strip of tiles off-screen with the ground's own tile program -- every mask's corners and turns, three and four layers, the four tints with every other tile bright enough that the gain cap binds (`capBinds`), both ways round, both opposite rules, the hard step, the cloud layer over a tile that varies both ways at a clock several periods on (its place and its drift), the lean the blended bump gives and its gloss, and the flat shine under one light -- and holds every pixel to `src/world/groundMath.ts`, which the node test sweeps: `ok` false means the program and the arithmetic have parted, and `builtOnKnobs` must be 0; it builds two programs of its own (no light, and one light) |
| `dem()`, `dem({ url: '/dem/monument', size: 4096, lift: -1580, feather: 300 })`, `dem({ centreX: 0, centreZ: 0 })`, `dem(null)` | Real-world ground (`src/world/demTerrain.ts`): a patch converted from an elevation raster by `tools/demconverter/dem2swg.py` (`base16.raw` and `base.json`, served from `public/dem/<name>/`) laid over this planet, the mesh and the collider rebuilt from the one array. `dem()` reports what is laid (its centre, size, lift, feather, samples, metres a sample and the ground's range), a `url` lays a patch, no `url` nudges the one laid so it can be walked into place, and `null` takes it away. A planet whose `PlanetDef.dem` names a patch lays it as it loads (Granyon, Rainier, Monument and Helensar); one whose files are missing keeps the planet's own noise and says so in the console |
| `groundRules()`, `groundRules({ all: 'rock_lok_whitecliff' })`, `groundRules({ rules: [], fallback: 'dirt_redsandy' })`, `groundRules({ reset: true })` | The ground rules of a real-elevation world (`src/world/demShader.ts`): which of a borrowed pack's families paint where, by elevation above sea level, slope and hollow. `groundRules()` reports the pack, the rules, the families they use and the ones they could; `{ all }` floods one family everywhere to see it underfoot, `{ rules, fallback }` swaps the whole set, in the shape `GroundRules` takes, and `{ reset: true }` puts the planet's own back. The ground is rebuilt each time, so a set is worked out in seconds rather than reloads |
| `slopes()`, `slopes({ radius: 4000 })`, `slopes({ at: [0, 0] })` | What the ground rules really see across this world, sampled on a grid (nine tenths of the patch by default): the spread of elevation, slope and hollow, and cuts taken from this ground for flat, steep, very steep, a gully and a ridge. A resampled survey flattens every wall, so thresholds guessed from the real place rarely fire; these are the ones that do |
| `families('lok')`, `families('lok', 'rock')` | The ground families a converted pack paints with (its terrain's shader table and nothing else: no heights, no buildings), each with its id, its name and its alternates, filtered by a word: the names a real-elevation world's `ground` rules are written against |
| `shadows(distance, minRadius)`, `shadows(240, 2)`, `shadows(1200)`, `shadowLook(radius, intensity)`, `ambient(row)`, `normals(1, -1)` | Retune shadows, ambient light and normal maps live. `shadows` moves the Graphics page's Shadow distance and Smallest shadow caster: inside the page's ranges (80 to 800 m, 0.5 to 8 m) they are written into its settings in memory and never saved, so the page shows them; past them (`shadows(1200)`) they are in force now and the page keeps its own, which the answer's `notes` say. The smallest caster works the casters' rule out again over every placed copy standing, what players put down included (`shadowCasters.ts`); the counts are of what is drawn. `normals` reaches the ground's own bump maps too, through its first number: at 0 the ground is as flat as it was before it had them (along with everything else in the world), at 2 its relief is doubled |

| `creator()`, `creator({ linkSelf: false })`, `creator({ untabledGroup: false })`, `creator({ fallbackColumns: 8, moreColumns: 16 })` | The creator's own table (`characters/customization.json`, `src/ui/creatorModel.ts`) and how the appearance page is laid out from it for the player's character: `loaded` (`not yet`, true, or false when the `customization` command has not been run, and then the page lays itself out from the packs as it always did), `laidOut` (`table` or `packs`), `hairNone` (`offer`, always true: the grid of styles offers no hair to every species, the owner's call; and `game`, what the game's own table said of this one: `may` go bald, `may not` (a Twi'lek, a Zabrak, a Trandoshan) or `says nothing`), each group with its row count, the rows that found nothing on this character (`unresolved`), what is hidden and why (a palette of one colour, a garment's own fit morph), the rows that follow another unseen (`followers`), the colours that set others (`links`), and `lastPick`, every key the last pick wrote. `linkSelf` false stops a colour setting the ones the table links to it (a female's skin her lips and eye shadow); `untabledGroup` false hides the group of ours at the bottom; the columns are for the swatches past the creation block and for a palette with no columns of its own. Every knob is ours |
| `hair()`, `await hair('hair_human_female_s04')`, `await hair(null)` | The hairstyles' grid on the appearance page and in the creator (`src/ui/hairGrid.ts`) for the player's character: what is worn, `fromTable` (laid out from the creator's table, or the one list of before), whether a cell for no hair leads (`none`, for every species with styles, the owner's call), `gameBald` (what the game's own table said of this species: `may`, `may not` or `says nothing`) and `keptFromBald` (every species the table kept from going bald, which is offered no hair all the same), each group (this gender's in the game's order, the other gender's, and what the game never offered) with its cells, how many have their picture and which is worn, `startsIn` (the style a new character of the species is put in, for one that may not go bald), the remembered hair colours (`hair\|index_color_1` and the like, which the next style takes) and `last`, the last change with the colours it carried. | `grapple()`, `grapple({ fire: true })`, `grapple({ fire: [x, y, z] })`, `grapple({ letGo: true })`, `grapple({ everyone: false })`, `grapple({ tune: { gravity: 0.6 } })`, `grapple({ reset: true })`, `grapple({ slam: 30 })`, `grapple({ stagger: true })`, `grapple({ tune: { daze: 0.9 } })`, `grapple({ fire: 'prop' })`, `grapple({ tune: { strength: 10 } })`, `grapple({ fire: 'vehicle' })`, `grapple({ drive: { throttle: 1, steer: 0, seconds: 8 } })`, `grapple({ drive: false })`, `grapple({ tune: { parkHold: 0 } })`, `grapple({ tune: { hullTest: false } })`, `grapple({ tune: { peerPose: 'picture' } })`, `grapple({ tune: { peerLead: 0.3 } })`, `grapple({ tune: { sendPoints: 4 } })` | The grappling hook. From wave 4 it goes to other players and you see theirs: `sent` is what the last state carried of your rope (`gr`: `[0, x, y, z, vx, vy, vz]` the dart flying, or `[1, slack, anchor, bends...]` a rope held, every point a world point, cut to `sendPoints` -- at most the wire's 16 -- at the anchor's end; `grf`: `[1, their relay id, index, x, y, z]` for each point riding another player's vehicle, in that vehicle's own frame; their `bytes`, and whether the line is `online`; `gr` is null with no rope out or while hidden in a jump); `rig` is whether the character owns a grapple rig, which `everyone: false` makes firing ask for (refused in words, "You carry no grapple rig."); `peers` is every other player's rope as this browser draws it (`ropes`: each one's `kind`, `dart` or `rope`, its `points` and `slack`, whether it is `shown` and `drawnHere`, the arm's eased `armWeight`, the `anchor` as drawn, and each point on a vehicle -- `vehicleOf` their relay id, its `index`, whether that vehicle is `shown` here and so the point put on it, `drawn` where it is drawn and `sent` the world point their game sent; `made`, the ribbons ever built for peers, which climbs when somebody first ropes and then stands still, since a peer who leaves hands theirs on, `spare`, and `peerLead`). `tune` moves `peerLead` (how far past its last state a peer's dart is flown on by its velocity, 0.15 s; kept in `PEER_ROPE_TUNE` in `src/net/peerRope.ts`, out of the hook's own table, and put back by `reset` with it) and `sendPoints` live. From wave 3c it rides another player's vehicle as well, as the box their game keeps (never asked of the engine: the rope's own arithmetic, `src/player/grappleBox.ts`) at a smooth pose of its own, dead-reckoned from the relay's velocity eased over `peerCorrect` (raw, every state's few milliseconds of jitter in when it was heard swung a tow by tens of metres a second) and held back by the picture's own mean lag, the rope drawn on their picture; while the rope rides it the body is held off that box by the rope's own box test, as off a hull this browser simulates (`hullTest: false` lets it pass through their sensor as the wave first had it, to compare): `frames` names such a point `peer:<their relay id>`, and `peer` reports `mode` (the pose: `smooth`, or `picture`, the glided picture led by the glide's own step, to compare), `rides` (whose vehicle the rope rides, 0 for none), `released` (whose the fast flight after a let-go answers to), `mayHurt` (whether that player could hurt you now: the server's friendly fire or a duel, which is the only place a slam that came of their vehicle harms; `lastSlam` gains `peer` and `spared`), `smoothToPicture` (the most the pose stood off their drawn picture over the last whole second, m) and `perMs` (that per m/s of their speed: about 0.02 measured with 5 ms of jitter on the states, where the smoke asks under 0.06), `jump` (the newest state's `miss` from where the pose expected it, how much of it the `timing` of when it and the last were heard accounts for, `past` which, `peerJump` and that, a teleport lets go as `moved`) and a row a `slots`; it lets go in words (`loose`) when that player is no longer shown on that vehicle. Nothing is ever put on their vehicle: it tows you and feels nothing. `fire: 'vehicle'` fires at the nearest of theirs too. `tune` moves `peerPose`, `peerCorrect` (0.5: a smaller one follows a change of speed sooner and passes more jitter on), `peerLag` and `peerJump` live; the box test's contacts and push-outs count in `hull`'s `contacts` and `pushed` as a hull test's do. From wave 3b it rides the vehicles this browser simulates, and reports for them: `frames` (each rope point's frame, `world` or the hull's id, the anchor first), `onHull`, `pivotSpeed` (m/s), `paidOut` (all the rope a hull has paid out since it was fastened), `slip` over the last whole second (`paid`, and the `catches`, `turns` and `reels` that slipped), `towed` and `towHold` (what is left of the tow's hold), `pull` over the last second (`impulse`, the N s put on hulls, and `hits`, the roped hulls' own hit tests fired, which must stay 0), `brake` (each roped hull: its latch -- set at the rope's first pull only on a hull at rest that nothing drives -- whether it has been `pulled`, whether it is `driven`, its `budget`, whether it is `moving` and its `lag`), `hull` (the hulls `marked` walk-through, the hull test's `contacts`, the metres it `pushed` the body out, its `calls` and `contactTests` over the last second, and the `gap` from the capsule to the nearest marked hull's bounds box at its true pose, which on a box-shaped hull must not be negative; inside an open or concave hull -- an open cockpit -- the box is not the hull, so it reads negative there and is no penetration test; and `floor`, whether the body rests on a marked hull's walkable face this frame, where the hull test takes its fall off and holds its apex, the body still hanging rather than standing), `near` (the hull families cast this frame), `bodyReads`, and `drive` (the vehicle the console is driving and the seconds left); `blocked` counts a span into a hull only when it reaches deeper than `wrapOffset`. The mark's kinds gain `vehicle` (drawn amber) and `creature` (a rideable creature's cuboid, refused; the rope passes through it). `fire: 'vehicle'` fires at the nearest vehicle in view; `drive` sets the nearest parked vehicle going with nobody on it, through its autopilot, for `seconds` (10 when left out), which is how a tow is tried alone, and `drive: false` stops it at once. `tune` moves the vehicle numbers live: the slip's (`slipKeep`, `slipDecel`, `slipTurn`, `slipReel`, `slipCorr`), the tow's (`towHold`, `towFriction`, `towDamp`), the parked brake's (`parkHold` -- 0 takes the brake away and a hanging player drags a parked speeder to the edge -- and `parkRest`), the hull test's (`hullLag`, `hullPush`, `hullTest`: false leaves a roped moving hull to the character controller and the solver, to compare), `hullLead` (false: the hulls where the last physics step left them, never led to the frame's time, to compare), `vehicles` (false: the rope and the dart pass through every hull, as before), `tighten` and `straightDeg`. At 144 fps a fast vehicle jitters a little against you: that is the game's own stutter of every fast vehicle (its drawn hull jumps by a physics step every second or third frame), not the rope's. From wave 3 it says what the rope `holds` (`anchor`, `thing` for a loose prop, or nothing, while `state` reads `holding` on a prop); `range`, the reach as progress makes it (`grapple.range`, the tune's own until something grants it); `thing`, the prop held (its `label`, its `mass`, its `distance` from the rope point, `whoMoves` -- the mass rule's word, `thing` for anything at or under the tether's `strength`, `both` up to `heavyRatio` times the player's 80 kg, `player` past that -- whether F is `yanking` it, how long its straight span has been `blocked`, and its `recalls`); `thingDistance`, the prop's distance as last measured, kept after a let-go so a yank's end says where it came to; and on a prop under the mark its `mass` and `whoMoves`. `fire: 'prop'` fires at the nearest loose prop inside the view's width and in reach (stand among the arrival's crates, which a world stands only with `localStorage['swg.scratch'] = '1'` set before it loads -- `props()` says whether it did -- and look at one): F held as it bites yanks it in, at the grip's own 14 m/s, until it is within 1.2 m or 1.5 s has run, and it is stopped where it came to and drops there, never thrown on (`lastLetGo` `yanked`); with no F walking away past the length drags it. `tune` moves the mass rule's numbers live (`strength`, `heavyRatio`, `yankAhead`, `yankMost`, `yankToss`, `tetherBlocked`, `tetherStandMargin`): `strength: 10` makes a crate too heavy to pull, which the dart refuses in words. From wave 2 it also reports its danger, look and sound: `lastSlam` (the last contact the slam rule judged past `slamFrom`: its `speed` against what was struck, its `harm`, whether the harm `took`, how long `ago`, and its `kind` -- `swing` on the rope, `released` in the fast flight after a let-go, `haul` for the haul's own push and `floor` for a landing, the last two costing nothing, `console` for the helper's own); `stagger` (`left`, the seconds it has to run, `daze`, the vignette's kick this frame, `shake`, the camera's own register, `drawnKick`, the kick the effects last drew); `released` (`on` and the `speed` it was let go at); `jolt` (the last catch's `speed`, the `shake` it writes and the seconds `left`); `arm` (degrees between the drawn left upper arm and the way to the rope's first point, null with no rope or rig) and `armWeight`; `lean` (degrees) and `leanSends` (the whole turn going to other players: while it leans past `leanLevel` and for `leanTail` seconds after it is level, so their figure of you eases upright rather than snapping); `camScale` (the orbit's scale, exactly 1 when the rope is not out); and `sounds` (whether they were asked for, whether the boost is Jedi Academy's whoosh or the game's own swing, the last requests with the mixer's key -- 0 is nothing started, which a driven tab always answers -- the `ratchets` of the last second and what `__debug.audio().recent` says of the hook's own ids). `slam: v` applies the slam rule once at `v` m/s against a wall a metre ahead along the view (its harm, its stagger and, the next frame, the flash, the arc and the sound); `stagger: true` plays a stagger with its shake and daze and no harm, for judging the screen effect by eye; `tune` moves every wave-2 number live (`slamFrom`, `slamPer`, `slamKill`, `slamGap`, `staggerFrom`, `stagger`, `shake`, `shakeTime`, `daze` -- 0 takes the screen effect away -- `dazeIn`, the jolt's, the lean's, the arm's, the camera's and the sound's, `windGain` turning the speed wind on). Then, as in wave 1: `grapple()` reports its `state` (`off`, `flying`, `attached`, and from wave 3 `holding`) and `everyone`; the `mark` (whether it shows, is held a moment or is the next anchor while one is in, how far off, and what is under it: `terrain`, `still`, `pane`, `water`, `door`, `prop`, `moving`), worked out again from the camera as it stands, so advance one step after a `look` first; the `hand`, the rope's point, and the `dart` in flight and how far it has flown; the rope's `length`, `spent`, `free`, `taut`, `slack` and `pivot`, its `points` and its counts (`rope`: wraps, ground wraps, unwraps, fan hits, fallbacks, refused unwraps, the caps); `blocked`, the spans through something right now, cast on the spot, which must be 0; the rope's words this frame (`modes`: on the ground, taut, held, afloat, hanging, sliding, swinging, the latch, seconds since it last pulled, hauling, lipping); `swim` (the rope's rise in degrees, whether it has the swim's vertical, the wading drag); `lastImpact` and `maxImpact`, the speed into what the body met; `gravity`; the actions muted this frame; and `cost`, the median and p99 µs of the rope's own work, timed from the first time this is asked. `fire: true` fires at what the mark shows, `fire: [x, y, z]` at a point, through the mark's own range and clear-line checks; `letGo` lets go; `everyone: false` takes the hook off every character (W4's rig); `tune` moves any number of `GRAPPLE_TUNE` live (`gravity` 1 is the game's own, which adds nothing; another value scales gravity while swinging, to compare); `reset` puts the table back as it ships |
| `saber().hit`, `saber({ stepLength: 0.2, maxSteps: 2 })`, `saber({ brushShare: 0 })`, `blades()`, `blades({ window: 0.2 })`, `blades({ tier: 5, style: 'strong' })`, `blades({ tier: null, style: null })`, `blades({ moves: { kataShare: 1, leapShare: 2 } })`, `blades({ reset: true })`, `clash()`, `clash({ reach: 0 })` | Where a blade lands. `saber().hit` is the player's: this swing's hits, what last frame's blades cost in casts and sub-steps, the brush's last victims and the tuning (`SABER_HIT` in `src/combat/saberHit.ts`, every number ours). A blade is cast along the ground it covered since the last frame, one cast per `stepLength` and never more than `maxSteps` of them, so a swing at a run no longer steps over a thin body; `maxSteps: 1` puts every blade in the game back to the single cast it made before. `brushShare` is what standing inside a merely lit blade costs, a share of the style's damage at most four times a second per body, and `brushShare: 0` is the switch that makes the game exactly what it was. `blades()` is everybody else's — the fighters' and the catalogue's, kept apart on purpose — with `lookup`, which says in words whether a swing can name what it touched at all, and `window`, how long each of the old one-hand swings cuts for, which decides how lethal a bladed body below the second tier is (from there up a lightsaber's swings are the move machine's, below). Its `moves` are the lightsaber move machine a fighter or a person swings from the second tier up (`src/world/npcSaber.ts`, the player's own `saber.ts` pressed by the brain): every NPC blade's swings, chains, specials, katas, leaps, lunges, kicks and back attacks, and the bolts that reached a lit blade, those it was placed to block and those it turned away, in total and per minute of the world's clock since `{ reset: true }`; `tier` and `style` put every NPC blade on one rung (0 and 1 are the old random swings, 2 and 3 chain in one style, 4 and 5 add the specials, the katas and the leaps) and one of the styles its hilt can swing, and `null` hands each its own back; `moves` moves the machine's own numbers (the deflect chance by tier, the chain lengths and rests, the Force, the share of chains opened with a kata or a kick, how often a leap is tried and across what gap, and `closeTo`, how near it closes before it strikes, which is nearer than the old swings' 1.9 m because a swept blade never landed from there). A chain throws several swings where the old clock threw one a cooldown, so each of its swings (and a staff's kick) lands only a share of the body's own blow, measured from when its last one landed against its own cooldown between blows -- a person's template's, a fighter's `swingEvery` plus half its `swingSpread` -- so over a fight no bladed body lands more than one blow a cooldown however many it swings, and a miss spends nothing; `moves: { blowRate }` is how many a cooldown (1, the body's own rate; a large number lets every swing of a chain land a whole blow, for a comparison). Each fighter's row in `fighters()` and each person's in `mobiles()` carries its own as `blade`. `clash()` is two blades meeting: the lit blades weighed last step, how many nobody named (two of those can never meet each other), the pairs tested, the clashes and where they were; `reach: 0` turns them off outright |
| `guns()`, `guns({ speed: 1 })`, `scars()`, `scars({ on: 0 })`, `marks()`, `marks({ scarLife: 60 })`, `forceLightning()`, `forceLightning({ width: 0.3 })`, `forceLightning({ test: true, at: [0, 2, 0], dir: [0, 0, -1] })`, `footprints()`, `footprints({ life: 30 })` | `guns()` is the ground guns' bolt speed: the multiplier in force, what a bolt is stretched to, and what every trigger that fires a bolt leaves the muzzle at now beside what its own weapon data gives, slowest first; `{ speed: 1 }` puts every one back to exactly its own. A ship's guns are not here. `scars()` says whether the mark a bolt leaves is being laid at all and how many of each family were laid and refused, `{ on: 0 }` being the switch that makes the game what it was; `marks()` is how a mark looks — how wide each family is drawn, how long it lasts — with any of its numbers moved by name, and the three that are spent when the marks are built refused by name in `refused`. `forceLightning()` is the world's Force beams and where their picture came from; every name in `FORCE_LIGHTNING` moves live (the two spent when the pool is built answer `atNextWorld`), and `{ test: true, at, dir }` fires one where you say, which is how lightning at a wall is looked at without a Jedi. `footprints()` is the last dozen prints laid and what became of every step — laid, refused for hard ground or aboard a hull, or too soon after the last — and every number of its own moves live (`enabled`, `length`, `width`, `stance`, `life`, `strengthLife`, `minStep`, `maxScale`, `bodies`); which ground keeps a print is `PRINT_SURFACES` in `src/world/surfaces.ts` |
| `bolts()`, `turrets()`, `turret(20)`, `shootPlayer()`, `heal()` | Blaster fights and the turrets used to test blocking |
| `god()`, `god(true)`, `god(false)` | God mode (`src/player/godMode.ts`): with it on nothing takes your health -- no blow, bolt, bite, fall, crash, burn, lava or drowning, since every one of them ends in `Player.takeDamage` and the flag is asked there first -- and nothing takes the hull of what you fly or ride: a ship through its own fight's `god` (a bolt still shows the shield's hit and takes nothing), anything else through its `invulnerable`, both given back exactly as they were when you leave the seat or put the mode down (a shuttle stays invulnerable, a ship made unhurtable with `shipCombat({ god: true })` stays so). Creatures and people go on fighting you, unlike noclip; a blow refused shows no red flash and no arc; `kill('player')` still kills; the display's quiet line says `God mode` while it is on. With no server it is always allowed; with one, only the world's admin may, and anybody else is refused in words (health comes off only in the browser of whoever is hit, so a browser in god mode cannot be killed and nobody else could tell); a mode turned on while no server answered is put down by itself the moment one that does not make this browser its admin takes the world. Never saved: a reload is always mortal. The answer is whether it is on, your health, the hull held (`fightGod` for a ship, `invulnerable` for anything else), and whether a server holds the world and this browser is its admin. A ridden speeder held invulnerable leaves its rider on it when it lands on its back, since being thrown is the hurt it does |
| `burn()`, `burn({ light: { dps: 8, seconds: 3 } })`, `burn({ out: true })`, `burn({ on: false })`, `breath()`, `breath({ seconds: 60 })`, `breath({ full: true })` | The player's own fire and breath, every number of both of them ours. `burn()` is the numbers in force and the burn as it stands; `{ light: { dps, seconds } }` sets you alight now, through the same path anything else would (so it is refused while you may not be hurt), `{ out: true }` puts it out in silence, `tick` is how often a blow lands, `cap` the longest one affliction may keep you alight, and `on: false` makes the game what it was. `breath()` is the air as it stands: `seconds` is a full lungful, `recoverSeconds` how long a full one takes to come back, `tick` and `damage` what running out costs, `sayAfter` the patience before a line is said, and `full: true` fills the lungs now. Both run under `advance`: `burn({ light: { dps: 8, seconds: 3 } })` then `advance(3)` spends the whole burn |
| `powers(['grip', 'pull'])`, `gadgets(['cryoban', 'trip_mine'])`, `creature(8)`, `fighter(3)`, `fighter(2, 'human', 'saber')` | The skills in the slots for either class (no argument lists them), and something to try them on. `fighter(n, species, arm)` asks for what each carries -- `'saber'`, `'melee'` or `'gun'` -- instead of the rack's own draw, which is how to put a blade against a gun on purpose |
| `fighters()`, `fighters({ gunRange: 10, fleeUnder: 0.5 })`, `fighters({ roll: { share: [0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1] } })`, `fighters({ roll: { recover: 0.2 } })`, `fighters({ jump: { saberFrom: 1 } })` | What every fighter out is thinking, and the body's own numbers. `fighters()` gives `out`, a `tune` (`FIGHTER_TUNE` in `src/world/npcs.ts`, every number of it invented: how fast it runs and wanders, how often it thinks and by how much that is spread, its reach, the range it shoots from, the cone its nose must be within, its two cooldowns and their spreads, how fast it turns, how near a goal counts as arrived, where its nerve breaks, how much of the indoor leash a wander may use, and the stuck window), a `brain` (the creatures' own `BRAIN_TUNE`, printed here so that both halves of a fighter's mind read in one place, but **shared with every creature on the planet** and moved by `__debug.mobileTune({ brain: { … } })` rather than from here), and a line per fighter: its name, what it carries, the brain's own word for what it is doing (idle, wander, alert, chase, attack, flee, return), what it is after (`you` for the player), its health, its nerve, how far it is from where it was stood, whether it is in a room, how many grudges it holds and how often it has been found stuck. An argument retunes the body's numbers live; nothing is saved, and the mind's numbers are not this call's to change. `roll` and `jump` move the two tables a fighter and a person from the catalogue throw themselves aside and jump by (`EVADE_TUNE` and `JUMP_TUNE` in `src/world/evade.ts`: the share a second each tier rolls while it is aimed at, the cooldown, what counts as being aimed at, the heights a jump level reaches, which tiers earn one and what ledge is worth one; all ours but Jedi Academy's own roll speed, roll length and jump heights), and each fighter's line carries its `roll` (rolls and hops so far, and whether it is rolling now) and its `jump` (its level, how high that is, the jumps it has made up to a ledge, and whether it is in the air now). `{ roll: { share: [0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1] } }` is every tier from 2 up evading the moment it is aimed at, which is the way to watch one. `{ roll: { recover: 0.2 } }` is how long a body stays committed after the roll's own 0.55 s before it may shoot, swing or roll again, in place of the whole of the roll clip's length, which is what a negative `recover` (the shipped -1) still means (`rollCommit`; a fighter and a person alike, and ours). Aimed at is your gun's line passing a body that is already fighting something, or a blow from four metres or more in the last second and a half; a body you merely look at down a raised gun does not move. The evade is Jedi Academy's roll and nothing else (the owner's D9): a hop aside in its place, and a lightsaber carrier's leap at what it fights, are written but ship off, and `{ roll: { hopShare: 0.35 } }` is how to see them. A jump is otherwise only ever up onto a ledge somebody it is chasing stands on, within its own reach, and a body keeps chasing such a somebody rather than giving them up after eight seconds only when its jump can really get it up there (the second Force level's 4.9 m; the plain hop and the first Force level reach nothing that does not already count as level) |
| `nav()`, `nav({ reach: 0.6, every: 0.25 })`, `nav({ describe: true })`, `nav({ door: true })`, `nav({ door: { legs: false } })`, `nav({ outdoor: { mobiles: false } })` | The indoor pathing (`src/world/nav/`). `nav()` gives `floors` (this pack carries floor meshes), `converted` (it carries anything at all out of a floor file, so `false` is the whole of "none of this is running and every body indoors steers as it always did"), how many rooms name a floor and how many have been read, how many searches have been asked for and what became of each (`found` a real path, `doorOnly` the doorway answer in a room with no mesh, `missed` nothing at all, `deferred` past the step's own budget), and what the last one cost in triangles opened and corners kept. An argument moves any of the invented numbers live (`NAV_TUNE` in `src/world/nav/navMesh.ts`: how near a corner counts as reached, how far a goal may move before a fresh path, how often one body may ask, the retry after a search that found nothing, the triangles one search may open, the searches one step may spend, the corners kept, how far above and below its feet a body looks for its floor and how far off it may stand, the points tested round a corner by a body too wide for the mesh, and how far past a doorway's middle a path aims). `{ describe: true }` reads every room of the building you are standing in and says what came out of the building — its cells, how many carry a floor and how many do not, its triangles, its doorways, its open edges, and how many cells had their adjacency rebuilt from the shared edges rather than taken from the file. The agent radius is not tunable: it is the player's own, and a node test fails if the two ever disagree. `outdoor` is the ground's grid beside it, with `slopeDegrees`, the angle the loaded grid was baked at, and `forMobiles`, whether that is gentle enough (45 degrees or less, `MOBILE_CLIMB_DEGREES`) for the world's people and creatures to be handed it as well as its fighters; `{ outdoor: { mobiles: false } }` takes it off them for a comparison. `{ door: true }` adds the join between the street and the rooms (`src/world/nav/doorway.ts`): `buildings` and `exits` (ways in worked out so far), `chosen` (doors a body outside made for), `throughs` (walks through one begun), `gaveUp` (a walk through that had not flipped the room in `through` seconds, the door then passed over for `refuse`), `noWayIn` (a goal in a building with no door a body can walk in by, when it steers as it always did), `handIns` and `handOuts` (rooms flipped under a walk in, and bodies walked out of a building), and `last`, the last of those: who, `in` or `out`, the building's model and the room, the door, and how many seconds the walk in took; `near` is the ways in of the building with rooms nearest you, each with the room it opens into, its middle, its sill and the two points (`out`, `in`) a body is walked through it by. `{ door: { legs: false } }` switches the join off, which is every body steering as it did before it -- at the wall from outside, and by the rooms' boxes from inside -- and any other number of `DOOR_TUNE` moves the same way (how far off the doorway's plane its two points stand, how near the outside point counts as at the door, how far ahead along the doorway's own axis the walk through aims, so a body that came from the side is brought square before the jambs, how long a walk through may take, how often the door is chosen again, how far a sill may stand over or under the ground outside, and `wallLook`, how far past its reach a body still asks whether a wall stands between it and its foe; a new `step` measures every building's ways in again) |
| `patrols()`, `patrols({ go: true })`, `patrols({ tune: { pause: 2, linger: [5, 10] } })`, `patrols({ tune: { walks: false } })`, `patrols({ tune: { combat: true } })` | The towns' walkers (`src/world/patrols.ts`), walking their rounds on the server's own clock: set going fifty to seventy seconds after they are stood, thirty to sixty seconds at a point the town marked for lingering and five at any other, back to the first after the last. `walkers` is every body out with a round, nearest first: the point it waits at (`at`) or walks from and the one it walks to (`to`, -1 while waiting), `legs` and `rounds` walked, `skipped` (legs given up after `giveUp` seconds, 30 and ours, without a metre's headway -- a point in a yard the ground's grid cannot see into, a kerb a body cannot climb -- so one bad leg never stops a round; time spent fighting or held still far off is not counted), how long the last leg took (`lastLeg`), the last few waits, how long before it sets off again, its room, its state, whether it is walking a doorway, and its `stuck` count (every time its stuck check has fired, never reset). `rows` is the nearest rows whose body walks, standing or not, with how many points each round has and how many it lingers at. `{ go: true }` puts you at the nearest such row, on its own floor indoors. `{ tune }` moves `PATROL_TUNE` live: `walks: false` stands every walker at its first point, and `combat: true` walks the combat walkers too, which the server never set going (it stood them at their posts). | `mood()`, `mood({ set: 'angry' })`, `mood({ body: 0 })` | The mood this player is in, whether the pack really had a branch for it, which moods the game offers and which of them a conversion can pose; set one without the chat line; and the switch that leaves the body exactly as it was and keeps only the mark on what you say (`MOOD_TUNE` in `src/player/moods.ts`) |
| `await mobile('rancor', 14, n)`, `await mobile('stormtrooper', 12, 3, { level: 60 })`, `await mobile('stormtrooper', 12, 3, { tier: 4 })`, `await mobile('stormtrooper', 12, 1, { weapon: 'lightsaber' })`, `mobiles()`, `mobiles('wolf')`, `await mobileRoles('bantha')` | Stand `n` of a catalogue entry ahead (an id, a name, or the best find; it waits for the model). `{ level: 60 }` stands them at that level with the health and blow a body of that level has: the middle health pool and damage of the catalogue's own emulator rows nearest the level, people's for a person and creatures' for anything else, through the very curve the catalogue puts every row through (`src/world/levelStats.ts`; the answer's `level` says which rows and levels it read, and with a catalogue built without emulator rows it is a level and nothing more), and so on the tier that level earns; `{ tier: 4 }` puts those bodies alone on a fighting tier, which wins over the tier `mobileTune({ tier })` puts everybody on; neither touches that one. `{ weapon: 'lightsaber' }` stands them holding a weapon off the rack of your choosing -- an id, a template or the game's name, found as `weapons(find)` finds one (an exact id, template or name first, then the plainest id or name holding the text: the quest, decorative and never-used ones last, then the shortest id, so `'dl44'` is the DL-44 and not the DL-44 XT) -- whatever their own creature's list, their name or their temper would have given them (`OwnArms.forcedTemplate` in `src/world/mobiles/arms.ts`), prepared before it is in their hands as every weapon a body holds is; a creature or a droid with no hand, and a grenade, an instrument or a dancer's prop, are refused in words, and the answer's `weapon` says what was found and how many hold it. With no server it is this browser's own spawn only. With a server the world's admin stands them through the server instead, and the weapon rides in the record (`weapon`, a template path the server checks), so every browser stands them holding the same thing; anybody else is refused in words, and a relay that speaks no such field arms them off their own list as before. Those already standing keep what they hold (`arm` puts something new in a hand already standing). List what is out (state, health, clip, target, tier, shadow, room, `airless` while its room has no collision built and it is holding its height, and a standing person's `post` with how far off it the body is, a town walker's `patrol` round, `door` while it is walking to or through a doorway, whether the ground's `grid` is handed to it, and `stuckEvents`, every time its stuck check has fired), or search the catalogue (the total, the first forty, and why one cannot be stood); an entry's pack, roles and gait speeds, with the walk and run it will move at |
| `await arm({ weapon: 'dl44' })`, `await arm({ target: 'crosshair', weapon: 'lightsaber' })`, `await arm({ target: 1234, weapon: 'carbine_e11' })`, `await arm({ target: 'stood:tatooine:12', weapon: 'lance_staff_wood_s1' })` | A weapon off the rack in the hand of a body already standing (`MobileManager.rearm`, `Mobile.rearm`): `weapon` is found as `weapons(find)` finds one, and `target` is what the crosshair rests on (`'crosshair'`, the default: the nameplate's own pick, asked at once), a body's key (`mobiles()` lists them) or the name the world knows it by. Whatever it held goes -- the rack's copy let go, a lightsaber's blade and its moves with it -- and the new one is prepared before it is in its hand, with the fighting roles of its carry row (the combat stance and gaits, the swings, the shots) laid over its pack's own again; how it idles, walks and runs and the mood it was stood in stay its own. A body that had nothing to fight with gets its own temper back, one left with nothing goes passive, and a lightsaber's colour and style come from the body's own seed. A creature or a droid with no hand, a grenade, an instrument or a prop, and a fighter (armed by its kind as it is stood: `fighter(n, species, 'saber' \| 'melee' \| 'gun')`) are refused in words. With no server it is this browser's alone; with a server a body on the wire is everybody's, so only the world's admin may, the word (`spawn` `arm`, the fourth wire, which needs the relay restarted) rides its record and every browser on the world re-arms its own copy, the admin's included, a browser standing it later is handed the same, and it lasts as long as the record does (a seen one forgotten after its respawn is armed off its own seed again); a body this browser keeps to itself (a follower, one of ours) is armed here only and the answer says so. The weapon it already holds asked for again does nothing (the server says it again to every browser that sees an armed body), and of two asked close together the last asked is the one held, whichever was made ready first |
| `people()`, `people({ go: true })`, `people({ where: 'cities', mood: true })`, `people({ gcw: 'rebel' })`, `people({ restand: true })`, `people({ tune: { most: 20, mostEssential: 30, postRadius: 5, reuseLook: 0.5 } })`, `people({ respawn: false })`, `people({ story: true })`, `people({ perch: true })`, `people({ nge: 'never' })`, `people({ roamEvery: [8, 20] })`, `people({ arrivalClear: 64 })` | The people who stand where the server stood them (`src/world/standingPeople.ts`), every row carried out of the snapshot's frame into the world's the first time a pass knows the layout's centre, at the row's own height outdoors as well as in (somebody on a bridge, a balcony, a hut or an Ewok village is held perched there until what they stand on is solid: `{ perch: true }`, below), each stood with its own creature's level, health, blow, temper, ranged attack and weapons over its body's, in its row's mood (a species rig's branch for it, lent; its own idle where the rig has none), in its row's own room, a patroller at the first point of its walk (at its own row, as the server stood it, where that point lies more than `routeReach` off, and measured from wherever it stands), a guard as the side holding the world, and a crowd's person and body drawn again from the row's key each life. `people()` gives `rows`, `up` (bodies standing, nearest first up to `most` who may be fought and `mostEssential` of the furniture beside them), `side` (whose guards stand), `down` (the dead waiting to come back), `indoors`, and what the last pass that really ran did: `stood`, `dropped`, `swapped` (put down to make room: with the cap full, a person standing more than `swapMargin` farther off than one waiting gives up the place, unless in a fight, so the cap stays nearest first as you walk) and `waiting` (people whose room is not built yet, who wait rather than falling through its floor); `standing` is each one up with its room and distance, `nearest` the rows nearest you in the world's frame, standing or not. `{ go: true }` puts you at the nearest, on its own floor and in its own room when it is indoors. `{ tune }` moves any number of `PEOPLE_TUNE` live (how near they stand and drop, the cap, a pass's share, `swapMargin`, and `postRadius` and `postEvery`, how far and how often a person steps about its spot, all ours), and `{ respawn: false }` keeps everybody killed dead, even when you walk away and back, until `{ respawn: true }` (somebody the game merely took away, fallen out of the world say, still comes back on their own clock). `{ where: 'cities', mood: true }` lists who stands in that part of the world's life (`cities`, `caves`, `tasks`, `poi`, `dungeons`...): who each was stood as, its body, the mood asked for and the `idle` it really stands in (`idle:npc_sad` when a rig lent one), what it is `holding` (a rack id, or null for empty hands), its `level`, its `post` (`still` for a town's still rows and anybody sat or lying down) and whether it is `essential`. `{ gcw: 'rebel' }` hands this world's towns to the Rebels and stands their guards (Imperial by default, the server's own rule for a world nobody holds, kept for the session), and `{ restand: true }` puts everybody down to be stood again on the next pass. `reuseLook` (0.7, ours) is the share of an unattackable crowd's draws that take a body already built. `{ story: true }` answers for the story's named people instead (`src/story/people.ts`), who are not these bodies: `shown`, what the journal's People tab shows (each named person met, by the name the character knows, where and when last seen, and gone once the character has learned it), and beside it `records`, the truth the tab never shows -- each person's own Standing and Trust toward the character, whether they are alive, their access (`open`, `wary`, `refused`, `vouched`; a track's burn refuses its own contacts), what the character knows and when they were last seen -- with what is owed (`debts`) and the companion's record. `{ perch: true }` answers for the bodies stood on something raised outdoors (`src/world/mobiles/perch.ts`): a body more than `lift` (0.3 m) over the terrain is held in the air at its row's height, standing still, while one probe a frame looks for what it stands on among what stands still, the terrain left out; found, it is set down on it, and a person with a `near` post who lands on something raised keeps to its spot (`still`). With nothing found, the streamer is asked every quarter of a second within `countWithin` (150 m) of you whether anything whose own footprint holds that point is still to come (`LayoutStreamer.builtAt`, asked of the objects there and not of whole regions): `built`, nothing is, and it is let down onto the terrain at once; `coming`, something is wanted and not yet solid, and it waits at most `wait` (6 s), a clock that stands still behind a loading screen; `far`, something is there that you stand too far off for the streamer to make solid (a small object at a short object reach), and it waits with no clock at all; and far off it is held. The answer is `held` (in the air this frame near you), `far` (in the air farther off), `landed`, `fell` (nothing was coming under it), `timedOut` and `again` since this world loaded, `ever` (bodies out that were ever perched) and the ten of those `nearest` you, each with how high it stands `over` the terrain now, what it `became` (`driven` for one handed to another browser), how long it `waited`, the streamer's last answer and its post; a few seconds after arriving `held` should be 0. Every number is ours and moves with `mobileTune({ perch })`. `sources` counts the `rows`, and the bodies `up`, by where each came from: `core3`, `herald`, `ours` (the Star Destroyer's crew) and `nge`, SOE's own people for Kashyyyk's seven zones and Mustafar, which every converter's `spawns` writes into `assets-private/nge/` from the reference beside it (`tools/swg/ngeref/`, shipped to every player by the owner's decision of 2026-10-19; `src/world/ngeOverlay.ts`), whose rows stand after everybody else's so no other `stood:` name moves, and on a server stand as the world's seeded bodies like any other, each named by its own key (`stood:<world>:<key>`), so alike in every browser converted from the same reference even where one machine's catalogue joins fewer bodies; `nge` says whether these packs have it, which worlds it lists, where it stands (`where`) and what the last world it lists made of it (`left`: '' stood, `server`, `off`, `refused` or `missing`). It stands everywhere with no opt-in (`{ nge: 'always' }`, the default); to compare, `{ nge: 'never' }` leaves it out and `{ nge: 'alone' }` stands it only where no server others share holds the world (a session's first world with a server saved counts as held, since its connect comes after that loading screen), each from the next arrival and in memory only. Packs converted before it have no index and stand none of it. One console line on each arrival on a listed world says how many people and wild areas came from it, or why it was left out. A row its spawner let loiter or wander steps about within its spawner's own radius (`roam`), every `NGE_OVERLAY_TUNE.roamEvery` seconds: `{ roamEvery: [8, 20] }` sets that pair live (ours, the owner's to feel; the default null takes `postEvery`'s, the standing people's own pace), `{ roamEvery: null }` puts it back. none on Kashyyyk or Mustafar. `ours()` counts them by kind and stage, gives `cap` (the forty places of part-of-the-furniture people, what the data's own hold and what is left for ours, who only ever take what the data leaves), `memory` (the mobiles' model memory against its budget: none of ours is stood past `memory` of it) and `tally` (stood, boarded, missed their shuttle, arrived, `outwalked` (an arrival still walking when its `walkMax` was out), let go stuck -- `giveUp` seconds without moving a metre or `lost` without getting a metre nearer --, left a building, put away for the data's people, and the last refusal). Nothing of ours is stood that would take the model memory past `memory` of its budget unless it wears a body already built, and while the data's own people are short of memory with nobody of their own farther off to give it back, the fewest of ours, farthest first, who together give back what they were short of are put away, once for each of the data's passes that was short, and nobody when all of ours together would not. `{ list: true }` is every one of them nearest first, each marked `ours: true`, with its stage, its body, its room and place, whether it is indoors, walking a doorway or still to pass a door first, whether it sits and how far it has to go. `{ port: true }` is the nearest port as its travellers see it -- its shuttle's phase now, its last, current and next rounds (when each lands, waits and leaves) and every traveller of them with the moment it appears, its phase by the clock and what it is doing if it stands -- and why a port is not ready (no collector, no town's list to draw from, its building not streamed in, its shuttle not stood); `{ port: 'Mos Eisley' }` names one. `{ buildings: true }` is the buildings near you with each one's kind, whether the data leaves it empty, how many places and seats it has, its doors and its places' states. `{ go: 'port' }` puts you by the nearest port's collector (or the one `port` names), `{ go: 'building' }` inside the nearest empty building with room for anybody, `{ go: 'bank' }` (any kind) the nearest of that kind. `{ tune }` moves `OURS_TUNE` (the switch, the pass, the reach, how many a pass and the memory share), `ROUTINE_TUNE` (the travellers) and `FILLER_TUNE` (the fillers, the counts per kind among them) live, all of it ours, each under its own name (`{ tune: { ours: { build: 200 }, routine: { reach: 1 }, fillers: { drop: 90 } } }`); a key only one table has may be given bare, and a bare key two tables have -- `build` and `drop` (a port's reach and a building's) and `reach` and `front` (a terminal's and a seat's) -- moves nothing and is named back under `ambiguous`; It answers in every zone, including one with no nebulae at all. Lightning follows the game's own rule: a nebula strikes by the game's chance for its rate on ticks of `strikeTick` on the clock the weather reads (`strikeChanceCap`, `strikeCube`, `strikeLifeMin`, `strikeLifeSpan` and `hitEvery` are the rule's numbers, read; the rest are ours), a bolt strikes every ship it passes within `hitShare` of the ship's radius and drains only the shield on that side, and each ship rests `hitEvery` seconds between strikes. Under that rule a ship is almost never struck: `bendWithin: 900` puts back this game's old aim, which moved a bolt whose far end fell within that many metres of your ship onto it, and `bendWithin: 0` is the game's rule again. `systems` moves how strongly a nebula weakens a ship per unit of its density (ours: no row of the game's tables sets one), taken once from the densest nebula holding the ship rather than added up over the several rows the game layers one cloud from; a ship takes it on its next step, and its spec is written only as it flies in or out. Every name in `NEBULA_TUNE` can be moved live: the ones that place the sheets (`density`, `sizeMin`, `sizeMax`, `centrePower`, the counts and the zone cap) refill the buffers on the spot and make no material, so nothing can compile on a live frame; `alpha` is the "too solid" knob for the sheets and costs nothing at all, and inside a big nebula, where the sheets fade out and the haze is nearly all you see, `shellAlpha`, `shellRadius` and `shellScale` are the knobs that move the picture. `drawWithin` is how far off a strike is still drawn (one too far to be seen strikes all the same), and `strikeReach` how near a nebula must be for its strikes to be worked out at all when no ship is inside it (one holding a ship is always asked). A zone is given as many strike records as its own rows can keep standing at once, with room to spare (rows that share a name strike together and are counted so), never fewer than `liveStrikes` nor more than `liveMost`; `beams`, `beamSegments`, `waveAlpha`, `liveStrikes`, `liveSpread` and `liveMost` are spent when a zone's bolt pool and strike records are made, so they take effect at the next zone load and the answer says so under `atNextZone`. `shown: false` hides them without freeing anything, which is the A and B for reading what they cost with `fxTiming` on. The player's own **Nebula opacity** slider (Esc → Graphics → Space) is a second number over all of this, kept apart from `alpha` and `shellAlpha` so neither writes the other: it thins the sheets and the haze together and eases how much a nebula you are in takes off the flare and the rays to match, all as uniforms, and the answer reports it as `opacity` (the `dim` beside it already includes it). `shellDepthTest: false` draws the haze the way the client's own near shader does, over everything in front of the camera. `strike: true` stands a bolt now rather than waiting for the clock, through the ship you fly half its reach beside its middle, on a side the tick picks so either shield may take it (or, flying none, where the nearest nebula's next tick would put it), and answers whether it struck, which is how the shield-only path is watched and how lightning is checked from a driven tab |
| `audio()`, `audio({ distance: { audible: 12 } })`, `sound('sound/cr_bantha_emote_vocalize.snd')`, `sound(id, [x, y, z])`, `soundGain(0, 3)`, `await audioSelfTest()` | Sound. Nothing can be heard from a driven tab, so this is how it is checked headless: `audio()` gives the context's state (`suspended` means nobody has clicked yet, which is the usual reason for silence), whether it is unlocked, the master and every layer's gain, each voice with its layer, priority, slot, distance and the gain the listener would hear it at, the voices waiting without a slot (`virtual`, counted as `waitingLoops`), how many sit in the four-times-a-second grid, the grid's own passes, the bank (templates, samples ready and still coming, megabytes against the budget, what was missing or evicted), the interface table, and `recent`, the last dozen sounds asked for with what became of each (`no sound pack`, `no such template`, `refused: no voice`, `dropped: no buffer in time`, `recorded (advancing)`). `counts.refusedNoSlot` is voices refused, one apiece, and `refusedRetries` the times one waiting asked again. Each voice also says where it stands in relation to the ear's own room (`space`, the building and cell it is in, `no place` for a bed or anything else that stands wherever the ear does, `not in the world` for the interface and the music bus), whether it is being heard through a wall (`muffled`, counted as `muffledVoices`) and what it is sending to the room's echo (`echo`). `echo` itself says which room the game has named (`room`, the interior table's own type, 7 in the tall halls), the console's override where one is set (`forcedRoom`), whether the ear's space is inside anything (`inside`), which of the two impulses is carrying it (`using`, null out of doors or with the setting off), their lengths in seconds, how many voices are in it and whether any send is really open (`sending`); `headphones` says whether the setting is on, whether the silent warm-up has been pushed through once this session (`warmedAsked` — the browser's own impulse load is asynchronous and nothing in script can see it finish, and switching the setting on during play already forces it through every slot that exists, so what the warm-up really covers is the boot case), and `disagreeing`, the slots left placing sound by the other rule, which must be 0. An object retunes the invented numbers live: `distance` (`audible`, `fadeShare`, `flatMin`, `flatReach`, `zone`, `muffleGain`, `muffleHz`, `muffleEase`), `grid` (`cell`, `far`, `rate`) and `mixer` (`writeRate`, `ramp`, `lookahead`, `retry`, `cutFade`, the echo's own `echoSend` — a pair, the ordinary room then the tall hall — `echoEase`, and `echoRoom`, which is not an amount but an override standing in for the interior table's own number); a `distance` or `grid` change files every looping sound again, so it takes at once, while `voices` and the two echo lengths (`mixer.echo`, read when the graph is built) need a reload. `sound(id)` plays one of the game's templates at the player, at a point, or with no place at all (`sound(id, false)`). `soundGain(category, x)` is the per-category multiplier over the game's own volumes, for settling how the very quiet one-shot beds read against their bed (no argument lists them). `audioSelfTest()` builds a second mixer of the same class in an offline context, plays a template of its own through it and checks the rendered peak against the gain the template, the category, the Effects slider and the master ask for, which needs neither a click nor the sound pack: `started` 0 means the mixer never began it, and `recent` says why |
| `ambience()`, `ambience({ dayFade: 5, roomFade: 0.2 })`, `ambience({ weatherParticles: true })` | The sound of the place, headless: which of the sky's own environment rows are playing as beds and at what share of the frame (the same weights the sky draws those rows at, so a bed's weight and `sky().mix` must agree), the day fraction the day and night beds are crossfaded by, how much of the frame the room you are in has taken (`insideShare`) and which of the interior table's rows that is, how many rows the table gave (the shared one and the planet's own), the planet's placed sound objects (`emittersInPack` is how many its pack holds and `sources.placed` how many joined the world, which differ only at the cap; `placedNear` is how many are within earshot, `placedLive` how many hold a voice and `placedSilent` how many the bank could not start, with `missingSounds` naming those; `inARoom` is how many stand in a building the streamer has put down and `lookingForARoom` how many are still to find one), the loops its particle effects name (`effectLoops`), any bed the sky's own rows name that the bank has no entry for (`bedsMissingFromBank`, which on the retail archives is one outpost and the four trail zones of one planet), and each weather channel's own sound with its weight. Both are read by every browser, with or without a server and whether or not it plays the story, raw frame on a planet (as `/loc`, a story's `near` and an area are written) and the game's own in a space zone, `y` absolute; a row is filed under `place/<id>`, which a story object names as its `template` with nothing in the story changed. `placements()` reports each layer (`source` `file` or `none`, its `problem` when the whole file was refused, its rows, how many stand, how many are `skipped` and why: `badRow`, `clash` (the other layer holds the id, and the world edit wins), `noPack`, `noModel`, `copies` (nothing stands in the dungeons' world of copies), `space` (no building in a space zone)), the fields a row carried that this build does not read, and every row with its layer, key, name, place in the file's frame and, standing, `solid` (its colliders this instant) and `tierLoaded`; `refused` names each row the reading skipped and why. `{ reload: true }` reads both files again and stands only what moved (the dev server does the same by itself the moment either file is saved, `placements:changed`); `{ go: '<id>' }` puts you beside a row; `{ snippet: '<id>' }` prints a story object and an area naming a row, ready to paste into a story; `{ snippet: 'example' }` prints a ready-made world-edit file round where you stand (a gallery house ahead and a props crate in its first room) to save as the file it names, which must not be committed unless it is meant to ship; `{ tune }` moves `DRESSING_TUNE`, every number ours: `floraWaitMs` (1500, how long the ground waits for the rows' flora circles), `standWaitMs` (6000, how long a loading screen and the seats wait for the rows to be filed, and the loading screen, within the same wait, for those filed into tiers already standing to be built), `nearReach` (6 m, within which O says a permanent thing is part of this world), `guard` (1.5 m round a story-layer row, past its own footprint -- its model's box turned the way it stands, so a house's back rooms are guarded as well as its doorstep -- in which a prop of yours is refused as part of this world, read live so it moves rows already standing; a world edit is guarded only where its row says `guard`) and `drift` (0.5 m, how far the ground under a row standing in the open may have moved before the editor's list says so: against the ground the row was saved on, which the editor writes as `ground` on every row it saves in the open, so a building raised or sunk on purpose is never flagged for it; a row written by hand with no `ground` is taken to stand on the ground, either way under a building, risen over a prop). A building that stands in play takes what was put down in its rooms before it, so a story prop in a world edit's house is drawn with its rooms. **The editor, on the dev server alone** (a release answers that it is the dev server's, and carries none of it): the Props and Housing tabs each have a Yours or Permanent switch, which starts on Yours every session and is never kept across a reload; on Permanent a thing taken from either tab is saved to the session's file (the story's dressing the first time), and the tab lists what this world stands for good of its kind, nearest first, each with its layer, why it is not standing, and whether the ground under it has moved, with Go to, Pick up, Delete (asked, its answer shut for `HOUSING_TUNE.armMs`), Rename, a name and a note, Solid (the row's own word, else the default it stands by: a seat or a table passable, the rest solid; an absent word stays absent until it is turned) and a copy of a story object or an area naming it, which goes to the clipboard or, where the page may not write it, the console; the Housing tab adds every gallery building with rooms, which a double-click takes in hand by its model, deed or not. L in hand (`placeLayer`, borrowed from the saber's on and off, which is not read while something is in hand; the Controls page of a release does not list it) moves where the thing is kept round: yours, the story's dressing, the world edits; a permanent row picked up goes between the two files only, since a permanent thing never becomes yours (Delete, O again or the bar's Put away takes it out of its file, on a second press within `confirmMs`, the first asking in place of the bar's verdict; while a row is in hand the list changes nothing of it, since its save writes the row the hand carries). A label at the top of the screen names the file whenever it is not yours (with nothing in hand, while the switch is on Permanent), the placing bar wears the same badge, and the ghost is drawn in the accent where it may stand. The first save to a file in a session and a refused spot (placed anyway) each wait for a second click within `confirmMs` (2500 ms, `PERMANENT_EDIT_TUNE`, ours), the question in place of the bar's verdict, and every save says its file and its id on the message line. O takes the nearer of yours and the permanent props, then your own doorstep, then a permanent building's origin within the doorstep's reach, each turned and anchored where it stood; Escape stands a permanent row again from its file, and a travel or the select screen leaves it as its file has it. A building kept for good may be raised or sunk 30 m (`GHOST_TUNE.mostPermanent`) and needs no deed's world, nor a deed's footprint (one taken by its model is judged on its own box and draws no grid), and a prop going to a file is not held off the story's guards. Each save is the dev server's own endpoint, which takes asks from this machine and the game's own page alone and writes one row at a time: it reads the file as it stands, writes back every other row exactly as it read it (fields this build does not read included, and a file it cannot read whole is refused rather than written over), puts a file in place whole through a dot-named copy beside it that never outlives the write, takes a file away when its last row goes (and a folder that leaves empty), and answers both layers as they read now, which are stood at once; the dev server's word that the file changed follows a moment later and finds nothing left to stand (the dev server answers the page's ask for either file from the disk as it stands, and one that is not there with a 404). Its own numbers are `PLACEMENTS_ENDPOINT_TUNE` (`bodyMax` 262144 bytes, and `renameTries` 6 times `renameWaitMs` 50 ms for a file another program holds open), which the report's `editor.endpoint` prints as this build has them; the dev server loads it at the first save and runs that code until it is restarted. The report's `editor` says where the switch and the destinations stand, the hand's source and destination, the question waiting for its second click, whether a save is out, what the editor last said and what the label says; `prop(undefined, { drop: true })` and `deed(undefined, { drop: true })` are the click, answering `said` (the question, or the file saved to) and `dropped`, and `prop(undefined, { take: true })` the pick-up key. Nothing the editor writes is committed by it: a file under `public/placements/` reaches players once it is committed and merged to main, and an uncommitted one stops a local release pack. A row may carry a world state, `state: 'restuss=ruins'` (`<key>=<value>`, lower-case words; any other shape is kept out of the row with a note and the row stands in every state), and then stands only while its world stands that way and is filed nowhere otherwise (`otherState`, never taken out of its file): on Rori the editor's list gives each row a **State** button that goes round every state, Restuss living only and Restuss in ruins only, which is how rubble placed by hand stands in the ruins alone; `npm run story:check` warns of a story object naming a row kept to one state |

R held turns it with the mouse moving up and down, F held raises it, Shift with R tips and rolls a prop, the wheel with either held steps to the grid). `placeHold()` reports the numbers, what a held key is doing now (`hold`: `turn`, `tumble` or `lift`) and the gain its last frame ran at, and `anchored`, whether a prop picked back up still stands where it stood. `{ tune }` moves them: `turnPerPx` (0.15° a pixel at a slow hand), `liftPerPxHouse` and `liftPerPxProp` (0.005 and 0.003 m), `knee` and `fast` (400 and 2000 px/s, between which the rate rises to `most`, 4, times the base), `sideways` (off: a sideways drag spins it too, mouse right clockwise), `flipTurn` and `flipLift` (mouse up turns it clockwise seen from above and raises it; flipped, the other way), `gridTurn`, `gridLiftHouse` and `gridLiftProp` (the wheel's notch with a key held: 15°, 0.25 m, 0.1 m), `dtFloor` (0.004 s, the shortest frame a hand's speed is worked out over), `anchorMove` and `anchorTurn` (0.3 m and 3°, how far the player walks or looks before a picked-up thing leaves its spot) and `barClickPx` (4, how far a press on the bar's ⟲ or ↕ may move and still be a click, a notch, rather than a drag). `{ drag: { which, dy, dx, ms, shift, kind } }` runs the arithmetic alone over that drag in frames of a sixtieth of a second and says how far it would turn, tip, roll or lift; the real path is `advance(0.2, ['KeyR'], true)` and then `mouse(0, -5)` with `advance(1 / 60, ['KeyR'], true)` a frame at a time, which `deed()`'s `yaw` and `prop()`'s `turn` then read |
| `story()`, `story({ chunkGap: 250, syncWait: 5000, wpPerSecond: 3, offerChunk: 12000 })`, `story({ repeatEv: 2000, killDedupe: 30000, sweep: 1000 })`, `await story({ tests: true })`, `await story({ reload: true })` | The character's story book (`src/story/`): who holds it (`host`: `local` with no server, an old relay, or a server whose greeting carries no story; `server` while a server whose greeting carries one holds it; `held` while the book is being settled after a claim, or the line that held it is coming back, when nothing may change it), whether the line holds a story at all (`storyLine`), whether it is `settled`, a `sync` waited on (`settling`), a book going up (`offering`, pieces sent of pieces) or coming down (`receiving`, characters in), waypoint words waiting their turn (`queued`), the book's revision (`rev`), the server revision it last matched (`base`), how many changes it holds that no server has seen (`local`, which is what the character's change counter reads), its waypoints (`waypoints`, `on`, `trackWp`), the conversation nodes and answers it has heard (`heard`) and the people met (`met`; `npcs` has each with when they were met and named), Standing and Trust on each track (`tracks`, which an answer under pressure moves), and `stats`: syncs, offers and pieces sent, books taken down, `taken` (this browser's copy taken by the server), copies `setAside` (played in two places: the server's stood and this one is kept in storage under `swg.story.<character>.aside.<n>`, the last three), batches applied, `gaps` (a batch out of turn, which asks for the book again), refusals and the last reason. On a server an operation answers `sent` and its outcome arrives as the server's batch, view or refusal, so call it again to read it; the story's `clock` is then the server's, moved for every character on it, since it is its one clock, and `list` says the sets are the server's own (its status page names them). `quests` is every job the book holds with its state, run, completions, outcome and each step's state and count; `host` the host's own report (as under `story().quests` or `story().server`); `view` what the view carries just now (the jobs tracked, every watch by kind, the jobs' waypoints and the story objects); `tracker` what the jobs followed are showing, line by line as they read, with their writes; `stands` where each story object was found in the world (null while it is not); and `results`, what came of each operation asked. `grant`, `offer`, `accept`, `decline`, `drop`, `restart`, `unstick`, `track` and `untrack` take a job's id (`test:goto`, or `goto` when only one set has it); `complete` finishes every step of a job that is running, or only the `step` named, as an action would. `clock` moves the story's own clock on by that many milliseconds and sweeps at once -- the way to let a timer run out in a tab that draws no frames, since the story keeps the shared clock and never the frame's -- and `null` puts it back. `list` adds every job the sets hold, with its givers and its state. `reload` reads the sets again (this browser's, or the server's). `remote` moves `REMOTE_TUNE` (`evPerSecond`, words a second sent to a server, 8 under its 10; `queueMax`, the most that wait their turn, 60). `tracker` moves `TRACKER_TUNE` (`max` and `linesMax` are read when the tracker is built, `hz`, `nearRound`, `farFrom`, `farRound`, `secondsUnder`, `autoTrack`) and `stands` moves `STAND_TUNE` (`find`, how far round an object's point its thing is looked for, `retry`, `rise`), all ours. A name with no prefix is a console signal (`debug:<name>`), which only the test set may wait on; answers the signal raised and whether the story could take it (`why` when it could not: no set read yet, no character, or the book held while it settles). On a server that runs the jobs it goes to the server, which takes it from its admin alone |
