Stand a type or a family ahead of the view (a hostile one goes for your ship; on foot they leave you alone until you hit one). **Any garage ship hull as an NPC** (`src/space/hullNpc.ts`, the NPC tab's "Any ship as an NPC" list does the same): `npcShip(<garage id>, { faction, tier, count })` stands one, or three in the side's formation, flying for `imperial`, `rebel`, `blacksun`, `pirate` or `neutral` (never `player`; neutral unless said) at a tier from 1 to 10 (1 unless said; above 5 read through the ladder below), as a type of ours made from the hull's own fields: the game's name for an NPC type drawn on it, else the garage's words; the taunt table the converter gives a type of that side and family; the tier chassis's fit where an NPC type it draws has one (its parts picked at the tier asked, as a typed ship's), else its stock fit; stock paint, no droid, its rooms hidden and nobody aboard; and it answers what it stood, its fit and how far ahead, or why not (the cap, no combat file, no such hull, a side or tier refused). Naming a side asks for the hull even where a family has its name (`'xwing'`), and a name no type or family has is taken as a hull. A hull bigger than a fighter has its group's slots spread by its own radius over `slotFits` (17 m, about the largest typed hull's, which the formation tables fit) and is stood further out than asked when its sphere, its nearest wingman's included, would come within `aheadClear` (50 m, the typed path's own floor) of you, so a fighter stands where a typed one would and a Star Destroyer past its own 1.5 km; `{ hulls: true }` prints both and an object moves them for the next group stood (both ours, both spacing rather than feel: a corvette's slots at the tables' 25 m stood three hulls in each other). Such a group is drawn as far off as its hull looks as large as a fighter does at the 4 km every other NPC ship is drawn to, and never nearer than its members can roam on their leader's route, and its pilots chase as many times further from where it was stood than 4 km as its hull is a fighter's size (17 m), so a Star Destroyer patrol is drawn and fights whole; a group listed by `npcShips()` says its `spread`, `drawReach` and `leash` when they are not a fighter group's. Every NPC pilot measures its sight (1.5 km in space) and its gun's reach from the part of each hull that stands out past a 17 m sphere toward the other (its box that way, a Star Destroyer's length off its bow and its height over its deck), which is nought for every one of the game's own NPC hulls, so they see and fire exactly as before, while a Star Destroyer now fires on what comes near and is fired on: from off its bow and stern, since the pilots' break-off still keeps them off its whole 1.3 km sphere and a fighter's bolts do not reach its side from there. The flown ship's fight (or with `target: true` the target's): stats, condition, components with grades, what is down and the top speed now; strike it through a layer or a slot, repair it, make it take nothing. Show an NPC pilot's line. `{ ladder: true }` prints the ladder above tier 5 (`src/space/tierLadder.ts`: SOE's own families run to tier 10 from publish 55 on): the tier whose component grade, pilot skill and strength each of tiers 6 to 10 is read at, tier 5's for all three until the owner moves them, and the highest tier a type may carry (`top`); an object moves any of them (a tier from 6 to 10 read as a tier from 1 to 5), and tiers 1 to 5 are never touched. The skill reaches every ship already in the air at once; the grade (its fit) and the strength (how hard it is to kill and how hard it hits) are made when a ship is stood, so they reach the next one stood (`npcShips({ ladder: { strength: { 10: 4 } } })`, then `await npcShip('xwing', { tier: 10 })`). The same ladder is sliders in the debug menu when `npcShips` is picked (its Tuning group; `src/space/tierDials.ts`): a slider a tier of each row and one for the top, each set through `tuneTierLadder` as the console sets it, and the line under them is the `npcShips({ ladder })` call that sets what was moved again, to copy or pin at start. Every number of the fight is invented (`shipStats.ts`, `npcBrain.ts`, `loadout.ts`, `roster.ts`, `tierLadder.ts`) |

| `__debug.session()` | who this browser is to a server and what it has made of the line: `mode` (`off`, `waiting`, `relay`, `server`), `authority` (`me` or `server`: everything new asks this, never the socket), the public `player` name, the `character` in play and its change `counter`, how the server settled it (`keep`), a tie waiting on you (`ask`), whether another browser took the character (`taken`), why a server turned this one away (`denied`, in the server's own words), `refused` (why the server would not give this browser the character it asked for, with the line left open so another can be chosen), `serverVersion` (which version of the language the far end speaks), `story` (which story its greeting says it holds: 0 for none, which leaves the story book with this browser), `props` (1 when its greeting says it keeps a player's props, which then are the server's while it holds the claim; 0 leaves them this machine's), `homes` (2 when its greeting says it moves a house where it stands; 0 leaves the pick-up key at a house's door opening the Housing tab's take-down, as before), and whether damage between players is on where you are (`friendlyFire`). `__debug.session({ hailWait: 2000 })` moves the one number this side invents: how long the browser waits for a server to speak first before deciding it is talking to the relay that came before (1000 ms). The clock is not here; it is `__debug.day()`'s. |
| `__debug.group()` | the fireteam as this browser holds it: `active` (false with no server, and then nothing is sent and the map offers no Group tab), the `roster` with each member's id, name, world, health and distance, whether you `leader`, an `invite` or a `trip` waiting, and how many words have been sent, said and heard. `__debug.group({ chevron: 60, lookAngle: 0.4, lookLift: 1.2, distanceHz: 4, bubbleSeconds: 6, log: 40, chars: 200, linesPerSecond: 4, max: 8 })` moves this side's own numbers, and `__debug.group({ ui: { chevrons: false, chevronLift: 2.6, hz: 4 } })` the roster's, `hz` taking effect at once even mid-group. The roster's `frames` counts the loop that carries the chevrons, which runs only while somebody is actually far enough off to have one — in a group where nobody is, it should not move at all. The distances it prints under `ranges` are the game's own radial menu table — an invitation 90 m, a trade 8 m, a duel 128 m, a death blow 10 m — and cannot be set from here. `max` and the roster's pools are read once, when it is built. The roster is the map's Group tab: the full stop (`.`, the `group` binding) opens the map on it and shuts the map pressed on it, `map('group')` does the same from here, the map's own Escape and close button shut it, and the map holds the mouse and stops play while it shows; its `open` says whether the map is showing it. With no server the tab is not there and the key does nothing at all. |
| `__debug.chat()` | the chat line and the bubbles over people's heads: whether the line is `open` and on which `scope`, how many `bubbles` are up, every `writes` this side has made to the page (sample it a second apart for the rate — a screen with nobody talking writes none), the `frames` its loop has run and whether it is `running` (it stops itself when the last bubble goes), and the `log` of the last lines said. `__debug.chat({ open: true, scope: 'group' })` opens the line without a keyboard and `__debug.chat({ send: '/who' })` types a line into it; `__debug.chat({ bottomPx: 200, bubbleWidth: 260, bubbleLift: 2.35, bubbleRange: 60, fade: 0.25, fadeSteps: 8, escapeHoldMs: 400, toMessageLine: false })` moves the invented numbers — `escapeHoldMs` is how long the game's keys stand aside after Escape has shut the line, which is what keeps that one press from also opening the game's menu, and the last stops chat also going to the message line once the display grows a log of its own. `pool` is read once, when the line is built. |
| `__debug.combat()` | shots, hits and health between players: `active` (false with no server, and then no bolt crosses and nobody can be hurt), `friendlyFire` (whether the server has damage between players switched on), `copies` (pictures of other people's bolts in the air here), `sent` and `heard` shots, `held` (shots not sent because this browser was firing faster than they may cross), `refused` (pictures not flown because there were already as many as there may be), `plain` (bolts drawn as a plain bolt because this browser had never loaded that weapon's own effect, which should climb once per weapon a stranger carries and then stand still), `recoloured` (bolts drawn in the plain colour because as many colours as there may be have already come off the wire), `hits` claimed and `hurts` taken, `blocks`, this player's own `hp` and whether they went over as `down`, and the `duels` in progress. `__debug.combat({ shotsPerSecond: 20, copies: 96, colours: 64, healthHz: 5, healthStep: 0.01, blameSeconds: 8 })` moves this side's own numbers (`healthHz` is held at ten and under, which is how often health is looked at; `shotSpace`, which names a shot, is read-only because moving it would rename every bolt in the air). The duel is the game's own word at the game's own 128 m (printed as `duelRange`, not settable): `__debug.combat({ ask: <a player's relay id> })` asks for one, `{ accept: true }` takes one up, `{ decline: true }` says no and `{ peace: true }` ends every one, which is how a script with no keyboard tries the whole path. |
| `__debug.together()` | going where the group goes: `active` (false with no server, and then no trip is offered and none can be taken), `following` (the world this browser is crossing to with the group, empty otherwise), `places` (how many members have said where they came out), `offered` and `taken` trips, words `sent` and `heard`, `lastWhy` (why the last trip taken up did nothing — already there, standing in somebody else's rooms, already crossing), `lastTrip` (the last world this browser told the group it was crossing to, with the point on it when it named one), `waited` (how long the last crossing waited under the loading screen for the leader's place, in milliseconds) and `hadPlace` (whether one ever came). `__debug.together({ spreadSpace: 150, spreadGround: 6, slots: 8, waitMs: 8000, keepMs: 120000, places: 12 })` moves this side's own numbers — every one of them is ours, not the game's: how far to one side of the leader a member comes out in space and on the ground, how many places there are in the ring, how long a crossing waits for the leader's word before arriving where its own world would have put it, how long a reported place is worth standing beside (and how long a trip nobody arrived from is held), and how many places are remembered. `__debug.together({ offer: true })` offers the group the world this browser is on and `{ take: true }` takes a waiting offer up without the panel, which is how a script with no mouse answers one; a trip this browser could not keep is refused before the yes goes out and says why, so no acceptance is spent on it. A hidden tab throttles its timers, so set `waitMs` to 0 in a script that drives a crossing. |

The world's own lairs, nests and people at their posts, which every browser stands for itself from the same data, are shared by saying so once a server that speaks of them answers: `seeding` says whether this browser does, `seen` and `unseen` count the words said that it has stood one or put one down, `seenWaiting` how many are waiting their turn under the allowance, `local` how many it was told to keep to itself because the world held as many as it may (600), `keptSeen` and `keptStood` split what it keeps into those and the ones an admin stood, and `seenDown` how many are dead just now and waiting out their own respawn. `__debug.owned({ asksPerSecond: 2 })` sets one of this side's own manners, `{ seenPerSecond: 8 }` how many `seen` words may go in any `seenSpan` seconds and `{ seenSpan: 2 }` that span (1.25 s, a sliding window a quarter of a second longer than the server's own second, and under its own 20, so none is dropped there whichever second it counts) and `{ seenWaiting: 64 }` how many may wait, and `{ deathGrace: 10 }` how long after a creature has been taken off this browser it may still say that one died; the rule's own numbers — 180 m, a quarter nearer, three seconds, and the server's own matching grace — are the server's and are printed by `http://localhost:8787/` under `own`. What stands in a world is what its data puts there and what an admin stands: the planet's one species stood in a ring round the arrival point is gone, switch and all. |

| `__sharedDay()`, `__debug.day()`, `__debug.day({ length: 3600 })`, `__debug.day({ length: null })` | the clock the world shares and what the day is doing with it: `clock.where` is `off` (no server address is set, so the day and the weather are this machine's wall clock, exactly as they always were), `shared` (a server is answering) or `adrift` (there was one and the socket has gone, so the last offset is kept and the day carries on at its own rate rather than jumping); `clock.offsetMs` is the offset in force and `clock.aimMs` the one it is walking to; `clock.weatherLagMs` is how far the weather's own reading of that clock is still behind it, and `clock.refused` counts the messages thrown away for carrying a clock or a delay that could not be true; `day.time` is the time of day and `day.shared` what the shared clock says it should be. The day is put right outright only where nothing was drawn — a loading screen, or frames that have been away longer than `gapSeconds` — and is otherwise walked to: a drift of less than `snapAt` of a day at between `minRate` and `ease` times its own rate, never backwards and never standing still, and anything larger by the shorter way round at `catchUp` times its own rate, which is seconds rather than a frame. The weather reads a walked copy of the same clock, so a correction never turns the wind in one frame. `__sharedDay({ release: true })` hands the day back after `__debug.time`, the room air's sun aiming or anything else that writes `day.time` has moved it — while one has, the display says `Time of day set from the console` and the day is its own; a server greeting the browser for the first time, and being told there is no server, hand it back by themselves. `__sharedDay({ ease, minRate, snapAt, catchUp, gapSeconds, phase, phaseSpread, pingSeconds, samples, easeMsPerSecond, snapMs, maxRttMs, weatherEaseMsPerSecond, weatherEaseMaxMs })` tunes it live; every one of those numbers is ours, not the game's. The limits it reports rather than tunes (the earliest and latest clock a server may claim, and the longest day) reject what cannot be true. `{ length: 3600 }` makes the whole day an hour long (any length from 1 s to a real day; `day.gameLengthSeconds` is the game's own 720, and `{ length: null }` puts it back), with no server: the day is walked by the frame's step over the length, so the sun's pace changes from the next frame and the hour never jumps, and how much of it is night stays the client's own share. While the shared clock is in use (a server answering, or one gone and its offset kept) the day is the server's: the world's admin asks the server for it (a `day` word, to a server on the third wire), which turns the day where it stands for everybody at once -- anchored at the hour it has reached, so nobody's sun moves -- and tells every browser on every world, writes it down so a restart keeps it, and hands it to everybody who arrives later in their greeting; the weather's own clock is not touched, so its schedule does not jump either. Anybody else is refused in words, and so is everybody against an older relay; the server's own start still sets it too (`npm run relay -- --day=<seconds>`, which stands for that run over what the admin last set). A server met later keeps a length set here as this browser's own and gives it back when it goes. The answer's `said` is what was done or why not, and `clock.dayAt` and `clock.dayFrom` are the anchor the server's last change of length left (0 for none). |
| `__aboard()`, `__aboard({ carrier: 3 })`, `__aboard({ carrier: 0 })`, `__aboard({ glideSeconds: 0.2 })` | Standing in a hull somebody else flies. The report says whether this browser's state is sending a vehicle of its own (`veh`), the hull it is in (`in`), or the hull the knob has been told to claim (`in (forced)`), what the last one carried and how long ago, and, for every other player who says they are in somebody's hull, whose, whether that hull is here to put them in, and where in it they are standing. A passenger sends no copy of the hull: whoever flies it sends it, so two people crewing one ship are drawn as one hull with two people in it rather than two hulls in the same place. `carrier` is a test override and is 0 in play: set to another player's relay id it reports the hull you are really standing in as theirs, so the whole path can be tried with two browsers before a friend's hull is a place you can board; it holds only while that player is really here, so a line that drops or a travel hands your own ship back by itself. `glideSeconds` is how fast a passenger's place in the hull's frame eases to each message. Both numbers are ours, not the game's (`ABOARD_TUNE` in `src/net/remotePlayers.ts`). |
| `__board()`, `__board({ reach: 12 })`, `__board({ wait: 5 })` | Boarding a hull somebody else flies. `builder` and `watcher` say that this browser can make one into a place to stand in and that something is listening for one going — both read `registered` in every browser, including one playing alone, since they are wired up at boot; what decides whether there is anything to board is whether another player is here with a ship that has rooms, which `__rooms()` lists per player. `reach` is how near the side of their hull you must stand for **E** to board it, and `wait` is how long boarding waits for their rooms to be built before it gives up and says so. Both are ours, not the game's (`BOARD_TUNE` in `src/space/docking.ts`). |
| `__peers()`, `__peers({ on: 0 })`, `__peers({ fight: 0 })`, `__peers({ shipPad: 1 })` | The other players as things you can hit. The report says whether the bodies are bound to a physics world at all, how many peers there are and how many bodies they hold between them, whether anything is listening for a blow (`hurts`), and a row per peer: their relay id and name, the key they hold in the one list of the living, whether their figure and the hull they ride each have a body, whether anything here may pick a fight with them (`fights`), whether the world's creatures may (`hunted`; `prey` at the top counts them), their health as their own browser last said it, whether they are down, and where they are standing. A peer's body is a sensor in a collision group of its own that collides with nothing, so a bolt, a blade and a sweep find them while the character controller, a ship's set-down probe, the weather's roof grid and the ground under a speeder all pass over them; nothing here ever takes health off anybody, since the player who was shot is the only one who does that. The world's creatures fight them whenever a server carries their blows, whatever the players' own switch says: a bite lands on their screen as the keeper says, the players' own rule with the keeper where the shooter stands. They join the one list of the living only while a blow struck here could reach their own browser — damage between players switched on where you are, or a duel — because nothing here could ever finish them and the local wildlife would otherwise chase them for ever. `on: 0` takes every body down, which is the baseline to compare a frame against; `fight: 0` leaves the bodies up and takes the peers off that list; `shipPad` grows the box round a hull by that many metres. `shipPad`, `on` and `fight` are ours (`PEER_BODY_TUNE` in `src/net/remoteBodies.ts`); the capsule and the middle of the figure are the player's own. A change to the capsule shows on bodies made after it, not on the ones already standing. `ropeHulls` lists whose vehicles the grappling hook may bite and ride (the relay ids of the peers whose shown ride has a box and a heard state, never a mount or a hull docked onto another), which the hook reads as a list made once a frame, after the peers are glided, and stamped with the clock the frame read at its start (the game hands it over; nothing on the peers' path reads a clock). |
| `__rooms()`, `__rooms({ board: 3 })`, `__rooms({ drop: 3 })`, `__rooms({ on: 0 })`, `__rooms({ step: 0 })`, `__rooms({ keepSeconds: 20 })`, `__rooms({ minStep: 0 })` | A hull somebody else flies, as a place you can stand in. The report says whether it is bound to a world at all, how long ago the rooms were last stepped (`sweptMsAgo`, the one number that says they are being simulated at all in a tab that draws nothing), a row per other player whose ride could be boarded (their relay id and name, whether their hull is drawn here, whether its picture says which vehicle it is), and a row per hull that has been made a place: how many rooms it has, how many room lights, whether the rooms are a model of its own or cells of the hull, whether somebody is standing in it, whether it has been cut loose from a hull that has gone, where the way in is, where the controls are, how big the rooms are and where the hull is now. `board` builds that peer's rooms (a load: the report on the next call says what it found); `drop` takes them down again, except under somebody standing in them, who is always taken out first. `on: 0` refuses to build any and takes down the ones that are up there and then, which is the baseline to compare a frame against; `step: 0` stops stepping the rooms' own physics; `keepSeconds` is how long a room is kept after its hull stops being drawn, so that a jump does not cost a rebuild; `minStep` is the shortest gap between two sweeps, which is how one sweep a frame is kept to one however many callers ask for it. Every number is ours (`REMOTE_ROOM_TUNE` in `src/net/remoteInterior.ts`); nothing is built until something asks, so with no server this holds nothing but the hook itself. |
| `__peerBlades()`, `__peerBlades({ borrow: 'always' })`, `__peerBlades({ swing: 0 })` | The other players' lightsabers. Each peer with a blade out is listed with the colour they chose, whether they are down, whether the blade is in the air rather than in their hand, and, per blade, which hand it is in (`right`, `left`, `staff`, `thrown`), whether it is glowing, how far out it is and how far off you are. `renderersMade` counts every blade renderer ever built for a peer: it must climb when somebody first lights one and then stand still, since a second one would mean a renderer is being built and thrown away every frame; `spare` is how many are waiting for the next peer, since a peer who leaves hands theirs on rather than disposing materials the portal renderer and the shadow cascades are holding (`forgets` says whether the game gave it the way to take them out of those sets, without which nothing is disposed at all). `borrowedLastFrame` counts the pooled flash lights peers' blades took. With the blade glow pass on it is 0: they light the world through that pass, exactly as your own blade does, and ask for no light. With the pass off they take only a light standing dark, and since your own blade, the fighters and a ship's room lights all ask earlier in the frame and hold theirs for the whole flash, a peer's blade is often unlit then -- which is the price of never taking one of yours away. `borrow: 'always'` asks whatever the pool is doing and, being last in the frame, takes the light your own blade or a cabin is using: for comparing by eye, not for playing with. `swing` is how hard a peer is taken to be swinging, which only lengthens the smear behind a blade already moving; how hard they really are is not on the wire. Every one of those numbers is ours, not the game's (`PEER_BLADE_TUNE` in `src/net/remoteBlades.ts`), and one below nothing is refused rather than floored. |
| `particles(30, true)`, `water()`, `await waterFx({ env: 'shader' })`, `await waterFx({ view: 'envOnly' })`, `flora()`, `cell()`, `scene()`, `player()` | Effects; the status line counts effects carried by particles and says how many were skipped at the caps, and a carrier emitter lists as texture none with its attachments and how many it carries; the water surface's height at a point; every water body with the shader it came from, its colour, opacity, ripple, drift, what it reflects and whether it is on screen (`waterFx({ env: 'shader' })` makes every body reflect its own shader's cube map, `{ env: 'sky' }` the area's day and night map, `{ envIntensity }` changes how hard it mirrors); with the effects on, `waterFx({ view })` shows what the reflections trace (`'confidence'`: red traced, blue fallback), add (`'added'`), fall back to (`'envOnly'`, which must look exactly like `postfx({ waterReflections: false })`) or trace alone (`'tracedOnly'`), `'off'` for the picture, and `{ maxDistance, thickness, thicknessPerMetre, minWeight, sky, strength }` tune the march; the result also says whether the pass drew and why not, and whether the mask holds its targets; flora, the portal cell you are in, the scene, the player's state |
| `sea()`, `sea(x, z)`, `sea({ on: false })`, `sea({ scale: 0.5 })`, `sea({ farFade: true })`, `sea({ solve: 0 })` | The **second** water reader, which only what floats consumes: the sea with the swell in it, so a hull on the open water bobs, pitches and rolls with the waves instead of riding a plane through the middle of them. `sea()` reports the numbers in force, the shader's own fade edges they lean on, the swell's reach on this planet and, in `why`, why nothing is bobbing when nothing is; `sea(x, z)` answers that reader at a point beside the flat one (`water(x, z)`). They agree exactly on a lake and in water under 0.3 m deep, and part over the open sea: the shader fades the swell in between, so a hull in 0.3 to 5 m of water bobs on a share of it (about half at 2.65 m) exactly as the drawn surface does. **What floats now includes bodies**: the player's swim line and every swimming creature's read this reader too and chase it (`afloat()` below), so a person and a rancor on the open sea ride the waves instead of holding still while the waves ride over them. Everything else in the game — the feet, the blade, the spawn heights, the splashes, the weather, the camera's own water line — keeps the flat table, which for a wading body is the right answer and for the rest is a thing nobody has asked for. The two rules that measure a floating hull's *own* height against the water read the sea too, because they have to: a hull's height now breathes, and on flat water `onWater` has only 0.85 m of headroom at a bike's ride height against crests of 1.08 m, so read against the flat table a hull would stop being "on water" exactly while it rode one. The shallows are flat only under 0.3 m of water: the shader's fade is `smoothstep(0.3, 5, depth)`, so a hull in three metres bobs by about half the sea. `on: false` puts the flat table back under every hull; `scale` is the bob's height without touching the picture (the near sea is 15 m a quad against waves of 7.8 m to 33.5 m, so the mesh draws about half the swell the hull is floating on — about 0.3 is what the one resolved wave alone contributes); `solve` is how many steps of the inverse are taken, since a wave carries the surface sideways as well as up (0 is 0.047 m r.m.s. out and 0.29 m at worst, 1 is 3 mm, and 1 is the default); `farFade` also mirrors the shader's camera-distance fade, off by default because the mesh goes flat past 900 m for want of vertices rather than for want of waves. All four are ours (`SEA_FEED` in `src/world/seaFeed.ts`); the wave arithmetic under them is `swellMath.ts`, pinned against the shader's own text |
| `afloat()`, `afloat({ track: 4 })`, `afloat({ damp: 0 })`, `afloat({ speed: 1 })` | How a body lies on water it is floating in — the player swimming and every creature whose pack has swimming clips, which share one spring (`AFLOAT` in `src/world/afloat.ts`). `afloat()` reports the numbers in force with where you are against your own float line right now: the flat table, the drawn surface over it, the swell between them, and `under`, how far beneath that surface you are lying. `under` should sit at the swim depth (1.1 m) and stay there while the wave moves; a figure that wanders is the spring losing the wave. `track` is how hard the body chases its line, and it is **8** rather than the 2.5 a swimming creature used to hold with — 2.5 is a fine number against a target that does not move, which is what it was tuned for, and it is 27 cm out of step on an eight-second swell a metre and a half trough to crest. `damp` takes some of the rise the body already has back out, and `speed` caps the chase so a surface that *jumps* — a travel, the sea being switched on, crossing from a lake to the open sea — swims the body up to its new line over a second or two instead of launching it. A flyer's hover is deliberately not this spring and keeps its old numbers. The swell itself is `sea()` above |
| `ripples()`, `ripples({ speed: 1.423 })`, `ripples({ draft: 0.5, impact: 1 })`, `ripples({ poke: [x, z] })`, `waterSurface()`, `waterSurface({ hide: 0 })`, `waterSurface({ most: 0.88, deep: 9 })`, `underwater()`, `underwater({ sight: 20 })`, `specks()`, `specks({ count: 400 })` | The water's height field and what is under the surface. `ripples()` reports the field (`carryMs` is how fast a ring is really seen to travel); `{ speed }` sets the coupling between neighbouring texels in metres a second (1.423 as it ships, the same at every detail setting; the grid caps it and says so once), `draft` is how hard a hull holds the surface down, `impact` how hard a fast arrival punches, and `{ poke: [x, z] }` drops a ring in at a world position. `waterSurface()` is how much the water's own surface hides of what is under it — uniforms on the one program every lake and sea wears, so it holds with the Effects setting off as well: `{ hide: 0 }` is the water exactly as it was, `most` how much of the bed shows through over deep water and `deep` how deep it must be before it hides anything. `underwater()` is the look under water — what the pass was given the last time it drew, what that came to and why it did not draw — with every key of `UNDERWATER_TUNE` moving live and a name it does not know said back in `ignored`; `specks()` is the specks drifting past the eye down there, whether they were drawn and why not, with their own knobs. Every number of all four is ours: the game had no under water at all |
| `lava()`, `lava({ intensity, glow, glowFrom, glowTo, axes })` | The lava drawn now: tables, and each look with where its textures came from (`client`, `partial` when a piece fell back, `stand-in` for a pack converted before the lava look); with an argument, tunes the look every lava material shares (brightness, how far the veins glow, which veins glow) or reads the client's texture coordinate the other way round (`axes: 'xzy'`, `'xyz'` restores) |
| `bladeGlow()`, `bladeGlow({ show: 'light' })`, `bladeGlow({ power: 3 })`, `bladeGlow({ wrap: 0.3, flat: 0, smooth: 0, keep: 0 })`, `bladeGlow({ smoothPx: 3, smoothTol: 0.4 })`, `bladeGlow({ occlusion: false })`, `bladeGlow({ flashes: true })`, `bladeGlow({ at: [x, y, z] })` | The light lit blades throw on what is around them: the blades counted now, whether the pass drew and why not, the light ceiling, the flash pool and the GPU time; the added light, normals or working box alone (`show: 'normals'`, `'rect'`, `null` back); live tuning of any `BLADE_GLOW_TUNE` number (the result's `tune` is what to bake); the wall march off; the old pooled lights back for comparison; and what a surface at a point gains from each blade. The pass shades with a normal rebuilt from the depth, which is each triangle's flat face, so five numbers are the retune that keeps a body, a column or a hull from showing its facets: `wrap` (0.8, where it was 0.3) and `flat` (the share of the light that ignores the facing, 0.3), `smooth` (how much of a normal smoothed over a 5 by 5 kernel is used, 1), `smoothPx` (pixels between its taps, 2) and `smoothTol` (a tap counts only while it lies within this sine of the pixel's own surface, so nothing is smoothed across a silhouette, 0.5), and `keep` (how much of the pixel's own brightness stays in the albedo, so a texture's detail shows, 0.3). `{ wrap: 0.3, flat: 0, smooth: 0, keep: 0 }` is the light as it was, to compare; `show: 'normals'` shows the smoothed normals. All of them are uniforms, so moving one compiles nothing, and all are ours |
| `torch()`, `torch({ on: true })`, `torch({ maxIrradiance: 4 })`, `torch({ ahead: 0.4, side: 0.25, rise: 0.1 })`, `torch({ maxIrradiance: 1e6 })` | The hand torch (F), which lights a building's rooms, a dungeon's and a ship's as well as the world, and a room seen through a doorway and the street seen out of one: `layers` is which of the portal renderer's passes light with it, read off the light's own layer mask, and should say `world and rooms` (the torch carries the actor layer as the flash pool does, set once as it is made, so the spot is in every room program's key from the first loading screen and turning it on or off builds nothing; on layer 0 alone, as it was, it lit nothing indoors). Whether it is on, whether it is held at the eye (first person and a cockpit) or carried at the head (third person), where it stood and what it pointed at on the last frame it was on, how far along the view the one ray a frame found something (`hit`), how far from the torch what it is aimed at is (`aimed`), its intensity, whether that was held back (`capped`) and the irradiance it puts there beside the sun's 2.4. In third person it is carried `ahead` of the head, `side` to its right and `rise` above the eyes and aimed at the crosshair's point, so its cone starts in front of the body and never lights the player's back; a crosshair point closer than `minAhead` in front of it (something between the camera and the head) is not aimed at. Its intensity is capped so what it is aimed at takes no more than `maxIrradiance` (7, a few suns), where at half a metre it took about 790; `{ maxIrradiance: 1e6 }` puts the old blinding torch back to compare. | `ssao()`, `ssao({ split: 0.5 })`, `ssao({ view: 'ao' })`, `ssao({ view: null, split: 0 })` | Ambient occlusion: the lights it read (`lights`), which set each region took (`lightSets`: `sky`, `rooms at 1` inside a building, or `rooms at 3` for rooms seen through a door from outside), whether water coverage was read, the values under the crosshair (`centre`: view depth, occlusion, ambient share, ceiling) and, while `fxTiming` is on, its GPU cost; `split: 0.5` shows the picture with occlusion on the left and without on the right; `view` shows the occlusion alone (`'ao'`: white open, grey shaded), `'fraction'` (how much of each surface's light is ambient: black sunlit, white shade), `'ceiling'` (the brightest a surface lit that way can be, on a log scale, mid-grey a ceiling of 1), `'region'` (where the rooms' lights are used, white, rather than the sky's) or `'multiplier'` (what the picture is multiplied by); `openBias`, `directShare`, `radius`, `power`, `fade: [start, end]`, `falloff`, `thin` and `region: false` tune it live for comparison, and null puts each back |
| `heat()`, `heat({ show: true })`, `heatPlume(10)` | The heat haze: what it drew last frame and why not (`product`, `sources`, `why`); tuning `show` (tints hot air cyan), `gate`, `clientOffset`, `fadeStart`, `fadeEnd`, `lift`, `plumeRange`, `minPlumePixels`, `lavaNoiseRate`, `plumeNoiseRate`; `heatPlume(seconds)` puts a plume 5 m ahead of the camera, crossing the view, for that long, to show the haze with no vehicle or gun; `fxView('heat')` shows the heat buffer (red its intensity, green and blue the noise) |

| `roomAir()`, `roomAir({ view: 'haze' })`, `roomAir({ showPrisms: true })`, `roomAir({ sunInto: true })` | The room's air: which building or ship, whether this frame is drawn from inside it, the doorways casting beams (size, reach, how squarely they face the sun, how much of each is sunlit), the lamps glowing and whether each is in sight, and the motes; numbers retune it live (`scatter`, `soft`, `noise`, `patch`, `lampSigma`, `haze`, `moteSize`, `moteShaftGain`, ...); `view: 'haze' \| 'patch' \| null` shows the beams and glow alone, or the sunlit patch mask (the patch needs `patch` above 0); `showPrisms` draws the beam volumes as wireframes; `freeze: true` holds the selection; `sunInto: true` sets the time of day so the sun shines straight into the nearest doorway; `fxView('lightShafts.haze')` shows the half-resolution haze target |

A number holds a level; `'rain'`, `'dust'` or `'snow'` forces a kind; `null` returns to the schedule. Objects: `{ level, kind, family, lifeDay, snap: true }` force (merged; all null is no force); `{ wetness, puddles, snowCover }` set at once; `{ timeScale: 60 }` or `{ skip: 30 }` (minutes) move the schedule's clock, and `{ timeScale: 1 }` puts it back on the shared wall clock; `{ find: 'moseisley' }` gives an area's centre for `teleport(x, z)`; `{ windHeading: 0 }` holds the wind (`null` frees it); `{ emitters: true }` adds the falling effects' emitter rows; `{ draw: false }` skips the weather pass to time it; `{ wrap: false }` stores that world materials skip the wet wrap (reload to apply). `sky()` now also shows `mix`, `gradients`, `clouds`, `shadows` and `wind` |
| `movers()`, `movers(false)`, `motionProbe()`, `motionProbe(x, y)`, `motionTune({ nearCut: [8, 25] })`, `fxView('velocity')`, `fxView('motionBlur.field')`, `fxView('motionBlur.tiles')` | The motion blur on moving things: every mover listed this frame with what it drew and why not (`all`, `animated`, `still`, `small`, `budget`, `fresh`, `jump`, `culled`, `hidden`), the draws against their caps, the fourteen warm shader variants and any made since (`warm`, `ready`, `failed`), `followFar` and the static cut; `motionProbe` draws a frame and reads the blur's radius field under the crosshair (or at a pixel from the top left): the radius either side in pixels, the view depth, whether a mover drew there, and the cut; `motionTune` retunes the near cut, the longest radius (`radiusOfHeight`), the gather's taps (`samples`) and the mover limits for this session; the views show the velocity product (movers coloured on black, blue the moving weight), the resolved radius field and the tile neighbourhood's maximum (hue the direction, brightness the length over 32 px) |
| `dof()`, `dof({ view: 'coc' })`, `dof({ force: true })` | Depth of field when aiming: draws one frame, then the focus under the crosshair (`metres`, `samples`, `amount`; null while the lens is not drawing, and `metres` null before a surface has been under it this aim), the shooter's plane (`minFocus`, `fade`), the largest radius, the lens grid (`stride`, `tileReach`, `taps`, `fill`) and the glow depth (`glow.objects`). Views: `'coc'` (green in focus, red near, blue far), `'focus'`, `'tiles'`, `'blur'`, `'glow'` (magenta where a bolt, flash, burst or blade keeps its own depth), `null`. Live tuning, no recompile: `aperture`, `apertureStop`, `maxCoc`, `keepShooterSharp: false` (the physical look), `taps: 24`, `glowLevel`. `force: true` draws as if aimed without the shoulder camera. To aim headless: `advance(0.5, ['Mouse2'], true)`. |

| `previewDof()`, `previewDof({ view: 1 })` | The wardrobe and creator dolls' lens: which path each draws by (`direct` straight to the canvas, `lens` once zoomed in enough for more than a pixel of blur), the gate in pixels, the span, and whether both program variants are compiled. `{ on, strength }` for this session; `{ view: 1 }` forces the lens and shows its CoC (green face, blue behind). |

**Measuring a slow spot.** Only frames of play are measured, and typing into the console takes the mouse from the game, which puts the Escape menu up and pauses it; so every measurement below waits for you to go back to the game. In a visible window at your usual settings, open the console (F12) and undock it into a window of its own (the ⋮ at its top right, then the first of the "Dock side" icons), so the game keeps its full size while it is measured. Stand where the frame is slow and face the way it is slowest, at the zoom you play at. Then paste these into the console one at a time:

```
await __debug.perf({ mark: 'me-street' })
await __debug.perf({ census: true })
await __debug.perf()
await __debug.perf({ gpu: true })
```

The first two answer at once: the mark saves the spot and the view under a name (use a new name for each place: `'theed-street'`, `'cantina'`) and the census prints what the scene holds. After each of the last two, press Enter, click **Resume** in the game's menu, keep still for about ten seconds without opening anything, and go back to the console, where the table has printed: the first is the frame report, the second the same with GPU time per pass. Copy the printed tables into the chat. After a change, come back to the same spot and view with `await __debug.perf({ at: 'me-street' })` (it goes behind a loading screen, as a teleport does), click Resume, let it settle a few seconds, and paste `await __debug.perf()` again the same way. To put one switch's old and new behaviour side by side without a reload, `await __debug.perf({ ab: { key: '<switch>' } })` and Resume (the switches are listed by `await __debug.perf({ switches: true })`); to see what moving about costs, `await __debug.perf({ fly: { speed: 40, seconds: 16 } })` and Resume, and then the same with `speed: 120`. A line saying frames went by paused means something was open during the measurement; measure again.

**Common problems.**

- *Something is missing or old after pulling*: run `npm run swg -- status assets-private`; converter changes need the pack reconverted, and the table under **Converting your own SWG install** says which command. Ships: `npm run swg -- ships @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (`--match=yacht` redoes one ship and keeps the rest).
- *A hitch the first time something appears*: a shader compiled on a live frame; the console says `shaders: N compiled during play`. Whatever appeared should be warmed up behind the loading screen or given to the compile queue (see `compileObjects` in `src/world/world.ts`). Adding or removing a light recompiles everything: lights are pooled (`effects.flash`) and never added at runtime.
- *`RuntimeError: unreachable` then `recursive use of an object`*: the physics engine panicked and that world can never be stepped again. Two causes are known. The character controller pushing a dynamic body with a trimesh collider (now off). And the engine's own continuous-collision list of fixed colliders going stale when ground was taken away on a step where nothing was moving fast, which with rapier3d-compat 0.20 panicked inside `World.step` (`wasm-function[91]`, the sweep, under `stepWithEvents`) as soon as a corpse or a body left lying far off fell through where that ground had been; it only happens while the world holds 512 fixed colliders or fewer, which is open country, and `Physics.keeper` in `src/core/physics.ts` works round it (fixed upstream in 0.21). The game catches such a panic wherever it is met, in the step or on any other call a frame makes into the engine (reading, writing or removing a body the world no longer holds is the usual way in, and removing one twice is one; `Physics.removeBody` never does): the frames stop, `__debug.physics()` reports `broken`, the message line says so, and the character is saved where it stands and the page reloaded straight back into it (`__debug.physics({ reload: false })` leaves the game stopped instead, which a driven tab wants). A new one inside the step means a bad trimesh or an unsupported shape pair; one outside it means a body or collider used after it was taken out, and the first `RuntimeError: unreachable` in the console, before the `recursive use` lines, is where.
- *A model's hardpoints, cells or names do not match*: GLTFLoader strips `:` from node names and keeps the original in `userData.name`; read names through `hardpointName()` and `cellIndexOf()`.
- *A ray or query finds nothing right after building a physics world*: the world must step once first.
- *Nothing is heard at all*: `__debug.audio().state` tells the causes apart. `suspended` means the browser has not let the context run yet, which one click on the world fixes. `running` with voices listed and `master` above 0 is a routing or volume problem. `bank.available` false means the sound pack is not converted: run `npm run swg -- sounds @SWG assets-private --retail-only`. `recent` says why each of the last dozen sounds did not play.
- *The world is silent but the interface clicks*: `__debug.ambience()`. An empty `beds` list with rows in `sky().mix` means the planet's sky pack was converted before the environment tables' ambient sounds were read (`npm run swg -- sky @SWG assets-private --retail-only`). `sources.placed` 0 with `pack` `none` means the planet's places have not been converted (`npm run swg -- sounds @SWG assets-private all --retail-only`), which leaves the area's beds playing and the town's own crowds and cantina bands silent. A bed with a weight and nothing playing is either a full pool (`audio().waitingLoops` counts the voices waiting, which are the non-positional slots in `VOICE_TUNE.flat`) or a sound the bank has no entry for, which `ambience().bedsMissingFromBank` names. `sources.missingSounds` says the same for the town's own emitters.
- *A room sounds like the street, or the whole world sounds like a cave*: `__debug.audio().echo`. `on` false is the menu's Room echo switch. `using` null with `inside` false means the ear is not taken to be in a room at all, which is the building the listener's own space names — the same number `audio().voices[].space` shows, so a voice reading `-1:-1` in a cantina is a sound that has not been told where it stands. `room` null means nothing has named the room the ear is in: it then takes the ordinary echo, which is right for 259 of the interior table's 265 rows, and only the six the table marks apart (the four capitol lobbies, Mos Eisley's cantina and one station greenhouse) need naming. `sending` false while `using` is 0 or 1 means no voice is in the room to be echoed — a bed, which stands wherever the ear does, is not, and neither is an interface click, so in a room whose only sound is its own bed there is nothing for the echo to carry and that is not a fault. `audio({ mixer: { echoSend: [0.2, 0.4] } })` moves the amounts live, `audio({ mixer: { echoRoom: 7 } })` stands the ear in a tall hall wherever it is (and `-1` gives the room back to the game), and `muffledVoices` counts what is being heard through a wall.
- *Which shader is a window, and what did the converter make of it*: `npm run swg -- materials @SWG --ship=<id>`; `dump @SWG <file> --strings` shows a file's readable strings when its layout is unknown, and `--hex` every chunk's bytes with the floats they would be, for decoding the numbers between the strings; `template @SWG object/…/shared_x.iff` shows an object's parameters through its base chain.

## On assets

The original SWG client assets (the `.tre` archives) are copyrighted by Sony Online Entertainment / Lucasfilm and cannot be redistributed, so they will never be committed to this repo. A private build could load them from a local install via a converter, but a public site cannot ship them. The plan instead:

- **CC0 low-poly packs** that already match the flat-shaded style: Kenney, Quaternius, Poly Pizza. Good for props, buildings and vehicles.
- **Mixamo** for a rigged humanoid and a large animation library (free with an Adobe account, licensed for use in games). The X Bot stand-in in this repo comes from the three.js examples.
- **Sketchfab** fan-made Star Wars models under CC-BY where the licence allows it, credited in a `CREDITS.md`.

GLTF is the loading format for all of it.

## Layout

```
docs/DESIGN.md   game design decisions
tools/swg/       SWG asset converter (TRE, IFF, .msh to GLB)
tools/story/     npm run story:check, the story checker's command line
src/
  core/      input, camera, physics wrapper
  data/      planet definitions
  world/     noise, terrain, props, creatures, day cycle, world streaming
  player/    character controller, primitive model, skinned rig
  combat/    class kits (Jedi, Bounty Hunter), effects, hit detection
  vehicles/  garage, vehicle physics, ship interiors, engine trails
  net/       who this browser is, the server client and the other players
  story/     the story's rules (the book, the loader, the step machine, the checker), run by the browser and the server alike, and the committed test set
  ui/        HUD, menus, galaxy map, loading screen, character select and creator
  main.ts    app wiring, game loop, the __debug helpers
server/            the multiplayer server: the socket, the clock, the world it keeps and who each player is
CLAUDE.md          the handoff for Claude Code: rules, architecture, lessons learned, what is next
```

## Converting your own SWG install

Everything the game shows from the original client (planets, buildings, ground textures, creatures, the player character) is converted from a locally owned install into `assets-private/`, which is git-ignored and never committed. The converter is `tools/swg/cli.mjs`; `docs/ASSETS.md` explains what may be converted and why, and `tools/swg/README.md` documents every command.

**Setup, once per machine.** Node 22.18 or newer (24 is fine), then `npm install`. That is all: the converter finds both installs by itself.

**The whole conversion, in one command.**

```bash
npm run swg -- convert
```

It finds the two installs (a `.env` first where there is one, then what Windows itself records of its installed games, then the usual places; it prints where it looked and asks rather than guessing), asks `status` what is missing, and runs exactly that — in the order the converter needs, several steps at once where they cannot tread on each other — until nothing is missing. Everything goes into `assets-private/`, which is git-ignored and never committed, and every step runs with `--retail-only`, which mounts only the SOE-origin archives and leaves an emulator project's own content out.

It asks in rounds, because `status` can only ask for work whose own preconditions are already there: it cannot ask for the character's parts before the player pack exists, nor for the clip pair before the parts rig does. So a step whose input this round has still to write waits for the round after rather than being run against a pack that is not there yet, and the rounds go on until nothing is asked for. A first conversion takes four or five of them, and the wardrobes and the loading pictures — which `status` has no way of asking for on a machine that has converted nothing — are converted with the rest. One command is not part of it: `sandbox`, which is optional; run it on its own when you want it. The `gallery` is, although it is the development world and has a section of its own below, because every building the Housing tab puts down is one of its models.

A first full run is a few hours and about 14 GB. While it runs it shows one moving display: how far through it is, which steps are running and the last line each of them printed. Every step's whole output goes to a file of its own under `assets-private/logs/convert-<time>/`, and the folder is printed.

**Ctrl+C** stops it cleanly and says what was finished; running it again carries on from whatever `status` says is left, so it can be done over several evenings. A step that fails does not stop the ones that do not depend on it: at the end it names what failed and where each one's log is, and comes back non-zero.

**SOE's final files.** Where the install is an NGE one whose indexes the converter knows (SWG Legends' is), every step also reads the files SOE added in its last publishes, which those archives hold and those indexes never reach (`docs/ASSETS.md` says what the names list is and why it is the one file of the game's the repository carries). Every step prints one line saying whether it did, and stamps what it writes, so the first `convert` after that change came in converts nearly everything once, about twenty minutes on a machine with packs already in place, and then asks nothing again. An install it does not recognise converts exactly what it did before.

| Add | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| `convert <folder>` | put the converted content somewhere other than `assets-private` |
| `--only=ships,space` | run only those steps, and say which of the things they read are being left out |
| `--jobs=N` | how many steps may run at once (it picks a number for the machine otherwise, and never runs more at once than the machine has cores) |
| `--dry-run` | print the round it would start now — every step, roughly how long it takes, what it waits for and what it may not run beside — with what waits for a later round and what cannot be asked for until these packs exist; run nothing |
| `--yes` | ask nothing, for a script (a folder it cannot find is then an error rather than a question) |
| `--swg=<dir>` `--jka=<dir>` | name the installs outright |

`npm run swg -- status assets-private` says what is in place and prints the command for anything missing; `convert` is that list, asked again as each pack arrives, run for you — plus the wardrobes and the loading pictures, which nothing on that list can ask for until another pack names them. Without Jedi Academy the saber moves, jumps and saber sounds are left out and the steps that need it say so.

**A `.env` names the folders once**, if you would rather not be asked: copy `.env.example` to `.env` (git-ignored) and put the folder holding the `.tre` archives in it, and Jedi Academy's `GameData` folder if you have the game:

```
SWG=C:/SWG
JKA=C:/Program Files (x86)/Steam/steamapps/common/Jedi Academy/GameData
```

The converter replaces `@SWG`, `@JKA` and `@CORE3` on its command line with those values, so the commands below work the same in cmd, PowerShell, Git Bash and a Unix shell, and paths with spaces need no quoting. A shell variable works too (`export SWG=...`, then `@SWG` as the argument), and the environment wins over `.env`.

**One command at a time.** Every command below runs exactly as it always has, and `convert` only ever runs these same commands; the list is here for anybody who wants one of them on its own, and for reading what each one does.

```bash
npm run swg -- verify @SWG --retail-only                                        # 1. which archives are retail (sanity check)
npm run swg -- planets @SWG --retail-only                                       # 2. which planets the archives hold
npm run swg -- snapshot @SWG all assets-private --radius=all --retail-only       # 3. every planet: objects, terrain, ground textures, sky, flora, places (long: minutes per planet)
npm run swg -- creatures @SWG assets-private --retail-only                      # 4. the creatures the planets spawn
npm run swg -- player @SWG assets-private --retail-only --jka=@JKA             # 5. the player character, dressed, with its animations and Jedi Academy's saber, jump and parry clips (@JKA: its GameData or base folder); always with --jka once you have it, a run without it drops them
npm run swg -- parts @SWG assets-private --retail-only                           # 6. the same character as parts (body, head, clothes on one skeleton), which the game prefers
npm run swg -- clips-save assets-private/player/human_male.glb assets-private/player/jka.clips --only=BOTH_
npm run swg -- clips-apply assets-private/characters/human_male/rig.glb assets-private/player/jka.clips   #    carry the Jedi Academy clips onto the parts rig
npm run swg -- species @SWG assets-private --retail-only                         # 6b. every playable species and gender as parts, with the character creator's index
npm run swg -- customization @SWG assets-private --retail-only                   #    the creator's own table: each species' tabs, rows and words, which colours follow or set which,
                                                                                  #    the bald rule, the hairstyles it was offered and each palette's creation colours (seconds; no model)
npm run swg -- loading @SWG assets-private --retail-only                         #    the game's own loading-screen pictures, one per planet (without them a planet is drawn from its sky colours); --list shows what the archives hold
npm run swg -- wardrobe @SWG assets-private --retail-only                        # 7. every wearable and hairstyle, with the game's names, body slots, species rules and a picture each (hairstyles too), for the wardrobe on I (optional, long);
                                                                                  #    every piece the game coloured with its colours (the palettes it lays on after its first pass included) and every piece it did not with a dye of ours
npm run swg -- wardrobe @SWG assets-private --retail-only --gender=female        #    ... and the women's (wardrobe/human_female); every humanoid species of a gender shares it
npm run swg -- wardrobe @SWG assets-private --retail-only --template=object/creature/player/shared_ithorian_male.iff                    #    the Ithorians have a skeleton of their own: their wardrobes (about 520 pieces each, their own cuts included, 172 of them worn unseen)
npm run swg -- wardrobe @SWG assets-private --retail-only --template=object/creature/player/shared_ithorian_female.iff --gender=female  #    (run `species` again after, so the index names them)
npm run swg -- mobiles @SWG assets-private --retail-only                         # 7b. every creature, droid and NPC for the spawner (B, the NPCs tab): models, shared animation packs, names, stats and the NPCs' outfits; after wardrobe and species (about 5 minutes and 1 GB; add --skip-existing to resume a run that stopped)
npm run swg -- weapons @SWG assets-private --retail-only                         #    every weapon, with its name, hands and picture, for the backpack and the rack on I, and
                                                                                  #    the instruments, which are held the same way and are not weapons (run `music` after, which reads this pack);
                                                                                  #    and the Force's own effects, which the game has: the particles, the sounds their client effects name and the beam appearances
npm run swg -- ships @SWG assets-private --retail-only                           #    every player ship, with its interior, the components its slots take, its droid and its paint, for the garage on G; and the NPC ships (combat.json)
npm run swg -- space @SWG all assets-private --retail-only                        #    every space zone: stations, asteroid fields, planets, sky and hyperspace points; Kessel, Ord Mantell and Deep Space
npm run swg -- sandbox @SWG assets-private --retail-only                          #    optional: a made-up system to fly fast in, 250 km across (nothing in it is the game's)
npm run swg -- water @SWG all assets-private --retail-only                        #    each planet's own water: the colour, opacity, ripples and drift of every lake and sea, and each lava shader's look
npm run swg -- sounds @SWG assets-private --retail-only --jka=@JKA                #    every sound the game can play, the samples they name and where each one is used (about 856 MB),
                                                                                  #    plus Jedi Academy's saber sounds and the frames its animations mark, and what every ship, part and vehicle sounds like
npm run swg -- floracollision @SWG assets-private --retail-only                   #    what each world's trees and rocks are solid at: the trunk, the rocks' cylinders and the
                                                                                  #    nothing-at-all the client authored, instead of a cylinder guessed from each model's box
                                                                                  #    (seconds; converts no model, and snapshot and flora write the same file as they go)
npm run swg -- radial @SWG assets-private --retail-only                           #    the pictures of each world's own grass, flowers and small plants and its far hills' tree cards,
                                                                                  #    one layer each, out of the shaders its terrain's radial families name (about two seconds and
                                                                                  #    100 MB over every world; converts no model; run after the worlds and `terrain`;
                                                                                  #    Mustafar plants none)
npm run swg -- doors @SWG assets-private --retail-only                            #    the doors in every building's doorways: where each hangs, how it slides and how far, how near
                                                                                  #    a body must come to open it and what it sounds like, as each pack's doors.json, and the door
                                                                                  #    models once into assets-private/doors (seconds; converts no building; run after the worlds and gallery)
npm run swg -- navgrid all assets-private                                         #    the ground each world can be walked on, so a body sent across a planet is given corners
                                                                                  #    instead of a goal (about two and a half minutes a world, and it opens no archive: no @SWG)
npm run swg -- scenes assets-private                                              #    the places the creation and selection screens stand a character in, cut out of the worlds
                                                                                  #    already converted (about twenty seconds; it opens no archive either, and never touches a pack)
npm run swg -- clouds assets-private                                              #    how cloudy and how dark each world's own sky is, off the sheets the client drew it with,
                                                                                  #    and the noise the volumetric clouds march through (nine seconds; no archive)
npm run swg -- deeds @SWG assets-private --retail-only                            #    the buildings a player can buy: what each makes, its name, its lots, its upkeep and
                                                                                  #    the grid the client drew while you placed it (run after gallery)
npm run swg -- travel @SWG assets-private --retail-only                           #    the travel terminals, the ticket collectors and the shuttles, where the game put them,
                                                                                  #    with the two models that draw them (run after the worlds).
                                                                                  #    It took no @SWG before this and now does: no snapshot places a travel terminal, so the
                                                                                  #    thing the player presses has to be converted here beside the row saying where it stands
npm run swg -- fittings @SWG assets-private --retail-only                         #    everything else the server stood inside the game's own buildings: the elevator panel beside a
                                                                                  #    lift, the bank terminal outside a bank, the sign over a cantina's door, the campsites and the
                                                                                  #    dungeon props (run after the worlds, as travel does)
npm run swg -- props @SWG assets-private --retail-only                            #    every prop and every piece of furniture in the game as one pack, which is what the Props tab
                                                                                  #    lists and a player puts down: 8,596 things in 2,894 models, about 900 MB (a minute and a half;
                                                                                  #    forty-four of them carry their own fire, spray, flame or smoke)
npm run swg -- music @SWG assets-private --retail-only                            #    the music players make: twenty songs, one track per instrument, and nothing else
                                                                                  #    (about 100 MB of samples; there is no world score in this game and none is wanted).
                                                                                  #    Run it after `weapons`: it reads that pack to learn which instruments the game has
npm run swg -- conversations @SWG assets-private --retail-only                    #    what the game's own people say: the server's conversations (structure only, out of the
                                                                                  #    Core3 reference) for the people the worlds stand, the heralds stood beside them, and the
                                                                                  #    client's own string tables those and everybody's greetings are in (seconds, about 1.3 MB;
                                                                                  #    run after `spawns`, which `status` asks for)
npm run swg -- status assets-private                                              # 8. what is in place, and the command for anything missing
npm run dev                                                                       # 9. play
```

`player` and `parts` also bake the standing loop's mood branches (`idle:<mood>`); `--no-moods` leaves them out. Run `parts` after `player`, as always: the parts rig is the one the game plays.

Step 3 also accepts one planet at a time (`snapshot @SWG tatooine assets-private/tatooine --center=auto --radius=all --retail-only`), and `--radius=500` for a quick look at just the starport area. Step 5 dresses the character in a shirt, trousers and shoes; `--wear=object/tangible/wearables/...,...` picks other clothes (`list @SWG wearables/` shows what exists), `--var=name=value` sets skin and hair colours (the command prints every variable), `--template=object/creature/player/shared_twilek_female.iff` picks another species.

**After pulling new code.** Run `npm run swg -- status assets-private` first: it reports each pack and prints the exact command for whatever is missing. In general:

| What changed | Command to rerun |
| --- | --- |
| Ground textures, terrain rules, building layers, the sky | `terrain @SWG all assets-private --retail-only` (refreshes every existing pack in about half a minute; it writes every alternate of every ground family with its bump and gloss maps, the bump map's height in the gloss map's green, the client's three border masks and its cloud tile, and each world's colour ramps with how bright its colour map runs, about 180 MB of pictures over every world where one picture a family was 60, and `status` asks for it until it has: terrain shaders version 4) |
| Just the sky: sun, moons, stars, colour ramps, skybox, reflection maps, the weather effects and sounds | `sky @SWG all assets-private --retail-only` (run it again after pulling the converter that converts the effects particles carry: the light dust storms, falling leaves and Mustafar's lightning; `status` asks for it until it has run) |
| Just the water: each lake's and sea's colour, opacity, ripple, drift and reflection map, and each lava flow's look (a pack converted before the lava look draws a stand-in, and both the console and `status` ask for this command) | `water @SWG all assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; `terrain` does it too) |
| Reflective metal and glass on buildings and props | `snapshot @SWG <planet> assets-private/<planet> --center=auto --radius=all --retail-only` (the shine is baked into each model's textures) |
| Buildings, objects, flora, a new planet | `snapshot @SWG <planet> assets-private/<planet> --center=auto --radius=all --retail-only` |
| Animated screens, waterfalls, glowing surfaces | `snapshot @SWG all assets-private --radius=all --retail-only`, then `gallery @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (the ships pack follows with its next `ships` run; `status` asks for all three until they are done) |
| Named places on the galaxy map | `pois @SWG all assets-private --retail-only` |
| Where a planet's map picture lies (Kashyyyk's and Mustafar's places looked shrunk toward the middle) | `maps @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; `status` asks while a `map.json` carries no frame) |
| The grass, flowers and small plants on the ground round you, and the tree cards on the far hills (without them neither is drawn and `__debug.grass().world` says so) | `radial @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; converts no model; writes `terrain/radial.json` and `terrain/radial.rgba` beside each world's terrain and nothing else; `status` asks while a world whose terrain plants radial flora has none, or has one made from another terrain file) |
| Tatooine's manifest still naming the old pack command's structures, rocks, debris and vaporators (five of its models from before the materials' format 6, which nothing with a layout draws) | `snapshot @SWG tatooine assets-private/tatooine --center=auto --radius=all --retail-only` (stop the dev server first; `status` asks while a world's manifest carries any category the snapshot does not make) |
| What trees and rocks you walk into (a forest was solid wherever a wide tree stood) | `floracollision @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; converts no model; `status` asks while a world with flora has no `flora-collision.json`) |
| The doors in the buildings' doorways (without them every doorway stands open) | `doors @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; converts no building, only the eighty door models into `assets-private/doors`; `status` asks while a world or the gallery has no `doors.json`, or was converted again after it was written) |
| The travel terminals, ticket collectors and shuttles, and the fittings on every other building | `travel @SWG assets-private --retail-only`, then `fittings @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (both must run after the worlds they join to; `status` asks for each until it has) |
| Skin, clothes, eyes, animations (walking, swimming, crouching, prone, the blaster carries, the emotes, the dances and their flourishes, the riding poses) | `player @SWG assets-private --retail-only --jka=@JKA`, then `parts @SWG assets-private --retail-only` and the `clips-save` and `clips-apply` pair (steps 5 and 6), then `species` for the other characters: the game plays the parts rig, so new SWG clips reach it only through `parts` and `species`, and the Jedi Academy clips through the bundle |
| The appearance page's layout: the game's own groups, rows, words and linked colours (without it the page lists every slider and colour the packs have, as before) | `customization @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; converts no model; `status` asks while the file is missing, older than the code, or has no eye colour row for the human male) |
| Creature models or clips, or how a rider sits on a mount | `creatures @SWG assets-private --retail-only` |
| The spawner's creatures, droids and NPCs: models, animations, names, stats, outfits | `mobiles @SWG assets-private --retail-only --skip-existing` (redoes only what changed; `--match=rancor` redoes some and keeps the rest) |
| How a rider sits on a vehicle | `gallery @SWG assets-private --retail-only --only=vehicles` |
| The NPC ships: their types, tier fits, formations, taunts and hit, target and explosion effects (`combat.json`; without it the NPC tab offers no starships) | `ships @SWG assets-private --retail-only` |
| Sound: every sound template, the samples they name, and the client data and tables that say where each one is used (without the bank the game is silent and `__debug.audio().bank.available` is false) | `sounds @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (about 856 MB; a second run copies nothing) |
| Where a planet's sounds are: the sound objects its snapshot places (crowds, cantina bands, fires, the starport announcer), each placed model's surface, and its buildings' rows of the interior table. Without them a planet still plays its area beds and its rooms' beds, and `__debug.ambience().sources.placed` is 0 | `sounds @SWG assets-private all --retail-only` (the bank again, then every planet's places; one planet by name instead of `all`) |
| What the game's own people say: the heralds' conversations and the places they mark, and the client's own reaction lines everybody with a way of speaking greets you in (without them everybody greets in our own words, and no herald stands) | `conversations @SWG assets-private --retail-only` (seconds; after `spawns`, and again when the Core3 reference or a world's people change, which `status` says) |

Two more sources add what the client files alone do not place. `--events` on `snapshot` includes buildout areas the game only shows during an event (`planets` lists which planets have any, with the event each needs). And what was the live server's -- the travel terminals and shuttles, the fittings, the deeds, the static objects the screenplays place, where the creatures and people stood, each mobile's level, health and damage, and what the server's people said (the structure of its conversations -- screen names, links and the client's own string ids, never its words or its code -- and who greets in which of the client's reaction tables) -- comes from the SWGEmu server scripts, **without anybody needing the emulator**: what the converter reads from them (names, places, turns, counts and stats, never the scripts) is kept in the checkout as `tools/swg/core3ref/` and ships with the launcher, and `travel`, `fittings`, `deeds`, `spawns`, `snapshot`, `mobiles` and `conversations` read it by default (and the relay reads the conversations' structure from it as they are). It is written by `npm run swg -- core3-reference @CORE3` from a Core3 checkout (`CORE3=<Core3/MMOCoreORB/bin/scripts>` in `.env`), which reads every answer back and compares it with a fresh read of the folder before it keeps anything; run it when the emulator's scripts change and commit what it writes. `--core3=<dir>` on any of the seven reads a folder live instead, and `--core3=none` reads neither (on `mobiles`: estimates from size and name). SOE's own people for Kashyyyk's zones and Mustafar, which Core3 holds nothing of, come the same way from `tools/swg/ngeref/`, the rows of SOE's own server data every client needs (the owner's decision of 2026-10-19), which `spawns` reads by default: `npm run swg -- nge-reference <a copy of that data> assets-private --swg=@SWG --retail-only` writes it in a checkout, keeping it only when the people built from it are the ones built from the copy; `--nge=<folder>` on `spawns` reads a copy live and `--nge=none` leaves them out.

Commands that write a single planet's pack take the planet's folder (`assets-private/tatooine`); commands that take `all` take the parent (`assets-private`). Reconverting is always safe: each command overwrites only its own files.

## Build

```bash
npm run build    # typecheck + production build into dist/
npm run preview  # serve dist/
```

A GitHub Pages workflow in `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` builds and deploys `dist/` on pushes to `main` when Pages is enabled for the repo.
